Querying Circumscribed Description Logic Knowledge Bases
Carsten Lutz, Quentin Manière, Robin Nolte

Abstract
Circumscription is one of the main approaches for defining non-monotonic description logics (DLs) and the decidability and complexity of traditional reasoning tasks, such as satisfiability of circumscribed DL knowledge bases (KBs), are well understood. For evaluating conjunctive queries (CQs) and unions thereof (UCQs), in contrast, not even decidability has been established. In this paper, we prove decidability of (U)CQ evaluation on circumscribed DL KBs and obtain a rather complete picture of both the combined complexity and the data complexity for DLs ranging from 𝒜 ​ ℒ ​ 𝒞 ​ ℋ ​ ℐ ​ 𝒪 \mathcal{ALCHIO} via ℰℒ {\cal EL} to various versions of DL-Lite. We also study the much simpler atomic queries (AQs).



1
Introduction

While standard description logics (DLs), such as those underlying the OWL 2 ontology language, do not support non-monotonic reasoning, it is generally acknowledged that extending DLs with non-monotonic features is very useful. Concrete examples of
applications include ontological modeling in the biomedical domain
[
\citeauthoryearRector2004
,
\citeauthoryearStevens et al.2007
]
and the formulation of access control policies
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti and
Samarati2003
]
. Circumscription is one of the traditional AI approaches to non-monotonicity, and it provides an important way to define non-monotonic DLs. In contrast to other approaches, such as default rules, it does not require the adoption of strong syntactic restrictions to preserve decidability. DLs with circumscription are closely related to several other approaches to non-monotonic DLs, in particular to DLs with defeasible inclusions and typicality operators
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Faella, and
Sauro2011
,
\citeauthoryearCasini and
Straccia2013
,
\citeauthoryearGiordano et al.2013
,
\citeauthoryearBonatti et al.2015b
,
\citeauthoryearPensel and
Turhan2018
]
.

The main feature of circumscription is that selected predicate symbols can be minimized, that is, the extension of these predicates must be minimal regarding set inclusion. Other predicates may vary freely or be declared fixed. In addition, a preference order can be declared on the minimized predicates. In DLs, minimizing or fixing role names causes undecidability of reasoning, and consequently, only concept names may be minimized or fixed
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Lutz, and Wolter2009
]
. The traditional AI use of circumscription is to introduce and minimize abnormality predicates such as
{\sf AbnormalBird}
, which makes it possible to formulate defeasible implications such as ‘birds fly, unless they are abnormal, which shouldn’t be assumed unless there is a reason to do so.’ Circumscription is also closely related to the closure of predicates symbols as studied, for instance, in
[
\citeauthoryearLutz, Seylan, and
Wolter2013
,
\citeauthoryearNgo, Ortiz, and Simkus2016
,
\citeauthoryearLutz, Seylan, and
Wolter2019
]
; in fact, this observation goes back to
[
\citeauthoryearReiter1977
,
\citeauthoryearLifschitz1985
]
. While DLs usually assume open-world semantics and represent incomplete knowledge, such closed predicates are interpreted under a closed-world assumption, reflecting that complete knowledge is available regarding those predicates. Circumscription may then be viewed as a soft form of closing concept names: there are no other instances of a minimized concept name except the explicitly asserted ones unless we are forced to introduce (a minimal set of) additional instances to avoid inconsistency.

A primary application of DLs is ontology-mediated querying, where an ontology is used to enrich data with domain knowledge. Surprisingly, little is known about ontology-mediated querying with DLs that support circumscription. For the important conjunctive queries (CQs) and unions of CQs (UCQs), in fact, not even decidability has been established. This paper aims to close this gap by studying the decidability and precise complexity of ontology-mediated querying for DLs with circumscription, both w.r.t. combined complexity and data complexity. We consider the expressive DL
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
, the tractable (without circumscription) DL
{\cal EL}
, and several members
of tge DL-Lite family. These may be viewed as logical cores of the profiles OWL 2 DL, OWL 2 EL, and OWL 2 QL of the OWL 2 ontology language
[
\citeauthoryearMotik et al.2009
]
.

One of our main results is that UCQ evaluation is decidable in all these DLs when circumscription is added. It is
2Exp
-complete in
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
w.r.t. combined complexity, and thus not harder than query evaluation without circumscription. W.r.t. data complexity, however, there is a significant increase from

coNP
- to
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
-completeness.
For
{\cal EL}
, the combined and data complexity turns out to be identical to that of
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
. This improves lower bounds from
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Faella, and
Sauro2011
]
.
All these lower bounds already hold for CQs. Remarkably, the
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
lower bound for data complexity already holds when there is only a single minimized concept name (and thus no preference relation) and without fixed predicates.
The complexities for DL-Lite are lower, though still high. A summary can be found in Table
1
. Evaluation is ‘only’
coNP
-complete w.r.t. data complexity for all considered versions of DL-Lite, except when role disjointness constraints are added (this case is not in the table). The combined complexity remains at
2Exp
when role inclusions are present and drops to
coNExp
without them. The lower bounds already apply to very basic versions of DL-Lite that are positive
in the sense that they do not provide concept disjointness constraints, and the upper bounds apply to expressive versions that include all Boolean operators.

We also study the evaluation of the basic yet important atomic queries (AQs), conjunctive queries of the form
A(x)
with
A
a concept name. Also here, we obtain a rather complete picture of the complexity landscape. It is known from
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Lutz, and Wolter2009
]
that AQ evaluation in
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
is
\textnormal{\sc coNExp}^{\textnormal{\sc NP}}
-complete w.r.t. combined complexity.
We show that the lower bound holds already for
{\cal EL}
. Moreover, our
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
-lower bound for the data complexity of (U)CQ-evaluation in
{\cal EL}
mentioned above only requires an AQ, and thus AQ evaluation in both
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
and
{\cal EL}
are
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
-complete w.r.t. data complexity. For DL-Lite, the data complexity drops to
PTime
in all considered versions, and the combined complexity ranges from
coNExp
-complete to
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
-complete, depending on which Boolean operators are admitted. A summary can be found in Table
2
.

Proof details are in the appendix.

Related Work.
A foundational paper on description logics with circumscription is
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Lutz, and Wolter2009
]
, which studies concept satisfiability and knowledge base consistency in the
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
family of DLs; these problems are interreducible with AQ evaluation in polynomial time (up to complementation). The same problems have been considered in
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Faella, and
Sauro2011
]
for
{\cal EL}
and DL-Lite and in
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti et al.2015a
]
for DLs without the finite model property, including a version of DL-Lite. The recent
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti2021
]
is the only work we are aware of that considers ontology-mediated querying in the context of circumscription. It provides lower bounds for
{\cal EL}
and DL-Lite, which are both improved in the current paper, but no decidability results / upper bounds. A relaxed version of circumscription that enjoys lower complexity has recently been studied in
[
\citeauthoryearStefano, Ortiz, and
Simkus2022
]
. We have already mentioned connections
to DLs with defeasible inclusions and typicality operators,
see above for references. A connection between circumscription and the
complexity class
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
was first observed in
[
\citeauthoryearEiter and
Gottlob1993
]
, and this complexity shows up in our data
complexity results. Our proofs, however, are quite different.

2
Preliminaries

Let
{\sf N_{C}}
,
{\sf N_{R}}
, and
{\sf N_{I}}
be countably infinite sets of
concept names
,
role names
, and
individual names
.
An
inverse role
takes the form
r^{-}
with
r
a role name, and a
role
is a role name or an inverse role. If
r=s^{-}
is an inverse role, then
r^{-}
denotes
s
.
An
{\cal ALCIO}
concept

C
is built according to the rule

C,D::=\top\mid A\mid\{a\}\mid\neg C\mid C\sqcap D\mid\exists r.D

where
A
ranges over concept names,
a
over individual names, and
r
over roles.
A concept of the form
\{a\}
is called a
nominal
.
We write
\bot
as abbreviation for
\neg\top
,
C\sqcup D
for
\neg(\neg C\sqcap\neg D)
, and
\forall r.C
for
\neg\exists r.\neg C
.
An
\mathcal{ALCI}
concept
is a nominal-free
{\cal ALCIO}
concept.
An
{\cal EL}
concept
is an
\mathcal{ALCI}
concept that uses neither negation nor inverse roles.

An
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
TBox
is a finite set of
concept inclusions
(CIs)

C\sqsubseteq D
, where
C,D
are
{\cal ALCIO}
concepts, and

role inclusions (RIs)

r\sqsubseteq s
, where
r,s
are
roles. In an
{\cal EL}
TBox
, only
{\cal EL}
concepts may be used in CIs,
and RIs are disallowed. An
ABox
is a finite set of

concept assertions

A(a)
and
role assertions

r(a,b)

where
A
is a concept name,
r
a role name, and
a,b
are individual
names. We use
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
to denote the set of individual names used
in
\mathcal{A}
. An
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
knowledge base (KB)
takes the form

\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
with
\mathcal{T}
an
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
TBox and
\mathcal{A}
an ABox.

\mathcal{ALCHI}
TBoxes and KBs are defined analogously but may not use nominals.

The semantics is defined as usual in terms of interpretations

\mathcal{I}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},\cdot^{\mathcal{I}})
with
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
the (non-empty)

domain
and
\cdot^{\mathcal{I}}
the
interpretation function
, we
refer to
[
\citeauthoryearBaader et al.2017
]
for full details. An interpretation
satisfies a CI
C\sqsubseteq D
if
C^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq D^{\mathcal{I}}
and
likewise for RIs. It satisfies an assertion
A(a)
if
a\in A^{\mathcal{I}}

and
r(a,b)
if
(a,b)\in r^{\mathcal{I}}
; we thus make the
standard
names assumption
. An interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is a
model
of a
TBox
\mathcal{T}
, written
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{T}
, if it satisfies all inclusions
in it. Models of ABoxes and KBs are defined likewise. For an
interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and
\Delta\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, we use

\mathcal{I}|_{\Delta}
to denote the restriction of
\mathcal{I}
to subdomain
\Delta
.

A
signature
is a set of concept and role names referred to as

symbols
. For any syntactic object
O
such as a TBox or an
ABox, we use
{\sf sig}(O)
to denote the symbols used in
O
and

|O|
to denote the
size
of
O
, meaning the encoding of
O

as a word over a suitable alphabet.

We next introduce several more restricted DLs of the DL-Lite family. A

basic concept
is of the form
A
or
\exists r.\top
. A

\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf core}^{\cal H}
TBox is a finite set of concept inclusions

C\sqsubseteq D
,
(concept) disjointness assertions

C\sqsubseteq\neg D
, and role inclusions
r\sqsubseteq s
where
C,D
are basic
concepts and
r,s

roles. We drop superscript
\cdot^{\mathcal{H}}
if no role inclusions are admitted,
use subscript
\cdot_{{\sf horn}}
to indicate that the concepts

C,D
in concept inclusions may be conjunctions of basic concepts,
and subscript
\cdot_{{\sf bool}}
to indicate that
C,D
may be
Boolean combinations of basic concepts, that is, built from basic
concepts using
\neg
,
\sqcap
,
\sqcup
.

A
circumscription pattern

is a tuple
{\sf CP}=(\prec,\mathsf{M},\mathsf{F},\mathsf{V})
, where
\prec
is a
strict partial order on
\mathsf{M}
called the
preference relation
,
and
\mathsf{M}
,
\mathsf{F}
and
\mathsf{V}
are a partition of
{\sf N_{C}}
.
The elements
of
\mathsf{M}
,
\mathsf{F}
and
\mathsf{V}
are the
minimized
,
fixed

and
varying
concept names.
Role names always vary to avoid
undecidability
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Lutz, and Wolter2009
]
.
The preference relation
\prec
on
\mathsf{M}
induces a preference relation

<_{\sf CP}
on interpretations by setting
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
if the
following conditions hold:

1.

\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
,

2.

for all
A\in\mathsf{F}
,
A^{\mathcal{J}}=A^{\mathcal{I}}
,

3.

for all
A\in\mathsf{M}
with
A^{\mathcal{J}}\not\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}}
, there
is a
B\in\mathsf{M}
,

B\prec A
, such that
B^{\mathcal{J}}\subsetneq B^{\mathcal{I}}
,

4.

there exists an
A\in\mathsf{M}
such that
A^{\mathcal{J}}\subsetneq A^{\mathcal{I}}

and for all
B\in\mathsf{M}
,
B\prec A
implies
B^{\mathcal{J}}=B^{\mathcal{I}}
.

A
circumscribed KB (cKB)
takes the form
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
where

\mathcal{K}
is a KB and
{\sf CP}
a circumscription pattern.
A model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
is a
model
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
, denoted
\mathcal{I}\models{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
, if no
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
.
A cKB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
is
satisfiable
if
it has a model.

A
conjunctive query (CQ)
takes the form

q(\bar{x})=\exists\bar{y}\,\varphi(\bar{x},\bar{y})

where
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
are tuples of variables and
\varphi

is a
conjunction of
atoms

A(z)
and
r(z,z^{\prime})
, with

A\in{\sf N_{C}}
,
r\in{\sf N_{R}}
, and
z,z^{\prime}
variables from

\bar{x}\cup\bar{y}
.
The variables in
\bar{x}
are the
answer variables
,
and
{\sf var}(q)
denotes
\bar{x}\cup\bar{y}
.
We take the liberty to view

q
as a set of atoms, writing, e.g.,
\alpha\in q
to indicate that
\alpha
is an atom in

q
. We may also write
r^{-}(x,y)\in q
in place of

r(y,x)\in q
.
A CQ
q
gives rise to an interpretation

\mathcal{I}_{q}
with
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{q}}={\sf var}(q)
,
A^{\mathcal{I}_{q}}=\{x\mid A(x)\in q\}
, and
r^{\mathcal{I}_{q}}=\{(x,y)\mid r(x,y)\in q\}
for all
A\in{\sf N_{C}}
and
r\in{\sf N_{R}}
.
A
union of conjunctive queries (UCQ)

q(\bar{x})
is a disjunction of CQs that all have the same answer variables

\bar{x}
. The
arity
of
q
is the length of
\bar{x}
, and
q
is
Boolean
if it is of arity zero. An
atomic query (AQ)

is a CQ of the simple form
A(x)
, with
A
a concept name.

With a homomorphism from a CQ
q
to an
interpretation
\mathcal{I}
, we mean a homomorphism from
\mathcal{I}_{q}
to
\mathcal{I}
(defined as usual).
A tuple
\bar{d}\in(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}})^{|\bar{x}|}

is an
answer
to a UCQ
q(\bar{x})
on an interpretation

\mathcal{I}
, written
\mathcal{I}\models q(\bar{d})
,
if there is a homomorphism
h
from a CQ
p
in
q
to
\mathcal{I}
with

h(\bar{x})=\bar{d}
. A tuple
\bar{a}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
is an
answer
to
q

on a cKB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
with
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
, written

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models q(\bar{a})
, if
\mathcal{I}\models q(\bar{a})
for all models

\mathcal{I}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.

Example 1
.

Consider a database about universities. The TBox contains domain
knowledge such as

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\sf University}&\sqsubseteq&{\sf Organization}\\[2.84526pt]
{\sf Organization}&\sqsubseteq&{\sf Public}\sqcup{\sf Private}\\[2.84526pt]
{\sf Public}\sqcap{\sf Private}&\sqsubseteq&\bot.\end{array}

Circumscription can be used to express defeasible inclusions.
For example, from a European
perspective, universities are usually public:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\sf University}&\sqsubseteq&{\sf Public}\sqcup{\sf Ab}_{U}\end{array}

where
{\sf Ab}_{U}
is a fresh ‘abnormality’ concept name that is
minimized. If the ABox contains

{\sf University}({\sf leipzigu}),{\sf University}({\sf mit}),{\sf Private}({\sf mit})

and we pose the CQ
q(x)={\sf Organization}(x)\wedge{\sf Public}(x)
, then the answer is
{\sf leipzigu}
.

We may also use circumscription to implement a soft closed-world assumption, similar in spirit to soft constraints in constraint satisfaction. Assume that the ABox additionally contains a database of nonprofit corporations:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\sf NPC}({\sf greenpeace})&{\sf NPC}({\sf wwf})\end{array}

and that this database is
essentially
complete
, expressed by minimizing
{\sf NPC}
. If we
also know that

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\sf IvyLeagueU}&\sqsubseteq&\exists{\sf ownedBy}.({\sf NPC}\sqcap{\sf Rich})\\[2.84526pt]
{\sf DonationBased}&\sqsubseteq&\neg{\sf Rich}\end{array}\\[8.53581pt]
\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\sf DonationBased}({\sf greenpeace})&{\sf DonationBased}({\sf wwf})\\[2.84526pt]
{\sf IvyLeagueU}({\sf harvard})\end{array}\end{array}

then we are forced to infer that our list of NPCs was not actually complete as all explicitly known NPCs are not rich, but a rich NPC must exist. A strict closed-world assumption would instead result in an inconsistency.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a description logic such as
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
or
{\cal EL}
and let
\mathcal{Q}
be a query language such as UCQ, CQ, or AQ. With
\mathcal{Q}
evaluation on circumscribed
\mathcal{L}
KBs
, we mean the problem to
decide, given an
\mathcal{L}
cKB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
with
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
, a
query
q(\bar{x})
from
\mathcal{Q}
, and a tuple

\bar{a}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})^{|\bar{x}|}
, whether

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models q(\bar{a})
. When studying the combined complexity
of this problem, all of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
,
q
, and
\bar{a}
are treated
as inputs. For data complexity, in contrast, we assume
q
,
\mathcal{T}
, and

{\sf CP}
to be fixed and thus of constant size.

3
Between
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
and
{\cal EL}

We study the complexity of query evaluation on circumscribed KBs for
DLs between
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
and
{\cal EL}
. In fact, we prove
2Exp
-completeness
in combined complexity and
\Uppi^{\mathrm{P}}_{2}
-completeness in data complexity
for all these DLs.

3.1
Fundamental Observations

We start with some fundamental observations that underlie the subsequent
proofs, first observing a
reduction from UCQ evaluation on circumscribed
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
KBs to UCQ
evaluation on circumscribed
\mathcal{ALCHI}
KBs. Note that a nominal may be
viewed as a (strictly) closed concept name with a single
instance. This reduction is a simple version of a reduction
from query evaluation with closed concept names to query evaluation on
cKBs in the proof of Theorem
2

below.

Proposition 1
.

UCQ evaluation on circumscribed
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
KBs can be reduced in
polynomial time to UCQ evaluation on circumscribed
\mathcal{ALCHI}
KBs.

Proof.

Let
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
be an
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
cKB, with

\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
, and let
q(\bar{x})
be a UCQ. Let
N
be the set
of individual names
a
such that the nominal
\{a\}
is used in

\mathcal{T}
. Introduce fresh concept names
A_{a},B_{a},D_{a}
for every

a\in N
. We obtain
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{T}
by replacing every
a\in N

with
A_{a}
and adding the CI
A_{a}\sqcap\neg B_{a}\sqsubseteq D_{a}
,

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{A}
by adding
A_{a}(a)
and
B_{a}(a)
for every

a\in N
, and
q^{\prime}
from
q
by adding the disjunct

\exists y\,D_{a}(y)
for every
a\in N
.
1
1

1

Strictly
speaking, we need to adjust
\exists y\,D_{a}(y)
so that it has
the same answer variables as the other CQs in
q
. This is easy by
adding to
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
a CI
\top\sqsubseteq T
for a fresh concept
name
T
and extending
\exists y\,D_{a}(y)
with atom
T(x)
for every answer variable
x
.
Set
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{T}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
. The
circumscription pattern
{\sf CP}^{\prime}
is obtained from
{\sf CP}
by minimizing
the concept names
B_{a}
with higher preference than any other
concept name (and with no preferences between them). We show in the
appendix that
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models q(\bar{a})
iff

{\sf Circ}_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\models q^{\prime}(\bar{a})
for all

\bar{a}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})^{|\bar{x}|}
.
∎

We are thus left with
\mathcal{ALCHI}
KBs. We generally assume that TBoxes
are in
normal form
, meaning that every concept inclusion in

\mathcal{T}
has one of the following shapes:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\qquad}l@{\qquad}r}\top\sqsubseteq{{A}}&{{A}}\sqsubseteq\exists{{r}}.{{B}}&\exists{{r}}.{{B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}\\
{{A_{1}}}\sqcap{{A_{2}}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}&{{A}}\sqsubseteq{{\lnot B}}&{{\lnot B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}\end{array}

where
{{A,A_{1},A_{2},B}}
range over
{\sf N_{C}}
and

r
ranges over
roles.
The set of concept names in
\mathcal{T}
is denoted
\mathsf{N_{C}}(\mathcal{T})
.

Lemma 1
.

Every
\mathcal{ALCHI}
TBox
\mathcal{T}
can be transformed in linear
time into an
\mathcal{ALCHI}
TBox
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
in normal form such that
for all cKBs
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
, UCQs
q(\bar{x})
that do
not use symbols from

{\sf sig}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\setminus{\sf sig}(\mathcal{T})
, and

\bar{a}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})^{|\bar{x}|}
:

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})\models q(\bar{a})\text{ iff }{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime},\mathcal{A})\models q(\bar{a}).

Let
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
be a cKB with
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
.
A
type
is a set of concept names

t\subseteq\mathsf{N_{C}}(\mathcal{T})
. For an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
,
we define

\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({d})\coloneqq\{{{A}}\in\mathsf{N_{C}}(\mathcal{T})\mid d\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}}\}.

For a subset
\Delta\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, we set

\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({\Delta})=\{\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({d})\mid d\in\Delta\}
.
We further write
\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{I})
for
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}})
.
Finally, we set

\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{T})\coloneqq\bigcup_{\mathcal{I}\text{ model of }\mathcal{T}}\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{I}).

For a role
r
, we write
t\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}t^{\prime}
if
for all
{{A,B}}\in\mathsf{N_{C}}
:

•

{{B}}\in t^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{T}\models\exists{{r}}.{{B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}

implies
{{A}}\in t
and

•

{{B}}\in t
and
\mathcal{T}\models\exists{{r}}^{-}.{{B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}

implies

{{A}}\in t^{\prime}
.

We next show how to identify a ‘core’ part of a model
\mathcal{I}

of
\mathcal{K}
. These cores will play an important role in dealing with
circumscription in our upper bound proofs.

Definition 1
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{K}
.
We use
{\sf TP}_{{\sf core}}(\mathcal{I})
to denote the set of
all types
t\in{\sf TP}(\mathcal{I})
such that

|\{d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\mid{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)=t\}|<|{\sf TP}(\mathcal{T})|

and set
{\sf TP}_{\overline{{\sf core}}}(\mathcal{I})={\sf TP}(\mathcal{I})\setminus{\sf TP}_{{\sf core}}(\mathcal{I})
and

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{\sf core}=\{d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\mid{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)\in{\sf TP}_{{\sf core}}(\mathcal{I})\}.

So the core consists of all elements whose types are realized not too
often, except possibly in the ABox. A good way of thinking about cores
is that if a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
is minimal w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
, then all
instances of minimized concept names are in the core. This is, however not strictly true since we may have
A\sqsubseteq B
where
A
is
\top
or fixed, and
B
is minimized.

The following crucial lemma provides a sufficient condition for
a model
\mathcal{J}
of
\mathcal{K}
to be minimal w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
, relative
to a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
that is known to be minimal w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
.

Lemma 2

(Core Lemma)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
and let
\mathcal{J}
be a model of
\mathcal{K}
with

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{\sf core}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
.
If

1.

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(d)

for all
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{\sf core}
and

2.

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{\sf core})={\sf TP}_{\overline{{\sf core}}}(\mathcal{I})
,

then
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.

We give a sketch of the proof of (the contrapositive of)
Lemma
2
. Assume that
\mathcal{J}
is not a model of

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
. Then there must be a model
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}
with

\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{J}
and to obtain a contradiction it suffices to
construct from
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
a model
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}
with

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
. Note that
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
may have domains of
different sizes. The elements of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{{\sf core}}
receive
the same type in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
as in
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
. For each non-core type
t
in

\mathcal{I}
, we consider the set
S_{t}
of types in
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
of those
elements that have type
t
in
\mathcal{J}
. Since
t
is realized in
\mathcal{I}
at
least
|{\sf TP}(\mathcal{T})|
many times, we have enough room to realize in

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
exactly the types from
S_{t}
among those elements that had
type
t
in
\mathcal{I}
, that is, within
(\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}})^{-1}(t)
. It is
easy to see that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
: it satisfies
\mathcal{T}
as it
realizes the same types as
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
and it satisfies
\mathcal{A}
since

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}|_{{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})}=\mathcal{J}^{\prime}|_{{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})}
. By construction
and since
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{J}
, it further satisfies
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
.

We next use the core lemma to show that if

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\not\models q(\bar{a})
for any CQ
q
and

\bar{a}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})^{|\bar{x}|}
, then this is witnessed by a
countermodel
\mathcal{I}
that has a regular shape. Here and in what follows,
a
countermodel
is a model
\mathcal{I}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
with

\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\bar{a})
. By regular shape, we mean that
there is a ‘base part’ that contains the ABox, the core of
\mathcal{I}
, as
well as some additional elements; all other parts of
\mathcal{I}
are
tree-shaped with their root in the base part, and potentially with
edges that go back to the core (but not to other parts of the
base!). We next make this precise.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
. Set

\Omega=\{rA\mid B\sqsubseteq\exists r.A\in\mathcal{T}\}
and fix
a function
f
that chooses, for every
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and

rA\in\Omega
with
d\in(\exists r.A)^{\mathcal{I}}
, an element

f(d,rA)=e\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}}
. Further choose,
for every
t\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
, a representative
e_{t}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

with
{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(e_{t})=t
.
We inductively define the set
\mathcal{P}
of
paths through
\mathcal{I}

along with
a mapping
h
assigning to each
p\in\mathcal{P}
an element of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
:

•

each element
d
of the set

\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}:={\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\cup\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}\cup\{e_{t}\mid t\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})\}

is a path in
\mathcal{P}
and

h(d)=d
;

•

if
p\in\mathcal{P}
with
h(p)=d
and
rA\in\Omega
with

f(d,rA)
defined and not from
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{{\sf core}}
, then

p^{\prime}=prA
is a path in
\mathcal{P}
and
h(p^{\prime})=f(d,rA)
.

For every role
r
, define

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l}R_{r}&=&\{(a,b)\mid a,b\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A}),r(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{(d,e)\mid d,e\in\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}},(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}}\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{(p,p^{\prime})\mid p^{\prime}=prA\in\mathcal{P}\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{(p,e)\mid e=f(h(p),rA)\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{{\sf core}}\}.\end{array}

Now the
unraveling
of
\mathcal{I}
is defined by setting

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}l}\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\mathcal{P}\qquad A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\ =\ \{p\in\mathcal{P}\mid h(p)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}\}\\[2.84526pt]
r^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\displaystyle\bigcup_{\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r}(R_{s}\cup\{(e,d)\mid(d,e)\in R_{s^{-}}\})\end{array}

for all concept names
A
and role names
r
. It is easy to verify
that
h
is a homomorphism from
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{I}
. This version of
unraveling is inspired by constructions from
[
\citeauthoryearManière2022
]
where
the resulting model is called an
interlacing
.

Lemma 3
.

Let
q(\bar{x})
be a UCQ and
\bar{a}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
.
If
\mathcal{I}
is a countermodel
against
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models q(\bar{a})
, then so is its
unraveling
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.

For some of our upper bound proofs, it will be important that the
reference model
\mathcal{I}
in the core lemma is finite and sufficiently
small. The following lemma shows that we can always achieve this.

Lemma 4
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
There exists a model
\mathcal{J}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
such that
|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|\leq{\left|{\mathcal{A}}\right|}+2^{2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}}
,
\mathcal{I}|_{\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}}=\mathcal{J}|_{\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{J}}}
, and
\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})=\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{J})
.

In the proof of Lemma
4
, we
construct the desired model
\mathcal{J}
by starting from

\mathcal{I}|_{{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\cup\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}}
and adding exactly

m={\left|{\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{T})}\right|}
instances of each type from

\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
.

3.2
Combined complexity

We show that in any DL between
{\cal EL}
and
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
,
the evaluation of CQs and UCQs on cKBs is
2Exp
-complete
w.r.t. combined complexity, starting with the upper bound.

Theorem 1
.

UCQ evaluation on circumscribed
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
KBs is in
2Exp
w.r.t. combined complexity.

By Proposition
1
, it suffices to consider
\mathcal{ALCHI}
.
Assume that we are given as an input an
\mathcal{ALCHI}
cKB

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
with
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
, a UCQ
q(\bar{x})
, and a tuple

\bar{a}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})^{|\bar{x}|}
. We have to decide whether or not
there is a countermodel
\mathcal{I}
against
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models q(\bar{a})
.

Fix a set
\Delta
of size
|{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})|+2^{2|\mathcal{T}|+1}
such that

{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\Delta
. Note that
\Delta
is sufficiently large so that
we may assume the base domain
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{{\sf base}}
of unraveled
interpretations to be a subset of
\Delta
. In an outer loop, our
algorithm iterates over all triples

(\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}},\Delta_{{\sf core}},T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}})
such that the
following conditions are satisfied:

•

\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}
is a model of
\mathcal{A}
with

\Delta_{{\sf core}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}\subseteq\Delta
;

•

\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}({\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}\setminus\Delta_{{\sf core}}})=T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}
;

•

\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}({\Delta_{{\sf core}}})\cap T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}=\emptyset
.

For each triple
(\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}},\Delta_{{\sf core}},T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}})
, we then check
whether the following additional conditions are satisfied:

(I)

\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}
can be extended to a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{T}

such that

(a)

\mathcal{I}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}}=\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}
,

(b)

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}})\subseteq T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}
, and

(c)

\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\bar{a})
;

(II)

there exists a model
\mathcal{J}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
such that

\mathcal{J}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}_{{\sf core}}}=\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}|_{\Delta_{{\sf core}}}
and
\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{J})=T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}
.

We return ‘yes’ if all triples fail the check and ‘no’ otherwise.

If the checks succeed, then the model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
from Condition (I)
is a countermodel against
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models q(\bar{a})
. In
particular, we may apply Lemma
2
, using the model
\mathcal{J}
from Condition (II) as the reference model, to show that
\mathcal{I}
is
minimal w.r.t.
<_{{\sf CP}}
. Conversely, any countermodel
\mathcal{I}_{0}

against
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models q(\bar{a})
can be unraveled into a
countermodel
\mathcal{I}
from which we can read off a triple

(\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}},\Delta_{{\sf core}},T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}})
in the
obvious way, and then
\mathcal{I}
witnesses Condition (I) and choosing

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{I}
witnesses Condition (II).

Of course, we have to prove that Conditions (I) and (II) can be
verified in
2Exp
. This is easy for Condition (II): by
Lemma
4
, it suffices to
consider models
\mathcal{J}
of size at most

{\left|{\mathcal{A}}\right|}+2^{2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}}
and thus we can iterate
over all candidate interpretations
\mathcal{J}
up to this size, check
whether
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
with

\mathcal{J}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}_{{\sf core}}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}|_{\Delta_{{\sf core}}}
and

\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{J})=T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}
, and then iterate over all
models
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}
with
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
to check that

\mathcal{J}
is minimal w.r.t.
<_{{\sf CP}}
.

Condition (I) requires more work. We use a mosaic approach, that is,
we attempt to assemble the interpretation
\mathcal{I}
from Condition (I) by
combining small pieces called
mosaics
.
Each mosaic contains the
base part of
\mathcal{I}
and at most two additional elements

e^{*}_{1},e^{*}_{2}
. We trace
partial homomorphisms from CQs in
q
through the mosaics, as follows.

Fix a triple

(\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}},\Delta_{{\sf core}},T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}})
. A

match triple
for an interpretation
\mathcal{J}
takes the form

(p,\widehat{p},h)
such that
p
is a CQ in
q
,

\widehat{p}\subseteq p
, and
h
is a partial map from

{\sf var}(\widehat{p})
to
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
that is a homomorphism from

\widehat{p}|_{{\sf dom}(h)}
to
\mathcal{J}
. Intuitively,
\mathcal{J}
is a mosaic and
the triple
(p,\widehat{p},h)
expresses that a homomorphism from

\widehat{p}
to
\mathcal{I}
exists, with the variables in
{\sf dom}(h)
being
mapped to the current piece
\mathcal{J}
and the variables in

{\sf var}(\widehat{p})\setminus{\sf dom}(h)
mapped to other
mosaics. A match triple is
complete
if
\widehat{p}=p
and

incomplete
otherwise. To make
\mathcal{I}
a countermodel, we must
avoid complete match triples. A
specification
for
\mathcal{J}
is a
set
S
of match triples for
\mathcal{J}
and we call
S

saturated
if
the following conditions are satisfied:

•

if
p
is a CQ in
q
,
\widehat{p}\subseteq p
, and
h
is
a homomorphism from
\widehat{p}
to
\mathcal{J}
, then
(p,\widehat{p},h)\in S
;

•

if
(p,\widehat{p},h),(p,\widehat{p}^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in S
and
h(x)=h^{\prime}(x)

is defined for all

x\in{\sf var}(\widehat{p})\cap{\sf var}(\widehat{p}^{\prime})
, then

(p,\widehat{p}\cup\widehat{p}^{\prime},h\cup h^{\prime})\in S
.

Definition 2
.

A
mosaic
for
(\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}},\Delta_{{\sf core}},T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}})
is a
tuple
M=(\mathcal{J},S)
where

•

\mathcal{J}
is an interpretation such that

1.

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}\uplus\{e^{*}_{1},e^{*}_{2}\}
;

2.

\mathcal{J}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}
;

3.

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(e_{i}^{*})\in T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}
if
e_{i}^{*}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
;

4.

\mathcal{J}
satisfies all
\exists r.A\sqsubseteq B\in\mathcal{T}

and all
r\sqsubseteq s\in\mathcal{T}
.

•

S
is a saturated specification for
\mathcal{J}
that contains only
incomplete match triples.

We use
\mathcal{J}_{M}
to refer to
\mathcal{J}
and
S_{M}
to refer
to
S
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a set of mosaics for

(\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}},\Delta_{{\sf core}},T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}})
. We say that
M\in\mathcal{M}
is

good in

\mathcal{M}
if for every

e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}

and every

A\sqsubseteq\exists r.B\in\mathcal{T}
with
e\in(A\sqcap\neg\exists r.B)^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
, we
find a mosaic
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
such that the following conditions are
satisfied:

1.

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}_{M}}(e)={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}_{M^{\prime}}}(e)
;

2.

e\in(\exists r.B)^{\mathcal{J}_{M^{\prime}}}
;

3.

if
(p,\widehat{p},h^{\prime})\in S_{M^{\prime}}
, then
(p,\widehat{p},h)\in S_{M}
where

h
is the restriction of
h^{\prime}
to range
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}\cup\{e\}
.

If
M
is not good in
\mathcal{M}
, then it is
bad
. To verify
Cond. (I), we start
with the set of all mosaics for

(\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}},\Delta_{{\sf core}},T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}})

and repeatedly and exhaustively eliminate bad mosaics.

Lemma 5
.

\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}
can be extended to a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{T}
that
satisfies Conditions (a) to (c) iff at least one mosaic survives
the elimination process.

We provide a matching lower bound for
Theorem
1
. It is rather strong as it
already applies to CQs, to
{\cal EL}
KBs, and uses a single minimized
concept name (and consequently no preferences) and no fixed concept
names. It is proved by a subtle reduction from CQ evaluation on
{\cal EL}
KBs with closed concept names, that is, with KBs
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})

enriched with a set
\Sigma
of closed concept names
A
that have to
be interpreted as
A^{\mathcal{I}}=\{a\mid A(a)\in\mathcal{A}\}
in all models

\mathcal{I}
. This problem was proved to be
2Exp
-hard in

[
\citeauthoryearNgo, Ortiz, and Simkus2016
]
. The reduction also sheds some light on the connection
between circumscription and closing concept names.

Theorem 2
.

CQ evaluation on circumscribed
{\cal EL}
KBs is
2Exp
-hard w.r.t. combined
complexity.
This holds even with a single minimized concept name and no
fixed concept names.

3.3
Data complexity

We show that in any DL between
{\cal EL}
and
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
, the evaluation of CQs
and UCQs on cKBs is
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
-complete w.r.t. data
complexity. We start with the upper bound.

Theorem 3
.

UCQ evaluation on circumscribed
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
KBs is in
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}

w.r.t. data complexity.

We may again limit our attention to
\mathcal{ALCHI}
. To prove
Theorem
3
, we show that if a countermodel
exists, then there is one of polynomial size (with TBox
and query of constant size). Note that this is not a
consequence of Lemma
4
since
there is no guarantee that, if the model
\mathcal{I}
from that lemma is a
countermodel, then so is
\mathcal{J}
. Once the size bound is in place, we
obtain the
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
upper bound by a straightforward
guess-and-check procedure.

Lemma 6
.

Let
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
be an
\mathcal{ALCHI}
cKB,

\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
,
q(\bar{x})
a UCQ, and
\bar{a}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})^{|\bar{x}|}
. If
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\not\models q(\bar{a})
,
then there exists a
countermodel
\mathcal{I}
with
|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|\leq({\left|{\mathcal{A}}\right|}+2^{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|})^{{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}^{\left|{q}\right|}}
.

To prove Lemma
6
, let
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
be an

\mathcal{ALCHI}
cKB,
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
, and let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
with
\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\bar{a})
. We construct a small countermodel by starting from the unraveling

{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{I}
and applying a quotient construction. The
latter is based on a suitable equivalence relation on
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}

which we define next. Recall that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
consists of a base part

\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and a tree-part with backedges to

\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

For
n\geq 0
and
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\setminus\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
,
we use
\mathcal{N}_{n}(d)
to denote the
n
-neighborhood
of
d
in

{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
up to
\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, that is, the set of all elements

e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\setminus\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
such that the
undirected graph

G_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}},\{\{d,e\}\mid(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\text{ for
some }r\in{\sf N_{R}}\})

contains a path
d_{0},\dots,d_{k}
with
d_{0}=d
,

d_{0},\dots,d_{k-1}\notin\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
d_{k}=e
,

0\leq k\leq n
.

Recall that the elements of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
are paths through

\mathcal{I}
, sequences
p=d_{0}r_{1}A_{1}\cdots r_{n}A_{n}
with

d_{0}\in\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
r_{i}A_{i}\in\Omega
; the

length
of
p
, denoted by
|p|
, is
n
. By definition
of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and of neighborhoods, for every
n\geq 0
and

d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\setminus\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, there is a
unique path
p_{d,n}\in\mathcal{N}_{n}(d)
that is a prefix of all
paths in
\mathcal{N}_{n}(d)
, that is, all

e\in\mathcal{N}_{n}(d)\setminus\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
take the form

p_{d,n}r_{1}A_{1}\cdots r_{k}A_{k}
.

For
n\geq 0
, the equivalence relation
\sim_{n}
on

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
is defined by setting

d_{1}\sim_{n}d_{2}
if

d_{1}=d_{2}\in\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
or

d_{1},d_{2}\notin\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and the following conditions
are satisfied:

1.

d_{1}=p_{d_{1},n}w
and
d_{2}=p_{d_{2},n}w
for some
w\in\Omega^{*}
;

2.

for every
w=r_{1}A_{1}\cdots r_{k}A_{k}\in\Omega^{*}
:

p_{d_{1},n}w\in\mathcal{N}_{n}(d_{1})\text{ iff }p_{d_{2},n}w\in\mathcal{N}_{n}(d_{2})

and if
p_{d_{1},n}w\in\mathcal{N}_{n}(d_{1})
, then

(a)

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(p_{d_{1},n}w)={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(p_{d_{2},n}w)
;

(b)

(p_{d_{1},n}w,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
iff
(p_{d_{2},n}w,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}

for all roles
r
and
e\in\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

3.

|d_{1}|\equiv|d_{2}|\!\!\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
.

For an element
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
,
we use
{\overline{d}}
to denote the equivalence class of
d
w.r.t.
\sim_{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}
.
The
quotient

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}/{\sim}_{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}
of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is the
interpretation whose domain is the set of all equivalence
classes of
\sim_{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}
and where

\begin{array}[]{rcl}A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}/{\sim}_{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}}&=&\{{\overline{d}}\mid d\in A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\}\\[2.84526pt]
r^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}/{\sim}_{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}}&=&\{({\overline{d}},{\overline{e}})\mid(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\}\end{array}

for all concept names
A
and role names
r
. It can be verified that

|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}/{\sim}_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}}}|\leq({\left|{\mathcal{A}}\right|}+2^{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|})^{{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}^{\left|{q}\right|}}
, as desired.

Lemma 7
.

Let
q(\bar{x})
be a UCQ and
\bar{a}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})^{{\left|{\bar{x}}\right|}}
.
If
\mathcal{I}
is a countermodel
against
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models q(\bar{a})
, then so is
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}/{\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}}}
.

It is in fact straightforward to show that

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}/{\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}}}
is a model of

\mathcal{K}
. Minimality w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
is proved using
Lemma
2
. The most subtle part of the proof of
Lemma
7
is showing that

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}/{\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}}}\not\models q(\bar{a})
. This
is done by exhibiting suitable ‘local’ homomorphisms from

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}/{\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}}}
back
to
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
so that from any homomorphism from a CQ
p

in
q
to
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}/{\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}}}
with

h(\bar{x})=\bar{a}
, we obtain a homomorphism from
p
to
\mathcal{I}
with the
same property by composition.
This finishes the proof of Lemmas
7

and
6
and of
Theorem
3
.

We next provide a matching lower bound for Theorem
3
. It already applies to AQs and
{\cal EL}
KBs, and when there is a single minimized concept name and no fixed concept name.
It is proved by a subtle reduction from the validity of
\forall\exists{\sf 3SAT}
sentences. Several non-obvious technical tricks are needed to make the reduction work with a single minimized concept name.
Our result improves upon a known
coNP
lower bound from
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti2021
]
.

Theorem 4
.

AQ evaluation on circumscribed
{\cal EL}
KBs is
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
-hard.
This holds even with a single minimized concept name and no fixed concept names.

4
DL-Lite

We consider the DL-Lite family of DLs.
Without circumscription, these DLs enjoy low complexity of query evaluation, typically
NP
-complete in combined complexity and in
AC
0
in data complexity (depending on the dialect).
With circumscription, the complexity tends to still be very high, though in some relevant cases it is lower than in
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
.

4.1
Combined complexity

Our first result shows that, when role inclusions are admitted, nothing is gained from transitioning from
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
to DL-Lite.
The proof is a variation of that of

Theorem

2
, but technically much
simpler.

Theorem 5
.

UCQ evaluation on circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf core}^{\cal H}
KBs is
2Exp
-hard w.r.t. combined
complexity.
This holds even with a single minimized concept name, no fixed concept names, and no disjointness constraints.

We now move to
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
as a very expressive DL-Lite dialect
without role inclusions and observe that the combined complexity
decreases.

Theorem 6
.

UCQ evaluation on circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
KBs is in

coNExp
w.r.t. combined complexity.

To prove Theorem
6
, we first observe
that we can refine the unraveling of countermodels
\mathcal{I}
from
Section
3.1
such that each element
outside of the base part
\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
has at most one successor
per role. This property allows us to simplify the notion of a
neighborhood in the quotient construction in
Section
3.3
. This, in turn, yields the
following result. Note that in contrast to
Lemma
6
, there is no double exponential
dependence on
|q|
.

Lemma 8
.

Let
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
be a
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
cKB with

\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
,
q(\bar{x})
a UCQ, and
\bar{a}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})^{|\bar{x}|}
. If
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\not\models q(\bar{a})
,
then there exists a
countermodel
\mathcal{I}
with
|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|\leq({\left|{\mathcal{A}}\right|}+2^{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|})^{{\left|{q}\right|}^{2}({\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1)}
.

Lemma
8
yields a
\textnormal{\sc coNExp}^{\textnormal{\sc NP}}

upper bound by a straightforward guess-and-check procedure: guess a
model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\bar{a})
and

|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
bounded as in Lemma
8
, and
use an oracle to check that
\mathcal{I}
is minimal w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
where
the oracle decides, given a cKB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
and a model
\mathcal{I}
of

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
, whether
\mathcal{I}
is non-minimal w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
by
guessing a model
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
; such an approach was
used also in
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Lutz, and Wolter2009
]
.

To obtain a
coNExp
upper bound as desired, we replace the oracle
with a more efficient method to check whether a given model
\mathcal{I}
of

\mathcal{K}
is minimal w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
. The crucial observation is that
instead of guessing a model
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
, it suffices to
consider certain interpretations
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
of polynomial size, derived
from sub-interpretations of
\mathcal{I}
, and guess models
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}

of
\mathcal{K}
. Intuitively, we decompose an expensive ‘global’ test into
exponentially many inexpensive ‘local’ tests. This even works in the
presence of role inclusions, that is, in
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\mathcal{H}}
, which
shall be useful in Section
5
.

Let
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
be a
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\mathcal{H}}
cKB with

\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{I}
a model of
\mathcal{K}
. For each role
r

used in
\mathcal{T}
such that
r^{\mathcal{I}}\neq\emptyset
, we choose a witness

w_{r}\in(\exists r^{-})^{\mathcal{I}}
. Every
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
gives
rise to an interpretation
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}l}\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}&=&\mathcal{P}\cup{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\cup\{w_{r}\mid r\text{ used in }\mathcal{T},r^{\mathcal{I}}\neq\emptyset\}\\[2.84526pt]
{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}&=&{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}\\[2.84526pt]
{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}&=&{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}}\cap({\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\times{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A}))\\[2.84526pt]
&&\cup\,\{(e,w_{s})\mid e\in(\exists s)^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}},\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r\}\\[2.84526pt]
&&\cup\,\{(w_{s},e)\mid e\in(\exists s)^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}},\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r^{-}\}.\end{array}

Note that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
is derived from the subinterpretation

\mathcal{I}|_{\mathcal{P}\cup{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})}
by ‘rerouting’ some role edges to
elements
w_{r}
. We have

{\left|{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}\right|}\leq{\left|{\mathcal{A}}\right|}+{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}
and will only consider sets
\mathcal{P}
with

{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
. It is not difficult to show
that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
, for all

\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
. The next lemma characterizes the
(non)-minimality of
\mathcal{I}
in terms of the (non)-minimality of the
interpretations
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
.

Lemma 9
.

The following are equivalent:

1.

There exists a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
;

2.

There exist a
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

with
{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
and a
family

(\mathcal{J}_{e})_{e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}}
of models of
\mathcal{K}
such that

\mathcal{J}_{e}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}
and

\mathcal{J}_{e}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}}=\mathcal{J}_{e^{\prime}}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}}
for all
e,e^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.

It should now be clear that we have established
Theorem
5
. After guessing the model

\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\bar{a})
, we check the
complement of Point 2 of Lemma
9
in a
brute-force way. More precisely, we first iterate over all

\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
with

{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
, then over all
interpretations
\mathcal{J}_{0}
with
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}

(as candidates for the common restriction of the models

(\mathcal{J}_{e})_{e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}}
to
{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}}
), then over
all
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, and finally over all models
\mathcal{J}_{e}
of
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{J}_{e}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}}=\mathcal{J}_{0}
, and test whether

\mathcal{J}_{e}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}
. We accept if for every
\mathcal{P}
and

\mathcal{J}_{0}
, there is an
e
such that for all
\mathcal{J}_{e}
the final check
fails. Overall, we obtain a
coNExp
algorithm.

We provide a matching lower bound that holds even for
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf core}
cKBs.
It is proved by reduction from the complement of Succinct3COL, which
is known to be
NExp
-complete
[
\citeauthoryearPapadimitriou and
Yannakakis1986
]
.

Theorem 7
.

UCQ answering on circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf core}
KBs is
coNExp
-hard
w.r.t. combined complexity. This holds even with a single
minimized concept name, no fixed concept names, and no disjointness constraints.

4.2
Data complexity

We now consider data complexity, where the landscape is less diverse.
Indeed, we obtain
coNP
-completeness for all DLs between

\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\mathcal{H}}
and
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf core}
, and both for UCQs and CQs.

Theorem 8
.

UCQ evaluation on circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\mathcal{H}}
KBs is in
coNP
w.r.t. data complexity.

To prove Theorem
8
, we again guess a model

\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\bar{a})
and
|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|

bounded as in Theorem
8
, and then verify
that
\mathcal{I}
is minimal w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
. For the latter, we introduce a
variation of Lemma
9
. The original
version of Lemma
9
is not helpful because
its Point 2 involves deciding whether, given an interpretation

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}
, there is a model

\mathcal{J}_{e}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}
of
\mathcal{K}
, and given that

{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}
there is no reason
to believe that this can be done in polynomial time in data
complexity. We actually conjecture this problem to be
coNP
-complete.
In the following, we vary the definition of the interpretations

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
so that their size is independent of that of
\mathcal{A}
.

Let
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
be a
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\mathcal{H}}
cKB with

\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{I}
a model of
\mathcal{K}
. We assume
\mathcal{T}
to be
in normal form. For an element
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, we define its

ABox type
to be

\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{A}}({e})=\{A\mid A\in\mathsf{N_{C}},\mathcal{K}\models A(e)\}.

Note that
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{A}}({e})
actually needs not be a proper type as
defined in Section
3
due to the presence of
disjunction in
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\mathcal{H}}
. If
e\notin{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
, which
is permitted, then
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{A}}({e})
is simply the set of all
concepts names
A
with
\mathcal{K}\models\top\sqsubseteq A
.

For each pair
(t_{1},t_{2})
such that
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{A}}({e})=t_{1}
and

\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({e})=t_{2}
for some
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, we choose such
an element
e_{t_{1},t_{2}}
; we use
E
to denote the set of all elements chosen in this way.
For each role
r
used in
\mathcal{T}
such that

r^{\mathcal{I}}\neq\emptyset
, we choose an element

w_{r}\in(\exists r^{-})^{\mathcal{I}}
. Now define, for every
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
,
the interpretation
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}=\;

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\cup E\cup\{w_{r}\mid r\text{ used in }\mathcal{T},r^{\mathcal{I}}\neq\emptyset\}

\displaystyle{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}=\;

\displaystyle{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}

\displaystyle{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}=\;

\displaystyle\{(e,w_{s})\mid e\in(\exists s)^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}},\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(w_{s},e)\mid e\in(\exists s)^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}},\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r^{-}\}

Note that
{\left|{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}\right|}\leq 4^{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}
.
We also define an ABox

\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P}}=\{A(a)\mid a\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}},A\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{A}}({a})\}

and set
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P}})
. The ABoxes
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P}}

act as a decomposition of the ABox
\mathcal{A}
, similarly to how the
interpretations
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
act as a decomposition of the
interpretation
\mathcal{I}
. Note that

\bigcup_{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P}}
does not contain role assertions. This is
compensated by the use of
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{A}}({a})
in the
definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P}}
and the fact that, since
\mathcal{T}
is in normal
form, all relevant endpoints of role assertions are ‘visible’ in the
ABox types. Note that this fails in the case of
{\cal EL}
where dropping
role assertions could result in CIs
\exists r.B\sqsubseteq A
to be left
unsatisfied, and such CIs are crucial for proving

\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
-hardness in that case, see the proof of
Theorem
4
.

The following is the announced variation of
Lemma
9
.

Lemma 10
.

The following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{I}\models{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
;

2.

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}\models{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}})
for all
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
with
{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
.

In the “
1\Rightarrow 2
” direction we
use the witnesses
e_{t_{1},t_{2}}
to extend a potential model

\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
of some

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}

to a model

\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
, obtaining a contradiction.
In
the “
2\Rightarrow 1
” direction, for a potential model

\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
, we can find a
\mathcal{P}
with

{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
so that we can construct
from
\mathcal{J}
a model
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
of

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
.

To establish Theorem
8
, it thus suffices to
argue that checking Point 2 of Lemma
10

can be implemented in time polynomial in
|\mathcal{A}|
. We iterate over all
polynomially many
sets
\mathcal{P}
with
{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1

(recall that
\mathcal{T}
is fixed)
and check whether

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}\models{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}})
in a brute force way. To verify
the minimality of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
,
we iterate over all models
\mathcal{J}
of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
with

\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
(of which there are only polynomially many,
thanks to the modified definition of

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
), and make sure that

\mathcal{J}\not<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
for any such
\mathcal{J}
. Overall, we
obtain a
coNP
algorithm.

The
coNP
upper bound turns out to be tight, even for

\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf core}
cKBs and CQs, and with very restricted circumscription
patterns. We reduce from 3-colorability.

Theorem 9
.

CQ evaluation on circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf core}
KBs is
coNP
-hard
w.r.t. data complexity.
This holds even with a single minimized concept name, no fixed
concept names, and no disjointness constraints.

5
Atomic Queries

We study the evaluation of atomic queries on circumscribed KBs, which
is closely related to concept satisfiability w.r.t. such KBs. In
fact, the two problems are mutually reducible in polynomial time.
Our
results from this section, summarized in
Table
2
, can thus also be viewed as completing the
complexity landscape for concept satisfiability, first studied in

[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Lutz, and Wolter2006
,
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Faella, and
Sauro2011
]
.

{\cal EL}
,
\mathcal{ALCHIO}

\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
,
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\cal H}

\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf core}
,
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf horn}^{\cal H}

Combined complexity

\textnormal{\sc coNExp}^{\textnormal{\sc NP}}
-c.
(†)

(Thm.
10
,
11
)

coNExp
-c.
(Thm.
12
,
13
)

\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
-c.
(‡)

(Thm.
14
)

Data complexity

\Uppi^{\textup{P}}_{2}
-c.
(Thm.
3
,
4
)

in
PTime

(Thm.
15
)

in
PTime

(Thm.
15
)

Table 2:
Complexity of AQ evaluation on circumscribed KBs.
(†)
: completeness already known for
\mathcal{ALC}(\mathcal{I}\mathcal{O})
.
(‡)
: hardness already known.

5.1
Between
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
and
{\cal EL}

Concept satisfiability w.r.t. circumscribed
{\cal ALCIO}
KBs was proved to be
\textnormal{\sc coNExp}^{\textnormal{\sc NP}}
-complete in
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Lutz, and Wolter2009
]
. The proof of the upper bound can easily be extended to cover also role inclusions. We thus obtain:

Theorem 10
.

[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Lutz, and Wolter2009
]

AQ evaluation on circumscribed
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
KBs is in

\textnormal{\sc coNExp}^{\textnormal{\sc NP}}
w.r.t. combined complexity.

An alternative way to obtain Theorem
10
for
\mathcal{ALCHI}
is to use Lemma
6
, which yields the existence of single exponentially large countermodels in the special case where the query
q
is of constant size (here
{\left|{q}\right|}=1
), and a straightforward guess-and-check procedure as sketched in Section
4.1
.
This can be lifted to
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
by a variation of (the proof of)
Proposition
1
tailored to AQs.

We next prove a matching lower bound for
{\cal EL}
, improving on an
Exp
lower bound from
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Faella, and
Sauro2011
]
.

Theorem 11
.

AQ evaluation on circumscribed
{\cal EL}
KBs is
\textnormal{\sc coNExp}^{\textnormal{\sc NP}}
-hard w.r.t. combined
complexity.
This holds even without
fixed concept names and with an empty preference order.

This is proved by a reduction from AQ evaluation on
\mathcal{ALC}
cKBs, which is known to be
\textnormal{\sc coNExp}^{\textnormal{\sc NP}}
-hard
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Lutz, and Wolter2009
]
.

Regarding data complexity, it suffices to recall that
Theorem
4
applies even to AQs and

{\cal EL}
cKBs.

5.2
DL-Lite

Recall that in Section
4.1
, we have
proved that UCQ evaluation over
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
cKBs is in
coNExp
w.r.t. combined complexity. We started with a guess-and-check
procedure that gives a
\textnormal{\sc coNExp}^{\textnormal{\sc NP}}
upper bound,
relying on countermodels of single exponential size as per
Lemma
8
, and then improved to
coNExp
using Lemma
9
. Here, we use the same
algorithm. The only difference in the correctness proof is that
Lemma
8
is replaced with
Lemma
6
as the former does not support
role inclusions and the latter delivers a single exponential upper
bound for queries of constant size.

Theorem 12
.

AQ evaluation on circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\cal H}
KBs is in
coNExp
w.r.t. combined complexity.

We match this upper bound even in the absence of role inclusions,
demonstrating that evaluating AQs and UCQs over
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
cKBs is
equally difficult.

Theorem 13
.

AQ evaluation on circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
KBs is
coNExp
-hard w.r.t. combined
complexity.

The proof is by reduction from the complement of the

NExp
-complete problem
Succinct
-
3COL
and relies on fixed concept names.

The situation is more favorable if we restrict our attention to
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf horn}^{\cal H}
cKBs, which is still a very expressive dialect of the
DL-Lite
family.
For
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf horn}^{\cal H}
, we prove that if there is a countermodel, then there is one of linear size, in drastic contrast to the
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
case where the proof of Theorem
13
shows that exponentially large countermodels cannot be avoided.

Theorem 14
.

AQ evaluation on circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf horn}^{\cal H}
KBs is in

\Uppi^{\textup{P}}_{2}
w.r.t. combined complexity.

A matching lower bound for
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf core}
cKBs can be found in
[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Faella, and
Sauro2011
]
.

We now move to data complexity, where we obtain tractability even for the maximally expressive
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\cal H}
dialect of
DL-Lite
.

Theorem 15
.

AQ evaluation on circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\cal H}
KBs is in
PTime
w.r.t. data complexity.

We sketch the proof of Theorem
15
.
Let
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
be a
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\cal H}
cKB with
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
,
A_{0}(x)
an AQ, and
a_{0}\in{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
. We construct an ABox
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
whose size is independent of that of
\mathcal{A}
and which can replace
\mathcal{A}
when deciding whether
a_{0}
is an answer to

A_{0}
. The construction of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
may be viewed as a variation of
the constructions that we have used in the proofs of
Theorems
6

and
8
to avoid an
NP
oracle. In
particular, it reuses the notion of ABox types from the latter.
However, the construction employed here works directly with ABoxes
rather than with countermodels.

Let
{\sf TP}(\mathcal{A})
denote the set of all ABox types
t
realized
in
\mathcal{A}
, that is, all
t
such that
{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{A}}(a)=t
for some

a\in{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
. For every
t\in{\sf TP}(\mathcal{A})
, set

m_{t}=\min({\left|{\{a\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\mid\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{A}}({a})=t\}}\right|},~4^{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|})

and choose

m_{t}
individuals
a_{t,1},\dots,a_{t,m_{t}}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
such that

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{A}}(a_{t,i})=t
for
1\leq i\leq m_{t}
. We assume that the
individual
a_{0}
of interest is among the chosen ones. Let
W
be the
set of all chosen individuals, that is,

W=\{a_{t,i}\mid t\in{\sf TP}(\mathcal{A}),~1\leq i\leq m_{t}\}
. Now define an ABox

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}&=&\{A(a)\mid a\in W,A\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{A}}({a})\}\end{array}

and set
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
. Note that

|{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})|\leq 8^{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}
,
and thus the size of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
depends only on the TBox
\mathcal{T}
,
but not on the original ABox
\mathcal{A}
. Also, note that,
just like the ABoxes
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P}}
from the proof of
Theorem
8
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
no longer contains role
assertions.

Lemma 11
.

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models A_{0}(a_{0})\!
iff
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\models A_{0}(a_{0})
.

It is important for the ‘only if’ direction of
Lemma
11
that we keep at least
4^{|\mathcal{T}|}

individuals of each ABox type
t
(if existent). In fact, this allows
us to convert a model
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
into a model of
\mathcal{K}
that
is minimal w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
, using arguments similar to those in the proof of
Lemma
2
. A crucial point is that if

a\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\setminus{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
and
t={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{A}}(a)
,
then
m_{t}=4^{|\mathcal{T}|}
which implies that there is a
regular type
t^{\prime}
such that the combination
(t,t^{\prime})
is realized at
least
2^{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}
many times in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
among the individuals
from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

Lemma
11
gives
PTime
membership as the size of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is bounded by a constant. We compute
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}

in polynomial time and check whether

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\models A_{0}(a_{0})
, which can be decided in

2Exp
by Theorem
1
, that is, in
constant time w.r.t. data complexity. The correctness of this
procedure immediately follows from Lemma
11
.

5.3
Negative role inclusions

DL-Lite is often defined to additionally include negative role
inclusions of the form
r\sqsubseteq\neg s
, with the obvious semantics.
It is known that these sometimes lead to increased computational complexity; see, for example,
[
\citeauthoryearManière2022
]
.
We close by observing that this is also the case for circumscription.
While querying circumscribed DL-Lite KBs (in all considered dialects) is
coNP
-complete w.r.t. data complexity for (U)CQs and in
PTime
for AQs, adding negative role inclusions results in a jump back to
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
.
We prove this by reduction from
\forall\exists{\sf 3SAT}
.
Some ideas are shared with the proof of
Theorem
4
, but the general strategy of the
reduction is different.

Theorem 16
.

AQ evaluation on
circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf core}^{\cal H}
KBs with negative role inclusions is
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
-hard.
This holds even without fixed concept names and with a single negative role inclusion.

6
Conclusion

We have provided a rather complete picture of the complexity of query
evaluation on circumscribed KBs. Some cases, however, remain open. For
example, the lower bounds in combined complexity for UCQ evaluation on

DL-Lite
cKBs given in Theorems
5

and
7
cannot be improved in an
obvious way to CQs, for which the complexity remains open. Also, the
lower bounds provided in
Theorems
13
,

16
and the
\Uppi^{\textrm{P}}_{2}
one from

[
\citeauthoryearBonatti, Faella, and
Sauro2011
]
rely on the preference relation
in circumscription patterns,
and it remains open whether the complexity decreases
when the preference relation is forced to be empty. Finally, it would
be interesting to study query evaluation under ontologies that are
sets of existential rules or formulated in the guarded (negation)
fragment of first-order logic, extended with circumscription. We
believe that these problems are still decidable.

Appendix A
Proofs for Section
3.1

To complete the proof of Proposition
1
, it suffices
to show the following.

Lemma 12
.

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models q(\bar{a})
iff

{\sf Circ}_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\models q^{\prime}(\bar{a})
for all

\bar{a}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

First assume that
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\not\models q(\bar{a})
. Then there is
a model
\mathcal{I}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\bar{a})
.
Let
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
be defined like
\mathcal{I}
except that

A_{a}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=B_{a}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{a\}
and
D_{a}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for
all
a\in N
. It is readily checked that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model of

{\sf Circ}_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
and that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\not\models q^{\prime}(\bar{a})
.

Now assume that

{\sf Circ}_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\not\models q^{\prime}(\bar{a})
. Then there is
a model
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
of
{\sf Circ}_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
with

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\not\models q^{\prime}(\bar{a})
. Since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model of

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, we have
a\in B_{a}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
for all
a\in N
. Moreover,
if
\{a\}\subsetneq B_{a}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, then we can find a model

\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
of
{\sf Circ}_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
with

\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
by setting
B_{a}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\{a\}
and

D_{a}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=A_{a}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\setminus\{a\}
. This contradicts the
minimality of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, and, consequently, we must have

B_{a}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{a\}
. But then also
A_{a}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{a\}
since

A(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
,
A_{a}\sqcap\neg B_{a}\sqsubseteq D_{a}\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
,
and the disjunct
\exists y\,D_{a}(y)
is not satisfied in

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. It can be verified that, consequently,
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is also a
model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
, and we are done.
∎

See
1

We omit a proof of the above lemma, which is entirely standard. The same normal form was used for
\mathcal{ALCHI}
, e.g., in
[
\citeauthoryearManière2022
]
.

See
2

Proof.

Assume to the contrary that
\mathcal{J}
is not a model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
As
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
by prerequisite, there is thus a model
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{J}
. To derive a contradiction, we construct a model
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}
with
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and show that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
.
For each type
t\in{\sf TP}_{\overline{{\sf core}}}(\mathcal{I})
, let

\begin{array}[]{rcl}D_{t}&=&\{d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\mid{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)=t\}\\[2.84526pt]
S_{t}&=&\{{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}(d)\mid d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}:{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(d)=t\}.\end{array}

We have

•

|D_{t}|\geq|{\sf TP}(\mathcal{T})|
by definition of

\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
and

•

|S_{t}|\leq|{\sf TP}(\mathcal{T})|
, by definition of
S_{t}
.

Moreover, the ‘
\supseteq
’ direction of Point 2 implies
S_{t}\neq\emptyset
.
We can thus find a surjective function
\pi_{t}:D_{t}\rightarrow S_{t}
.

Let
\pi
be the function that is obtained as the union of
the (domain disjoint) functions
\pi_{t}
, for all
t\in{\sf TP}_{\overline{{\sf core}}}(\mathcal{I})
.
By definition of
\pi
and of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{\sf core}
, the domain of
\pi
is

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\big(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{\sf core}\cup{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\big).

Now consider the range of
\pi
.
By Point 1 and the ‘
\subseteq
’ direction of Point 2,

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(d)\in{\sf TP}_{\overline{{\sf core}}}(\mathcal{I})

iff

d\notin\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{\sf core}
. By definition, the range
of
\pi
is thus

\{{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}(d)\mid d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{\sf core}\}.

Extend
\pi
to domain
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
by setting
\pi(d)={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}(d)
for all

d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{\sf core}\cup{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
.
It is easy to see that now,
\pi
is a surjective function from
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
to
{\sf TP}(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})
.
We construct an interpretation
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\mid{{A}}\in\pi(d)\}

\displaystyle{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{(d,e)\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\times\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\mid\pi(d)\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}\pi(e)\}

for all concept names
A
and role names
r
. Notice that

\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}({d})=\pi(d)
, hence

\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})
. We first show that

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
by considering each possible shape
of assertions and inclusion (recall that
\mathcal{T}
is in
normal form):

•

{{A}}(a)
.
Since
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
, we have
a\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
, hence
{{A}}\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}({a})
.
By definition of
\pi
, we have
\pi(a)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}({a})
, hence
{{A}}\in\pi(a)
.
The definition of
{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
yields
a\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

•

{{r}}(a,b)
.
Since
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
, we have
(a,b)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
, hence
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}({a})\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}({b})
.
By definition of
\pi
, we have
\pi(a)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}({a})
and
\pi(b)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}({b})
.
The definition of
{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
yields
(a,b)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

•

Satisfaction of CIs of shape
\top\sqsubseteq{{A}}
,
{{A_{1}}}\sqcap{{A_{2}}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
,

{{A}}\sqsubseteq{{\lnot B}}
and
{{\lnot B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
immediately follows
from
\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}

being a model of
\mathcal{T}
.

•

{{A}}\sqsubseteq\exists{{r}}.{{B}}
.
Let
d\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.
Then
{{A}}\in\pi(d)
.
Since
\pi(d)\in\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})
, there exists
d^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
such that
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}({d^{\prime}})=\pi(d)
.
Since
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
, there exists
e^{\prime}\in{{B}}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
such that
(d^{\prime},e^{\prime})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
.
In particular,
{{B}}\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}({e^{\prime}})
and
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}({d^{\prime}})\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}({e^{\prime}})
.
It follows from
\pi
being surjective that there exists
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
with
\pi(e)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}({e^{\prime}})
.
Then
e\in{{B}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
and
(d,e)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
by definition of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, hence
d\in(\exists{{r}}.{{B}})^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

•

\exists{{r}}.{{B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
.
Let
d\in(\exists{{r}}.{{B}})^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.
Then there exists
e\in{{B}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
such that
(d,e)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.
By definition of
{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, we have
\pi(d)\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}\pi(e)
.
Since
{{B}}\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}({e})=\pi(e)
and
\exists{{r}}.{{B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
, the definition of
\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}
yields
{{A}}\in\pi(d)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}({d})
.
Thus
d\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

•

r\sqsubseteq s
.
Let
(d,e)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. Then
\pi(d)\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}\pi(e)
.
Since
\mathcal{T}\models r\sqsubseteq s
, it is immediate that
\pi(d)\rightsquigarrow_{{s}}\pi(e)
.
Therefore the definition of
{{s}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
yields
(d,e)\in{{s}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
.
We make use of the following claim.

Claim 1
.

A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\odot A^{\mathcal{I}}
iff
A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\odot A^{\mathcal{J}}
,
for each concept name
A
and
\odot\in\{\subseteq,\supseteq\}
.

Let
A
be a concept name.
We prove the claim for
\odot=\;\subseteq
only; for
\odot=\;\supseteq
, the arguments are similar.

“
\Rightarrow
”.
Let
A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}}
and
d\in A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
; we need to show that
d\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
.
First assume that
d\in\Delta_{\sf core}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Then

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(d)
and

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(d)={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}(d)
. So

A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}}
clearly implies

d\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
, as required. Now assume that

d\notin\Delta_{\sf core}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Let

t={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(d)
. Then
{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}(d)\in S_{t}

and consequently we find a
d^{\prime}\in D_{t}
with

\pi(d^{\prime})={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}(d)
. This implies

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(d^{\prime})={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}(d)
. Since

d^{\prime}\in D_{t}
, we have

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)=t={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(d)
.
So again,
A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}}
implies

d\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
, as required.

“
\Leftarrow
”.
Let
A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\subseteq A^{\mathcal{J}}
and
d\in A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
; we need to show
d\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
.
First assume that
d\in\Delta_{\sf core}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Then

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(d)
and

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(d)={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}(d)
. So

A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\subseteq A^{\mathcal{J}}
clearly implies

d\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
, as required. Now assume that

d\notin\Delta_{\sf core}^{\mathcal{I}}
and let

t^{\prime}={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(d)
. By definition of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, we
have
t^{\prime}=\pi(d)
. By definition of
\pi
, there is a

t\in{\sf TP}_{\overline{{\sf core}}}(\mathcal{I})
such that
