. Then
d\in D_{t}
and
t^{\prime}\in S_{t}
. The
former yields
{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)=t
and due to the
latter, there is a
d^{\prime}
such that

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(d)=t={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)
and

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}(d)=t^{\prime}={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(d)
.
So again,
A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\subseteq A^{\mathcal{J}}
implies

d\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
, as required.

It is easy to see that since

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
, the claim implies
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{J}
.
We have derived a contradiction and conclude that
\mathcal{J}
is a
model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
, as desired.
∎

Proof.

We use Lemma

\mathcal{I}
as the reference model, and it

\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}

(*)

and the fact that

for all

is satisfied. We next
verify that Condition 2 is also satisfied. First, let

d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}
. We have to show
that
{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(d)\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
. If

d\in\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, then
h(d)=d
. From

d\notin\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, we get

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
and it remains to apply
(
*
). If
d\notin\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, then

h(d)\notin\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and again we may use (
*
).
Conversely, let
t\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
. We have to show that
there is a
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}
with

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}}(d)=t
. But that
d
is
e_{t}
since
h(e_{t})=e_{t}
and by
(
*
).
∎

See
4

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
and let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
Assume that
t_{i}\notin\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, for every
t\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
and
1\leq i\leq m
. Define
\mathcal{J}
by setting

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}c@{~}l}\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}&=&{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\cup\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}\cup\{t_{i}\mid t\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I}),1\leq i\leq m\}\\
{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}&=&({{A}}^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})\cup\{t_{i}\mid{{A}}\in t,1\leq i\leq m\}\\
{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}&=&({{r}}^{\mathcal{I}}\cap(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\times\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}))\hfill\text{(i)}\\
&&\cup\,\{(e,t_{i})\mid e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({e})\rightsquigarrow_{{{r}}}t,1\leq i\leq m\}\hfill\text{(ii)}\\
&&\cup\,\{(t_{i},e)\mid e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},t\rightsquigarrow_{{{r}}}\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({e}),1\leq i\leq m\}\hfill\text{(iii)}\\
&&\cup\,\{(t_{i},t^{\prime}_{j})\mid t\rightsquigarrow_{{{r}}}t^{\prime},1\leq i,j\leq m\}\hfill\text{(iv)}\end{array}

for all concept names
A
and role names
r
. It is easy to see
that
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}}({e})=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({e})

for all
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
and

\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}}({t_{i}})=t
for all
t\in\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{T})
and
1\leq i\leq m
. This implies that

\mathcal{I}_{\mid\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}}=\mathcal{J}_{\mid\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{J}}}

and
\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})=\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{J})
as desired.
It also implies that Conditions 1 and 2 from Lemma
2
are satisfied.
To show that
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
, it thus suffices to
show
that
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
. It is clear by construction that

\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{A}
. For
\mathcal{T}
, we consider all different
forms of axioms:

•

Satisfaction of CIs of the form
\top\sqsubseteq{{A}}
,
{{A_{1}}}\sqcap{{A_{2}}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
,

{{A}}\sqsubseteq{{\lnot B}}
and
{{\lnot B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
immediately follows
from
\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{J})\subseteq\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{I})
and
\mathcal{I}

being a model of
\mathcal{T}
. We distinguish two cases:

•

{{A}}\sqsubseteq\exists{{r}}.{{B}}
.
Let
d\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

–

If
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, then we have
d\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Since
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
, there exists
e\in{{B}}^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(d,e)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
If
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, then
(d,e)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
due to Case (i) in the definition
of
r^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Otherwise
t:=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({e})\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
.
In particular,
{{B}}\in t
and
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({d})\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}t
.
Therefore
t_{1}\in{{B}}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
(d,t_{1})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
due to Case (ii), proving
d\in(\exists{{r}}.{{B}})^{\mathcal{J}}
.

–

If
d\notin\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, then
d=t_{i}
for some
t\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
and
1\leq i\leq m
.
By definition of
\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
, there exists
d^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
s.t.
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({d^{\prime}})=t=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}}({d})
.
From
d\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, we get
A\in t
.
Therefore
d^{\prime}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and since
\mathcal{I}
is
a model of
\mathcal{T}
, there exists
e\in{{B}}^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(d^{\prime},e)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
In particular,
{{B}}\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({e})
and
t\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({e})
.
If
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
, then
e\in B^{\mathcal{J}}
and
Case (iii) in the definition of
{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}

ensures
(d,e)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, which gives
d\in(\exists{{r}}.{{B}})^{\mathcal{J}}
.
If
e\notin\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
, then
t^{\prime}=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({e})\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
. Let
e^{\prime}=t^{\prime}_{j}
for some
j
with

1\leq j\leq m
.
Case (iv) in the definition of
{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
yields
(d,e^{\prime})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, which again gives
d\in(\exists{{r}}.{{B}})^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

\exists{{r}}.{{B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
.
Let
d\in(\exists{{r}}.{{B}})^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Then there exists
e\in{{B}}^{\mathcal{J}}
such that
(d,e)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
From each case in definition of
{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, we easily get
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}}({d})\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}}({e})
.
Since
e\in{{B}}^{\mathcal{J}}
we have in particular
{{B}}\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}}({e})
.
Combined with
\mathcal{T}\models\exists{{r}}.{{B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
and the definition of
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}}({d})\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}}({e})
, we obtain
{{A}}\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{J}}({d})
, that is
d\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

r\sqsubseteq s
.
Let
(d,e)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
If this is due to Case (i) in the definition of

{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, then

(d,e)\in{{s}}^{\mathcal{J}}
since
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
.
For all other three cases, it suffices to note that,
due to
r\sqsubseteq s\in\mathcal{T}
,
t_{1}\rightsquigarrow_{{r}}t_{2}
implies
t_{1}\rightsquigarrow_{{s}}t_{2}
for all types
t_{1},t_{2}
.

Finally, we remark that the size of
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
is bounded by
{\left|{{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})}\right|}+{\left|{\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}}\right|}+m\cdot{\left|{\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})}\right|}
.
From
{\left|{\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}}\right|}\leq m\cdot{\left|{\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{core}}(\mathcal{I})}\right|}
and
{\left|{\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{core}}(\mathcal{I})}\right|}+{\left|{\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})}\right|}={\left|{\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{I})}\right|}\leq m
we obtain

{\left|{\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}}\right|}\leq{\left|{\mathcal{A}}\right|}+m^{2}
.
Recalling
m={\left|{\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{T})}\right|}\leq 2^{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}
,
we obtain
|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|\leq{\left|{\mathcal{A}}\right|}+2^{2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}}
as required.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for Section
3.2

See
5

To prove the “
\Leftarrow
” direction of Lemma
5
, assume we are given
a good mosaic
M_{0}
.
We aim to extend
\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}
, hence
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}
, into a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{T}
that
satisfies Conditions (a) to (c).
For each good mosaic
M
, each
e\in(A\sqcap\neg\exists r.B)^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
for some
A
such that
A\sqsubseteq\exists r.B\in\mathcal{T}
,
we choose a good mosaic
M^{\prime}
such that:

1.

{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}_{M}}(e)={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}_{M^{\prime}}}(e)
;

2.

e\in(\exists r.B)^{\mathcal{J}_{M^{\prime}}}
;

3.

if
(p,\widehat{p},h)\in S_{M}
, then
(p,\widehat{p},h^{\prime})\in S_{M^{\prime}}
where

h^{\prime}
is the restriction of
h
to range
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}\cup\{e\}
.

We use
\mathsf{ch}^{{{r}}.{{B}}}_{M,e}
to denote this chosen
M^{\prime}
.
Then, starting from
M_{0}
, we build a tree-shaped set of words which witnesses the acceptance of
M_{0}
.

Definition 3
.

The mosaic tree
M_{0}^{+}
is the smallest set of words such that:

•

(M_{0},\emptyset,\emptyset)\in M_{0}^{+}
;

•

If
w\cdot(M,\_,\_)\in M_{0}^{+}
and
e\in(A\sqcap\neg\exists r.B)^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
for some
A
such that
A\sqsubseteq\exists r.B\in\mathcal{T}
, then
w\cdot(M,\_,\_)\cdot(\mathsf{ch}^{{{r}}.{{B}}}_{M,e},{{r}}.{{B}},e)\in M_{0}^{+}
.

It remains to ‘glue’ together the interpretations
\mathcal{J}_{M}
according to the structure of
M_{0}^{+}
.
Since a mosaic
M
may occur more than once,
we create a copy of
\mathcal{J}_{M}
for each node in
M_{0}^{+}
of the form
w\cdot(M,\_,\_)
.
We do not duplicate however elements from
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}
as they precisely are those we want to reuse.
Hence only the two special elements
e^{*}_{1}
and
e^{*}_{2}
may be duplicated.
When a node
w\cdot(M_{1},r_{1}.B_{1},e_{1})\cdot(M_{2},r_{2}.B_{2},e_{2})
is encountered, we merge element
e_{2}
from
M_{2}
with the already-existing
e_{2}
from
M_{1}
, which is indicated with a superscript
w\cdot(M_{1},r_{1}.B_{1},e_{1})
as follows
e_{2}^{w\cdot(M_{1},r_{1}.B_{1},e_{1})}
.
Other elements from
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
are considered freshly introduced by this node, which is indicated with a matching superscript, that is:
e^{w\cdot(M_{1},r_{1}.B_{1},e_{1})\cdot(M_{2},r_{2}.B_{2},e_{2})}
.
Formally, the copying and merging of elements is achieved by the
following duplicating functions, defined for each
w\cdot(M,r.B,e)\in M_{0}^{+}
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}l}\lambda_{w\cdot(M,r.B,e)}:\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}\cup\{(e_{i}^{*})^{w},(e_{i}^{*})^{w\cdot(M,r.B,e)}\}\\
d\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}d&\text{if }d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}\\
d^{w}&\text{if }d=e\notin\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}\\
d^{w\cdot(M,r.B,e)}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

The desired model
{\mathcal{I}}
can then be defined as:

{\mathcal{I}}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\cdot(M,r.B,e)\in M_{0}^{+}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{w\cdot(M,r.B,e)}({\mathcal{J}_{M}}),

that is the domain (resp. the interpretation of each concept name and each role name) of
\mathcal{I}
is the union across all
w\cdot(M,r.B,e)\in M_{0}^{+}
of the image by
\lambda_{w\cdot(M,r.B,e)}
of the domain (resp. the interpretation of each concept name and each role name) of
\mathcal{J}_{M}
.

By definition, each
\lambda_{w\cdot(M,r.B,e)}
is a homomorphism from
\mathcal{J}_{M}
to
{\mathcal{I}}
.
Due to Condition 1 in the definition of good mosaics, if the range of two duplicating functions overlap, then the common element satisfies the same concepts, so concept membership in
{\mathcal{I}}
transfers back to
\mathcal{J}_{M}
.
More formally:

Lemma 13
.

For all
w\cdot(M,r.B,e)\in M_{0}^{+}
, all
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
and all
{{A}}\in\mathsf{N_{C}}
, it holds that
\lambda_{w\cdot(M,r.B,e)}({d})\in{{A}}^{{\mathcal{I}}}
iff
d\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
.

Proof.

Let
u_{1}=w_{1}\cdot(M_{1},r_{1}.B_{1},e_{1})\in M_{0}^{+}
be a node from the mosaic tree,
d_{1}
an element from
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}}
and
{{A}}\in\mathsf{N_{C}}
.

“
\Leftarrow
”.
This is the easy direction.
Assume
d_{1}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}}
.
It follows from the definition of
{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}}
that
\lambda_{u_{1}}({d_{1}})\in{{A}}^{{\mathcal{I}}}
.

“
\Rightarrow
”.
Assume
\lambda_{u_{1}}({d_{1}})\in{{A}}^{{\mathcal{I}}}
.
By definition of
{{A}}^{{\mathcal{I}}}
there exists a node
u_{2}=w_{2}\cdot(M_{2},r_{2}.B_{2},e_{2})
from the mosaic tree, and an element
d_{2}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}
such that
\lambda_{u_{1}}({d_{1}})=\lambda_{u_{2}}({d_{2}})
.
We refer to this equality as
(*)
and distinguish 5 cases.

1.

d_{1}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
or
d_{2}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
.

(*)
yields
d_{1}=d_{2}
.
Interpretation
\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}
preserves
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}
, hence
d_{2}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
.
Interpretation
\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}
preserves
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}
, hence
d_{1}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}}
.

In the remaining cases, we thus assume
d_{1},d_{2}\notin\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
.

2.

d_{1}=e_{1}
and
d_{2}=e_{2}
.

(*)
yields
M_{1}=M_{2}
and
d_{1}=d_{2}
.

3.

d_{1}\neq e_{1}
and
d_{2}=e_{2}
.

(*)
yields
w_{1}=w_{2}\cdot(M_{2},r_{2}.B_{2},e_{2})
and
d_{1}=d_{2}
.
In particular,
M_{1}=\mathsf{ch}^{r_{2},B_{2}}_{M_{2},e_{2}}
.
From Condition 1 in the definition of good mosaics, we obtain
d_{1}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}}
.

4.

d_{1}=e_{1}
and
d_{2}\neq e_{2}
.

The same arguments as for Case 3 but this time with
M_{2}=\mathsf{ch}^{r_{1},B_{1}}_{M_{1},e_{1}}
.

5.

d_{1}\neq e_{1}
and
d_{2}\neq e_{2}
.

(*)
yields the existence of
w\cdot(M,r.B,e)
such that
w_{1}=w_{2}=w\cdot(M,r.B,e)
.
It follows that
M_{1}=\mathsf{ch}^{r,B}_{M,e}
and
M_{2}=\mathsf{ch}^{r,B}_{M,e}
.
Using the same arguments as in Cases 3 and 4, we obtain
d_{1}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}}
. ∎

With Lemma
13
in hand, we are ready to show that
{\mathcal{I}}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
.

Lemma 14
.

{\mathcal{I}}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

We consider each possible shape of assertion and axiom in
\mathcal{K}
w.r.t. the normal form from Lemma
1
.

•

Assertions
{{A}}(a)
and
{{p}}(a,b)
from
\mathcal{A}
are satisfied in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}
, which is preserved in
\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}
(Condition 2 in the definition of a mosaic), hence are also satisfied in
{\mathcal{I}}
.

•

Axioms with shapes
{\top}\sqsubseteq{A}
,
{A_{1}\sqcap A_{2}}\sqsubseteq{A}
,
{{A}}\sqsubseteq{{\lnot B}}
and
{{\lnot B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
are satisfied from Lemma
13
and the fact every mosaic is only allowed to give proper types to its element.

•

Axioms with shapes
\exists{r}.A_{1}\sqsubseteq A_{2}
and
{p}\sqsubseteq{r}
are satisfied from Lemma
13
and Condition 4 in the definition of mosaics, precisely requiring those to hold.

•

Axioms with shape
{A_{1}}\sqsubseteq\exists r.A_{2}
are those that really involve the mosaics.
Let
d_{1}\in{{a}}_{1}^{{\mathcal{I}}}
.
By definition of
{{a_{1}}}^{{\mathcal{I}}}
, there exist a node
u=w\cdot(M,\_,\_)\in M_{0}^{+}
and an element
d_{1}^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
such that
d_{1}^{\prime}\in{{a_{1}}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
and
\lambda_{u}({d_{1}^{\prime}})=d_{1}
.
If
d_{1}^{\prime}\in(\exists r.A_{2})^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
, then we pick
d_{2}^{\prime}\in A_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
and
(d_{1}^{\prime},d_{2}^{\prime})\in r^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
.
It then follows immediately that
\lambda_{u}({d_{2}^{\prime}})\in A_{2}^{{\mathcal{I}}}
and
(\lambda_{u}({d_{1}^{\prime}}),\lambda_{u}({d_{2}^{\prime}}))\in r^{{\mathcal{I}}}
, ensuring
d_{1}\in(\exists r.A_{2})^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Otherwise
d_{1}^{\prime}\notin(\exists r.A_{2})^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
, thus we have
u_{0}=u\cdot(M_{0},r.A_{2},d_{1}^{\prime})\in M_{0}^{+}
with
M_{0}=\mathsf{ch}^{r.A_{2}}_{M,d_{1}^{\prime}}
.
Notice
\lambda_{u}({d_{1}^{\prime}})=\lambda_{u_{0}}({d_{1}^{\prime}})
.
From Condition 2 in the definition of good mosaics, we get
d_{1}^{\prime}\in(\exists r.A_{2})^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}}
hence we pick
d_{2}^{\prime}\in A_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}}
and
(d_{1}^{\prime},d_{2}^{\prime})\in r^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}}
.
It then follows immediately that
\lambda_{u_{0}}({d_{2}^{\prime}})\in A_{2}^{{\mathcal{I}}}
and
(\lambda_{u_{0}}({d_{1}^{\prime}}),\lambda_{u_{0}}({d_{2}^{\prime}}))\in r^{{\mathcal{I}}}
, ensuring
d_{1}\in(\exists r.A_{2})^{\mathcal{I}}
.

∎

This proves
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}
can indeed by extended into a model of
\mathcal{T}
.
Notice that Condition (a) is clearly satisfied due to Condition 2 in the definition of mosaics.
Similarly, Condition (b) is clearly satisfied from Condition 3 in the definition of mosaics joint with Lemma
13
.
It remains to verify Condition (c), that is for all
p\in q
, there is no homomorphism from
p
in
{\mathcal{I}}
.

By contradiction, assume there exists a CQ
p\in q
and a homomorphism
\pi:p\rightarrow{\mathcal{I}}
.
Notice that for each
v\in\mathsf{var}(p)
s.t.
\pi(v)\notin\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
, we thus have
w
such that
\pi(v)=d^{w}
for some
d\in\{e^{*}_{1},e^{*}_{2}\}
.
To identify from which nodes the facts of
\pi(p)
come involving

d^{w}
, we notice the following consequence of the definition of functions
\lambda_{w}
for
w\in M_{0}^{+}
.

Remark 1
.

Let
w\in M_{0}^{+}
and
d\in\{e^{*}_{1},e^{*}_{2}\}
.
For all
w^{\prime}\cdot(M,r.B,e)\in M_{0}^{+}
, we have
d^{w}\in\lambda_{w^{\prime}\cdot(M,r.B,e)}({\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}})
iff one of the two following conditions holds:

•

w=w^{\prime}\cdot(M,r.B,e)
;

•

w=w^{\prime}
and
d=e
.

It follows that, for each
v\in\mathsf{var}(p)
s.t.
\pi(v)\notin\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
, the set of nodes
W_{v}=\{w^{\prime}\cdot(M,r.B,e)\in M_{0}^{+}\mid\pi(v)\in\lambda_{w^{\prime}\cdot(M,r.B,e)}({\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}})\}
is finite.
On
M_{0}^{+}
, we consider the order given by the prefix relation, that for all
w_{1},w_{2}\in M_{0}^{+}
, we define
w_{1}\leq w_{2}
iff
w_{1}
is a prefix of
w_{2}
.
We now denote
W_{p}
the prefix closure of
\bigcup_{v}W_{v}
, where
v
ranges over
v\in\mathsf{var}(p)
s.t.
\pi(v)\notin\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
.
In particular,
(M_{0},\emptyset,\emptyset)\in W_{p}
.
For every
w\in M_{0}^{+}
, we define the interpretation:

\mathcal{I}_{w}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}w^{\prime}\cdot(M,r.B,e)\in M_{0}^{+}\\
w\leq w^{\prime}\cdot(M,r.B,e)\end{subarray}}\lambda_{w^{\prime}\cdot(M,r.B,e)}({\mathcal{J}_{M}}).

We further set
p_{w}\subseteq p
consisting of those atoms from
p
that are mapped by
\pi
in
\mathcal{I}_{w}
, and
\pi_{w}
the subsequent homomorphism, that is the restriction of
\pi
that maps to
\mathcal{I}_{w}
, i.e.:

\pi_{w}=\pi|_{\pi^{-1}(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{w}})}.

In particular
p_{(M_{0},\emptyset,\emptyset)}=p
and
\pi_{(M_{0},\emptyset,\emptyset)}=\pi
.
We now claim the following:

Lemma 15
.

For all nodes
u=w\cdot(M,r.B,e)\in W_{p}
,
we have
(p,p_{u},\pi^{\prime})\in S_{M}

where
\pi^{\prime}=(\lambda_{u})^{-1}\circ\pi_{u}|_{\Delta}

with
\Delta=\pi_{u}^{-1}(\lambda_{u}({\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}}))
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on elements of
W_{p}
, starting from its finitely many maximal elements w.r.t.
\leq
.

Base case.

Assume
u=w\cdot(M,r.B,e)\in W_{p}
is maximal for
\leq
.
that is for all
u^{\prime}\in M_{0}^{+}
s.t.
u\leq u^{\prime}
, we have
u^{\prime}\notin W_{p}
.
In particular, for all
v\in\mathsf{var}(p)
,
\pi(v)\in\lambda_{u^{\prime}}({{\mathcal{J}_{M^{\prime}}}})
, where
M^{\prime}
is the mosaic of node
u^{\prime}
, implies
\pi(v)\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
.
Therefore
\pi_{u}^{-1}(\lambda_{u}({\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}}))=\mathsf{var}(p_{u})
and, since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}
is preserved in every mosaic (Condition 2 in the definition), it follows that
p_{u}
fully embeds in
\lambda_{u}({\mathcal{J}_{M}})
via
\pi_{u}
.
From
S_{M}
being saturated, we thus have
(p,p_{u},(\lambda_{u})^{-1}\circ\pi_{u})
which is the desired triple.

Induction case.

Consider
u=w\cdot(M,r.B,e)\in W_{p}
and assume the property holds for all
u\in W_{p}
with
u\leq u^{\prime}
.
We cover
p_{u}
by the set of all
p_{u\cdot(M^{\prime},r^{\prime}.B^{\prime},e^{\prime})}
s.t.
u\cdot(M^{\prime},r^{\prime}.B^{\prime},e^{\prime})\in W_{p}
and by the remaining atoms
p_{=u}
from
p_{u}
.
Notice that this is not a partition of
p_{u}
as those subqueries may overlap.
In particular, two distinct subqueries
p_{u\cdot(M_{1},r_{1}.B_{1},e_{1})}
and
p_{u\cdot(M_{2},r_{2}.B_{2},e_{2})}
may only share those variables which are mapped to
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
and, if
e_{1}=e_{2}
, on
e_{1}
(that is also
e_{2}
in this case).
Respectively,
p_{u\cdot(M^{\prime},r^{\prime}.B^{\prime},e^{\prime})}
and
p_{=u}
may only share variables that are mapped either on
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
or on
e^{\prime}
.
We cover as well the homomorphism
\pi_{u}
by all
\pi_{u\cdot(M^{\prime},r^{\prime}.B^{\prime},e^{\prime})}
s.t.
u\cdot(M^{\prime},r^{\prime}.B^{\prime},e^{\prime})\in W_{p}
and by the homomorphism
\pi_{=u}
which is defined as the restriction of
\pi_{u}
to variables from
p_{=u}
.
Notice that from
S_{M}
being saturated, we obtain
(p,p_{=u},(\lambda_{u})^{-1}\circ\pi_{=u})\in S_{M}
.
We further apply the induction hypothesis on each
u\cdot(M^{\prime},r^{\prime}.B^{\prime},e^{\prime})\in W_{p}
, which provides corresponding triples
(p,p_{u\cdot(M^{\prime},r^{\prime}.B^{\prime},e^{\prime})},\pi^{\prime})
in each
S_{M^{\prime}}
.
From Condition 3 in the definition of good mosaics, if follows that each
(p,p_{u\cdot(M^{\prime},r^{\prime}.B^{\prime},e^{\prime})},\pi^{\prime\prime})
belongs to
S_{M}
, where
\pi^{\prime\prime}
denotes the restriction of
\pi^{\prime}
to range
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}\cup\{e^{\prime}\}
.
It remains to form the union (in the sense given in the definition of a saturated mosaic) of all those subsequent triples, along with
(p,p_{=u},(\lambda_{u})^{-1}\circ\pi_{=u})
, to obtain the desired triple.
Let us first focus on two triples
(p,p_{u\cdot(M_{1},r_{1}.B_{1},e_{1})},\pi_{1}^{\prime\prime})
and
(p,p_{u\cdot(M_{2},r_{2}.B_{2},e_{2})},\pi_{2}^{\prime\prime})
and consider
v\in\mathsf{var}(p_{u\cdot(M_{1},r_{1}.B_{1},e_{1})})\cap\mathsf{var}(p_{u\cdot(M_{2},r_{2}.B_{2},e_{2})})
.
From our covering of
p_{u}
, we have seen that either
\pi_{u}(v)\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
, or, if
e_{1}=e_{2}
, that
\pi(v)=e_{1}
.
In any case, restricting
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
to
{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}\cup\{e_{1}\}}
preserved
\pi_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime\prime}
being defined on
v
, and, since issuing from the same
\pi_{u}
, equal.
Let us now move to a triple
(p,p_{u\cdot(M^{\prime},r^{\prime}.B^{\prime},e^{\prime})},\pi^{\prime\prime})
and the triple
(p,p_{=u},(\lambda_{u})^{-1}\circ\pi_{=u})
.
Let
v\in\mathsf{var}(p_{u\cdot(M^{\prime},r^{\prime}.B^{\prime},e^{\prime})})\cap\mathsf{var}(p_{=u})
.
From our covering of
p_{u}
, we know
\pi_{u}(v)\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}\cup\{e^{\prime}\}
.
Again restricting to
{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}\cup\{e^{\prime}\}}
preserved
\pi^{\prime\prime}
being defined on
v
and, since issuing from the same
\pi_{u}
, equal to
\pi_{=u}
on
v
.
Therefore, we can form the union of all those triples, since they are 2-by-2 compatible, which provides the desired triple in
S_{M}
.
∎

This concludes the proof of the “
\Leftarrow
” direction of Lemma
5
, as existence of such a homomorphism
p
then yields a complete triple in
S_{M_{0}}
, contradicting
M_{0}
being a mosaic.

We now turn to the “
\Rightarrow
” direction.
Assume that
\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}
can be extended to a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{T}
that
satisfies Conditions (a) to (c).
Up to introducing
{\left|{\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{T})}\right|}
copies of each element from
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
, we can safely assume
\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
.
Indeed, the resulting interpretations is still a model of
\mathcal{K}
and still satisfies Conditions (a) to (c).

Set
\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}:=\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}
and
S_{M_{0}}:=\{(p,\widehat{p},h|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}})\mid p\in q,\widehat{p}\subseteq p,h:\widehat{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}\text{ is a homomorphism}\}
.
Condition (c) ensures in particular that
S_{M_{0}}
only contains incomplete triples.
We denote
M_{0}:=(\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}},S_{M_{0}})
this initial mosaic.

Starting from
\mathcal{M}:=\{M_{0}\}
, we extend
\mathcal{M}
into a set of mosaics in which each mosaic
M\in\mathcal{M}
is good in
\mathcal{M}

and
realized in
\mathcal{I}
, meaning that
\mathcal{J}_{M}
homomorphically embeds into
\mathcal{I}
.
Notice
M_{0}
is indeed realized in
\mathcal{I}
, but may not be good in
\{M_{0}\}
.
To pursue the construction, given a mosaic
M\in\mathcal{M}
being realized in
\mathcal{I}
and some
e\in(A\sqcap\lnot\exists r.B)^{\mathcal{J}_{M}}
with
A\sqsubseteq\exists r.B\in\mathcal{T}
,
we show how to extract from
\mathcal{I}
another mosaic
N
to succeed to
M
, and we add
N
to
\mathcal{M}
.
Since the number of mosaics is finite, the construction of
\mathcal{M}
terminates.
We further check that all mosaics in the resulting
\mathcal{M}
set are good in
\mathcal{M}
(and in particular
M_{0}
).

To formalize the construction, we introduce along each mosaic
M
a function
\tau_{M}
being a homomorphism
\mathcal{J}_{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
.
We also assume chosen, for every
{{r}}.{{A}}\in\Omega
, a function
\mathsf{succ}^{\mathcal{I}}_{{{r}}.{{A}}}

that maps every element
e\in(\exists{r.A})^{\mathcal{I}}
to an element
e^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(e,e^{\prime})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
e^{\prime}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

Definition 4
.

We define
\mathcal{M}
as the smallest set such that:

•

M_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
and we set
\tau_{M_{0}}=\mathsf{Id}_{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}}
, where
\mathsf{Id}_{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}}
denotes the identity function.

•

For each
M_{1}\in\mathcal{M}
and for each
e_{1}\in(A\sqcap\lnot\exists r.B)^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}}
with
A\sqsubseteq\exists r.B\in\mathcal{T}
, we denote
\tau_{1}:=\tau_{M_{1}}
and set
e_{1}^{\prime}:=\tau_{1}(e_{1})
.
Since
\tau_{1}
is a homomorphism and
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
,
we obtain
e_{1}^{\prime}\in(\exists{r.B})^{\mathcal{I}}
and can set

e_{2}^{\prime}:=\mathsf{succ}^{\mathcal{I}}_{{{r}}.{{B}}}(e_{1}^{\prime})
.
If
e_{2}^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
, then we set
e_{2}:=e_{2}^{\prime}
, otherwise we set
e_{2}
to either
e^{*}_{1}
or
e^{*}_{2}
such that
e_{1}\neq e_{2}
.
We let
\tau_{2}
be the function that maps elements of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
to themselves,
e_{1}
to
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{2}
to
e_{2}^{\prime}
.
We now define a new mosaic
M_{2}
.
Its domain is
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}\cup\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
.
Its interpretation
\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}
is given by:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}&=&\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}\cup\{e_{1},e_{2}\}\\
{{C}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}&=&{{C}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}}\cup\{e_{k}\mid e_{k}^{\prime}\in{{C}}^{\mathcal{I}},k=1,2\}\\
{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}&=&{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}}\cup\{(e_{1},e_{2})\mid\mathcal{T}\models{r}\sqsubseteq{p}\}\\
&&\phantom{{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}}}\cup\{(e_{2},e_{1})\mid\mathcal{T}\models{r^{-}}\sqsubseteq{p}\}\end{array}

Its specification is
S_{M_{2}}=\{(p,\widehat{p},\tau_{2}^{-1}\circ h|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}\cup\{e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime}\}})\mid p\in q,\widehat{p}\subseteq p,h:\widehat{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}\text{ is a homomorphism}\}
.
We now add
M_{2}\in\mathcal{M}
, and if
\tau_{M_{2}}
is not already defined, then we set
\tau_{M_{2}}:=\tau_{2}
.

Recalling that
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
it is then straightforward to verify that
M_{2}
, from the induction case, is a mosaic satisfying all conditions to succeed to
M_{1}
for
e
and
A\sqsubseteq\exists r.B
, and
that
\tau_{2}
(thus
\tau_{M_{2}}
) is a homomorphism.
These properties are verified by the next two lemmas.

Lemma 16
.

Each
M\in\mathcal{M}
is a well-defined mosaic. Furthermore,
\tau_{M}
is a homomorphism from
\mathcal{J}_{M}
to
\mathcal{I}
.

Proof.

The base case consisting of verifying that
M_{0}
is a mosaic is trivial, and
\mathsf{Id}_{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}}
is indeed a homomorphism.

We move to the induction case.
Consider
M_{2}
and
\tau_{2}
as obtained from some
M_{1}\in\mathcal{M}
and
e_{1}\in(A\sqcap\lnot\exists r.B)^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}}
with
A\sqsubseteq\exists r.B\in\mathcal{T}
.
By induction hypothesis,
M_{1}
is a mosaic and
\tau_{1}:=\tau_{M_{1}}
is a homomorphism from
\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}
to
\mathcal{I}
.

We first verify that
\tau_{2}
is a homomorphism:

•

Let
u\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}
.
If
u\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}}
, then in particular
u\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
hence
\tau_{2}(u)=u\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}}\subseteq{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Otherwise,
u=e_{k}
for
k=1
or
k=2
with
e_{k}^{\prime}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
In that case, notice
\tau_{2}(u)=e_{k}^{\prime}
which concludes.

•

Let
(u,v)\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}
.
If
(u,v)\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}}
, then in particular
u,v\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
, hence
(\tau_{2}(u),\tau_{2}(v))=(u,v)\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{0}}}\subseteq{{p}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Otherwise, if
(u,v)=(e_{1},e_{2})
with
\mathcal{T}\models{r}\sqsubseteq{p}
, then notice that
(\tau_{2}(u),\tau_{2}(v))=(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
.
Since
e_{2}^{\prime}
is the successor of
e_{1}^{\prime}
for
{{r}}.{{B}}
in
\mathcal{I}
, and
\mathcal{I}
models
\mathcal{T}
, we obtain
(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{I}}
as desired.
Otherwise we have
(u,v)=(e_{2},e_{1})
with
\mathcal{T}\models{r^{-}}\sqsubseteq{p}
, then notice that
(\tau_{2}(u),\tau_{2}(v))=(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime})
.
Since
e_{2}^{\prime}
is the successor of
e_{1}^{\prime}
for
{{r}}.{{B}}
in
\mathcal{I}
, and
\mathcal{I}
models
\mathcal{T}
, we have
(e_{2}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime})\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{I}}
as desired.

We now verify that
M_{2}
is a well-defined mosaic.

•

Regarding
\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}
:

1.

By definition, we have
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}\uplus\{e^{*}_{1},e^{*}_{2}\}
as desired;

2.

Also by definition, we have
\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}
;

3.

From interpretations of concepts being inherited from
\mathcal{I}
that satisfies Condition (b), it follows that indeed
{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}(e_{i}^{*})\in T_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}
if
e_{i}^{*}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
;

4.

From
\mathcal{I}
, being a model of
\mathcal{K}
, it follows that
\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}
satisfies in particular all
\exists r.A\sqsubseteq B\in\mathcal{T}

and all
r\sqsubseteq s\in\mathcal{T}
.

•

Regarding
S_{M_{2}}
, it is the restriction of a saturated specification (up to
\tau_{2}
, which essentially rename elements), and hence is also saturated.
From
\mathcal{I}
satisfying Condition (c), we obtain that
S_{M_{2}}
doesn’t contain any complete triple. ∎

Lemma 17
.

For all
M\in\mathcal{M}
, the mosaic
M
is good in
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

Let
M_{1}\in\mathcal{M}
and
e_{1}\in(A\sqcap\lnot\exists r.B)^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}}
with
A\sqsubseteq\exists r.B\in\mathcal{T}
.
Consider
M_{2}
as obtained from the induction case of Definition
4
.
We have
M_{2}\in\mathcal{M}
and verify the three additional conditions:

1.

Interpretations of concepts names being directly imported from
\mathcal{I}
, we immediately have
{\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}}(e_{1})={\sf tp}_{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}(e_{1})
;

2.

Since we set
e_{2}^{\prime}:=\mathsf{succ}^{\mathcal{I}}_{{{r}}.{{B}}}(e_{1}^{\prime})
in the construction of
M_{2}
, it follows naturally that
e\in(\exists r.B)^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}
;

3.

This is immediate as any partial homomorphism from either
S_{M_{1}}
(resp.
S_{M_{2}}
) is the restriction to
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}}
(resp.
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}
) of a complete homomorphism to
\mathcal{I}
, which can in turn be restricted to
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{2}}}
(resp.
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{M_{1}}}
).
In fact, this proves: if
(p,\widehat{p},h)\in S_{M_{2}}
, then
(p,\widehat{p},h^{\prime})\in S_{M_{1}}
where

h^{\prime}
is the restriction of
h
to range
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}\cup\{e_{1}\}
, but also “the converse”, that is: if
(p,\widehat{p},h)\in S_{M_{1}}
, then
(p,\widehat{p},h^{\prime})\in S_{M_{2}}
where

h^{\prime}
is the restriction of
h
to range
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{{\sf base}}}\cup\{e_{1}\}
. ∎

See
2

Proof.

We give a polynomial time reduction from (Boolean) CQ evaluation on
{\cal EL}
KBs
with closed concept names, which is
2Exp
-hard
[
\citeauthoryearNgo, Ortiz, and Simkus2016
]
.
A
KB with closed concept names
takes the form
\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}

with
\mathcal{K}
a KB and
\Sigma\subseteq{\sf N_{C}}
a set of closed
concept names.
We say that an interpretation
\mathcal{I}

respects

\Sigma
if for all

A\in\Sigma
,
d\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
implies
A(d)\in\mathcal{A}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{I}
is a
model
of
\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}
if
\mathcal{I}
is a
model of
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{I}
respects
\Sigma
. Thus, the only
instances of a closed concept name are those that are
explicitly asserted in the ABox. Now the problem proved to be

2Exp
-hard in
[
\citeauthoryearNgo, Ortiz, and Simkus2016
]
is: given a KB with closed
concept names
\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}
and a Boolean CQ
q
, decide
whether
\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}\models q
. We remark that the formalism
in
[
\citeauthoryearNgo, Ortiz, and Simkus2016
]
also admits closed role names, but these are
not used in the hardness proof.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}
be an
{\cal EL}
KB with closed
concept names,
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
, and
q
a Boolean CQ.
Specializing to
{\cal EL}
the normal form presented in Section
2
,
we assume every concept inclusion in

\mathcal{T}
to have one of the following shapes:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\qquad}l@{\qquad}r@{\qquad}r}\top\sqsubseteq{{A}}&{{A}}\sqsubseteq\exists{{r}}.{{B}}&\exists{{r}}.{{B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}&{{A_{1}}}\sqcap{{A_{2}}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}\end{array}

where
{{A,A_{1},A_{2},B}}
range over
{\sf N_{C}}
and

r
ranges over
{\sf N_{R}}
.
We construct a
circumscribed
{\cal EL}
KB
{\sf Circ}_{\sf CP}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
with

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{T}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
, and a CQ
q^{\prime}
such that

\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}\models q
iff
{\sf Circ}_{\sf CP}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\models q^{\prime}
.

A natural first attempt is to use use
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=\mathcal{K}
and

q^{\prime}=q
, and to minimize the concept names in
\Sigma

while letting all other concept names vary. Apart from using
more than a single minimized concept name, this does not work
since the extension of closed concept names may be too large.
As a simple example, consider the empty ABox and the TBox

\top\sqsubseteq\exists r.A
with
A\in\Sigma
. This KB
is unsatisfiable when
A
is closed, but satisfiable when
A

is minimized. We thus use a more refined approach.

Let
{\sf CP}
be the circumscription pattern that minimizes the
fresh concept name
M
and lets all other symbols vary. In
what follows, we assemble
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and define

q^{\prime}
. For
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, we start from
\mathcal{A}
and extend with
additional assertions.
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
contains the CIs from
\mathcal{T}
in a modified form, as well as additional CIs, see below for
details.

We start with connecting the concept names in
\Sigma
to the
minimized concept name
M
:

\displaystyle A\sqsubseteq M

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }A\in\Sigma

(1)

Much of the reduction is concerned with preventing
non-asserted instances

d
of closed concept names
A\in\Sigma
, that is, elements
d

that satisfy

A
but are not an individual from
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
such that

A(d)\in\mathcal{A}
. There are three cases to be distinguished.

We first consider the case that
d=a\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
, but

A(d)\notin\mathcal{A}
. We mark such
a
with
a fresh concept name
L
:

\displaystyle\overline{A}(a)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }a\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\text{ with }A(a)\not\in\mathcal{A}

(2)

\displaystyle A\sqcap\overline{A}\sqsubseteq L

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }A\in\Sigma.

(3)

where
\overline{A}
is a fresh concept name for each
A\in\Sigma
.

We next want to achieve that any model
\mathcal{I}
that satisfies
a\in L^{\mathcal{I}}
for some ABox individual
a
also satisfies

\mathcal{I}\models q
and is thus ruled out as a countermodel
against the query being entailed. To
this end, we add a copy of
q
to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle A(x)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }A(x)\in\varphi

(4)

\displaystyle r(x,y)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }r(x,y)\in\varphi

(5)

assuming w.l.o.g. that
{\sf var}(q)\cap{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})=\emptyset
.

We want the copy of
q
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
to only become ‘active’
when the
concept name
L
is made true at some individual from
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
;
otherwise, it should
be ‘dormant’. The individual names in
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
, in contrast,
should always be active. To achieve this, we
include in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
the following assertions where
X
is
a fresh concept name indicating activeness and
u
is a
fresh role name:

\displaystyle X(a)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }a\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A}),

(6)

\displaystyle u(x,a)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }a\in{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})\text{
and }x\in{\sf var}(q)

(7)

We then define the CQ
q^{\prime}
to be

q^{\prime}=q\wedge\bigwedge_{x\in{\sf var}(q)}X(x),

and add to
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\exists u.L\sqsubseteq X.

(8)

Elements generated by existential quantifiers on the right-hand
side
of CIs in
\mathcal{T}
should of course also be active. Moreover,
dormant elements should not trigger CIs from
\mathcal{T}
as
this might generate ‘unjustified’ existential (thus active)
elements.
We deal with these issues by including in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
the following relativized version of
\mathcal{T}
(recall
\mathcal{T}
is in normal form):

\displaystyle X\sqsubseteq A

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }\top\sqsubseteq A\in\mathcal{T}

(9)

\displaystyle X\sqcap A\sqsubseteq\exists r.(X\sqcap B)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }A\sqsubseteq\exists r.B\in\mathcal{T}

(10)

\displaystyle X\sqcap\exists r.(X\sqcap B)\sqsubseteq A

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }\exists r.B\sqsubseteq A\in\mathcal{T}

(11)

\displaystyle X\sqcap A_{1}\sqcap A_{2}\sqsubseteq A

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }A_{1}\sqcap A_{2}\sqsubseteq A\in\mathcal{T}

(12)

Note that, when activating the copy of
q
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
by
making
X
true at all its individual names, then the
CIs (
9
)–(
12
) apply also there and may make
concept names
A
with
A\in\Sigma
true at elements

x\in{\sf var}(q)
. Because of (
1
), this
may lead to models in which the copy of
q
is activated to
be incomparable to models in which it is not activated
regarding

<_{\sf CP}
. This can be remedied by adding to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle M(x)

\displaystyle\quad\text{ for all }x\in{\sf var}(q).

(13)

The second case of non-asserted instances
d
of closed concept
names is that
d
is not from
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\cup{\sf var}(q)
.
The idea is that models with
such instances
d
should be non-minimal.
We achieve this by including in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
a fresh individual

t
that must satisfy
M
, but not necessarily anything
else. Since any existential instance
d
of a closed concept
name must also satisfy
M
, we can find a model that is
preferred w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
by using
t
as a surrogate
for
d
, that is, making true at
t
exactly the concept
names true at
d
, rerouting all incoming and outgoing role
edges from
d
to
t
, and then removing
d
from the
extension of all concept and role names. We thus include in

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle M(t).

(14)

The third and last case of non-asserted instances
d
of closed
concept names is that
d
is
t
or an individual in the copy
of
q
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Since these individuals satisfy
M
, we may make true at them
a closed concept name without producing a
non-minimal model. To fix this problem, it suffices to
prevent the individuals in
{\sf var}(q)\cup\{t\}
to be used
as witnesses for existential restrictions in
\mathcal{T}
, as this is
the only possible reason for creating non-asserted instances of
closed concept names of the described form. It is, however,
easy to identify such witnesses as they must be active and
satisfy the concept name
X
. We thus add

\displaystyle\overline{X}(a)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }a\in{\sf var}(q)\cup\{t\}

(15)

\displaystyle u(x,a)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }a\in{\sf var}(q)\cup\{t\}\text{
and }x\in{\sf var}(q)

(16)

\displaystyle X\sqcap\overline{X}\sqsubseteq L.

(17)

where
\overline{X}
is a fresh concept name.
This finishes the construction of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

Claim.

\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}\models q
iff
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\models q^{\prime}
.

“
\Leftarrow
”.
Let
\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}\not\models q
.
Then, there is a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}
such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
.
W.l.o.g. assume that
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\cap({\sf var}(q)\cup\{t\})=\emptyset
. We construct an interpretation
\mathcal{J}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\cup{\sf var}(q)\cup\{t\}

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq A^{\mathcal{I}}\cup\{x\mid A(x)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle r^{\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq r^{\mathcal{I}}\cup\{(x,y)\mid r(x,y)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle X^{\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle M^{\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{A\in\Sigma}A^{\mathcal{J}}\cup{\sf var}(q)\cup\{t\}

for all concept names
A\notin\{X,M\}
and role names
r
.
From
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{K}
and the construction of
X^{\mathcal{J}}
, it easily follows that
\mathcal{J}\models\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
.
To show
\mathcal{J}\models{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
, we are left to prove that
\mathcal{J}
is minimal.

Assume to the contrary that there is a model
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
of

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{J}
.
As
M
is the only minimized concept name, there must be some
a\in M^{\mathcal{J}}
with
a\notin M^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
.
We must have
a\notin{\sf var}(q)\cup\{t\}
since
M(x)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
for
x\in{\sf var}(q)\cup\{t\}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is
a model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. But then, the definition of
M^{\mathcal{J}}

implies

a\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
with
A\in\Sigma
. By definition of
A^{\mathcal{J}}
, in turn,

a\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
or
A(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. In the former case,
it follows from the fact that
\mathcal{I}
respects

\Sigma
that
A(a)\in\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, and thus

A(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
in both cases. Now,
CI (
1
) and
a\notin M^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
imply
that

\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is not a model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
, a contradiction.

We are left to show that
\mathcal{J}\not\models q^{\prime}
.
Assume to the contrary that there is a homomorphism
h
from
\mathcal{D}_{q^{\prime}}
to
\mathcal{J}
.
We derive a contradiction to
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
by showing
that
h
is also a homomorphism from
\mathcal{D}_{q}
to
\mathcal{I}
.
First, note that
h
is a function from
{\sf var}(q)
to

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
since
{\sf var}(q^{\prime})={\sf var}(q)
and for each

x\in{\sf var}(q^{\prime})
, we have
X(x)\in q^{\prime}
and thus
h(x)\in X^{\mathcal{J}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Finally, observe that
\mathcal{I}
is identical to the restriction of

\mathcal{J}
to
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and the concept and role names that occur
in
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}
.

“
\Rightarrow
”.
Assume that
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\not\models q^{\prime}
. Then there is a
model
\mathcal{I}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
with
\mathcal{I}\not\models q^{\prime}
.
We show that w.l.o.g, we may assume that
\mathcal{I}
satisfies
several additional conditions that shall prove to be
convenient
in what follows.

Claim 2
.

There is a model
\mathcal{I}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
with
\mathcal{I}\not\models q^{\prime}
that satisfies the following properties.

(a)

a\in A^{\mathcal{I}}

A(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

for all

,

(b)

(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}}

(c)

We may construct the desired

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\setminus\{a\in{\sf var}(q)\cup\{t\}\mid A(a)\notin\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle u^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(x,y)\mid u(x,y)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\begin{split}r^{\mathcal{I}}&\coloneqq r^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\setminus\{(x,y)\mid r(x,y)\notin\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\text{ and }\\
&\phantom{\coloneqq r^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\setminus\{(x,y)\mid\;\,}\{x,y\}\cap({\sf var}(q)\cup\{t\})\neq\emptyset\}\end{split}

for all concept names
A
and role names

.
Obviously,
\mathcal{I}\not\models q^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and

to

hold by construction.
To show that

, note the following:

•

\mathcal{I}

) by construction and the fact that

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}

•

\mathcal{I}

Hence

Consequently, it is easy to see that for all CIs

•

We are left to show that
\mathcal{I}

is minimal and
\mathcal{I}\leq_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.

This finishes the proof of Claim
2
.

We aim to show that the interpretation
\mathcal{J}
, obtained as the

\mathcal{I}
to domain
{X^{\mathcal{I}}}
, is a model of

\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}
with
\mathcal{J}\not\models q
.
We assume that the properties from

are satisfied, and Property (a) yields

Assertion (

) yields

{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
and since

Since

to

\mathcal{I}

)–(

),

\mathcal{T}
as well.

To see that
\mathcal{J}\not\models q
, assume to the contrary that there is a homomorphism

from

to
\mathcal{J}
.
As
\mathcal{J}
is the restriction of
\mathcal{I}
to

to
\mathcal{I}

.

We are left to argue that
\mathcal{J}
respects
\Sigma
.
Assume to the contrary that there is some
A\in\Sigma
and
d_{0}\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
with
A(d_{0})\notin\mathcal{A}
.
We have
d_{0}\in X^{\mathcal{I}}
by construction of
\mathcal{J}
.
First assume that
d_{0}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
. Then (
2
),
(

), (

), and (

) activate the

in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

to

, contradicting

.

The more laborious case is

. As we show

of

Intuitively, in the construction of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}

with

by making true at

at

, making false all concept names at

. This forces us to make

true at

, and

) to

in

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. But then the CIs (

)–(

) apply to that

. In detail:

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(A^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\{d_{0}\})\cup\{t\mid d_{0}\in A^{\mathcal{I}}\}\cup{\sf var}(q)

\displaystyle L^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\begin{split}r^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&\coloneqq(r^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\{(d,e)\mid d_{0}\in\{d,e\}\})\\
&\phantom{\coloneqq}\cup\{(e,t)\mid(e,d_{0})\in r^{\mathcal{I}}\text{ and }e\neq d_{0}\}\\
&\phantom{\coloneqq}\cup\{(t,e)\mid(d_{0},e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}}\text{ and }e\neq d_{0}\}\\
&\phantom{\coloneqq}\cup\{(t,t)\mid(d_{0},d_{0})\in r^{\mathcal{I}}\}\\
&\phantom{\coloneqq}\cup({\sf var}(q)\times{\sf var}(q))\end{split}

for all concept names
A\neq L
and role names
r
.

Because
\mathcal{I}
satisfies

) and (

) and

cannot be from

, we have

by construction of

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Thus
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
, as desired. It remains to

is a model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
.
We have

because

and

\mathcal{I}
and of

to

To see that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
satisfies the CIs (
1
), let

with

.
If

) and (

),

.
Otherwise, we have

, yielding

via

1
).
We must have

and thus

by

of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
obviously satisfies CIs (

) and (

) by

L^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.

For CIs (
9
), consider some
X\sqsubseteq A\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
and let

.
We know that

as

by construction.
If

If

, note that Point
(a)
of Claim (

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}

\displaystyle t\in A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\text{ iff }d_{0}\in A^{\mathcal{I}}

(†)

for all concept names

.
Thus,
d_{0}\in X^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Together with the fact that
\mathcal{I}
as a model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
must satisfy

, we have that
d_{0}\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Ergo,

follows via (
†
‣
B
).
Similarly, if

, then

holds by construction, and as

satisfies

, we have
x\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
.
This yields

by construction.

For CIs (
10
), consider some
X\sqcap A\sqsubseteq\exists r.(X\sqcap B)\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
and let

.
Again,
x\neq d_{0}
.
If
x\in{\sf var}(q)
,

follows directly as
(x,x)\in r^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
and
x\in(X\sqcap B)^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
hold by construction.
If
x=t
, Property (
†
‣
B
) implies
d_{0}\in(X\sqcap A)^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Together with the fact that
\mathcal{I}
as a model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
must satisfy

, there must be some

Observe that Point

\displaystyle\begin{cases}t&\text{if }y=d_{0}\\
y&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

for all role names

We thus have

.

We omit the details for the cases of CIs (

) and (
