Appendix C
Proofs for Section
3.3

In the main part of the paper, we have defined neighborhoods only for
the unraveling
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
of the interpretation
\mathcal{I}
. In the proofs, we
also consider neighborhods in the quotient

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}/{\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}}}
. We thus start with
a more general definition of neighborhoods.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be an interpretation and
\Delta\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.
For
n\geq 0
and
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\Delta
, we use

\mathcal{N}_{n}^{{\mathcal{I}},\Delta}(d)
to denote the
n
-neighborhood of

d
in
\mathcal{I}
up to

\Delta
, that is, the set of all elements

e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\Delta
such that the undirected
graph
G_{\mathcal{I}}
associated with
\mathcal{I}
contains a path

d_{0},\dots,d_{k}
with
0\leq k\leq n
,
d_{0}=d
,

d_{0},\dots,d_{k-1}\notin\Delta
, and
d_{k}=e
.

So the neighborhoods
\mathcal{N}_{n}(d)
defined in the main body of
the paper are now called
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}},\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}(d)
.

See
7

We prove that
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}/{\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}}}

is indeed a model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
with
\mathcal{J}\not\models q(\bar{a})
.
The key to proving the latter is to exhibit suitable local homomorphisms from
\mathcal{J}
back to
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.
The existence of a homomorphism
h
from
a CQ

p(\bar{x})
in
q
to
\mathcal{J}
with

h(\bar{x})=a
would then contradict
the fact that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is
a countermodel of
q
.
Indeed, such a homomorphism
h
would map each connected component
C
of
p
into a
{\left|{q}\right|}
-neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{{\left|{q}\right|}}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}({\overline{c}})
, for some
c\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
and where
{\overline{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}
stands for the set
\{{\overline{e}}\mid e\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}\}
.
By exhibiting a homomorphism
\rho_{c}:\mathcal{N}_{{\left|{q}\right|}}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{{\left|{q}\right|}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime},{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}({c})
such that
\rho_{c}^{-1}({\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}})\subseteq{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
, we can find a homomorphism from
C
to
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.
Taking the union of homomorphisms for
all of
p
’s connected components
gives a homomorphism from the full
p
to
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.

Except for the use of Lemma
2
, concerned with circumscription, we roughly follow
[
\citeauthoryearManière2022
]
, with slight simplifications as we are not considering negative role inclusions.
For all
n\geq 0
, all
c\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, and all
d\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}(c)\setminus\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, we denote
w_{n,c}^{d}
the (unique) word such that
d={p_{n,c}w_{n,c}^{d}}
.
Let us first formulate two remarks concerning the constructed interpretation
\mathcal{J}
.

Remark 2
.

Combining Conditions 1 and 2.(a) from the definition of
\sim_{n}
, gives: if
d_{1}\sim_{n}d_{2}
, then
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}({d_{1}})=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}({d_{2}})
.

Remark 3
.

If
d_{1}\sim_{n}d_{2}
,
then
d_{1}\sim_{m}d_{2}
for any
m\leq n
.

We now define homomorphisms
\rho_{c}
, mentioned in the proof sketch, inductively on
n
increasing from
0
to
{\left|{q}\right|}
.
Starting from the element
{\overline{c}}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
, we can naturally carry it back as
\rho_{c}({\overline{c}})=c\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime},{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}({c})
.
Assume now that we have defined
\rho_{c}({\overline{d}})
for some
{\overline{d}}\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
and that we are moving further to an element
{\overline{e}}\in\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
along an edge
({\overline{d}},{\overline{e}})
in
\mathcal{J}
.
In the case of
{\overline{e}}\notin{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
, the following lemma produces an element
e^{\prime}
which is to
\rho_{c}({\overline{d}})
(that is
d^{\prime}
in the below statement), what
{\overline{e}}
is to
{\overline{d}}
.
We will thus set
\rho_{c}({\overline{e}})
to
e^{\prime}
.
The case disjunction that arises reflects
e^{\prime}
being either a successor of
d^{\prime}
in the corresponding tree-shaped structure of the unraveling
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
(which is identified in the quotient
\mathcal{J}
as
{\left|{e}\right|}\equiv{\left|{d}\right|}+1\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
), or the converse (identified as
{\left|{d}\right|}\equiv{\left|{e}\right|}+1\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
).

Lemma 18
.

Given two elements
{\overline{d}},{\overline{e}}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
, if there exists a role
{{p}}
such that
({\overline{d}},{\overline{e}})\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, then there exists a unique element
rB\in\Omega
such that one of the two following conditions is satisfied:

r
+
.

{\left|{e}\right|}\equiv{\left|{d}\right|}+1\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
,

w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1},e}^{e}=w_{{\left|{q}\right|},d}^{d}rB
and
\mathcal{T}\models{r}\sqsubseteq{p}
.
Furthermore, for all
d^{\prime}\sim_{n}d
, the element
e^{\prime}=d^{\prime}rB
belongs to
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
and satisfies
e^{\prime}\sim_{n-1}e
.

r
-
.

{\left|{d}\right|}\equiv{\left|{e}\right|}+1\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
,

w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1},d}^{d}=w_{{\left|{q}\right|},e}^{e}rB

and
\mathcal{T}\models{r^{-}}\sqsubseteq{p}
.
Furthermore, for all
d^{\prime}\sim_{n}d
, we have
e^{\prime}
such that
d^{\prime}=e^{\prime}rB
and the prefix
e^{\prime}
satisfies
e^{\prime}\sim_{n-1}e
.

Proof.

Notice the two conditions are mutually exclusive:
{\left|{e}\right|}\equiv{\left|{d}\right|}+1\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
and
{\left|{d}\right|}\equiv{\left|{e}\right|}+1\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
would imply
0\equiv 2\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
, which is impossible as
2{\left|{q}\right|}+3>2
. Furthermore, in each case
{{r}}{{B}}
is defined as the last letter of the word
w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1},e}^{e}
(resp
w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1},d}^{d}
), which is unique and does not depends on the choice of
e
(resp
d
) nor on
{{p}}
.
This proves the uniqueness.

We now focus on the existence and the additional property.
From the definition of
{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, there exist
({d_{0}},{e_{0}})\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
such that
{\overline{d_{0}}}={\overline{d}}
and
{\overline{e_{0}}}={\overline{e}}
. Recall
{\overline{d}},{\overline{e}}\notin{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
, hence
d_{0},e_{0}\notin{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}
.
Therefore the definition of
{{p}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, yields two cases:

•

We have
e_{0}=d_{0}{{r}}{{B}}
with
\mathcal{T}\models{r}\sqsubseteq{p}
.
It follows that
{\left|{e_{0}}\right|}={\left|{d_{0}}\right|}+1\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3

and
w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1},e_{0}}^{e_{0}}=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-1,d_{0}}^{d_{0}}{{r}}{{B}}
, immediately yielding the same properties for
d
and
e
as
({\overline{d_{0}}},{\overline{e_{0}}})=({\overline{d}},{\overline{e}})
.

Consider now
d^{\prime}
and
1\leq n\leq{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}
s.t. now
d^{\prime}\sim_{n}d
.
Transitivity gives
d^{\prime}\sim_{n}d_{0}
, and we have in particular
w_{n,d^{\prime}}^{d^{\prime}}=w_{n,d_{0}}^{d_{0}}
.
Recall that
e_{0}=d_{0}{{r}}{{B}}
, hence Condition 2 from the definition of
d^{\prime}\sim_{n}d_{0}
ensures
d^{\prime}{{r}}{{B}}
belongs to
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.
Notice it is now sufficient to prove
d^{\prime}{{r}}{{B}}\sim_{n-1}e_{0}
: that is because
{\overline{e}}={\overline{e_{0}}}
, hence transitivity will conclude the proof.
It should be clear that
w_{n-1,d^{\prime}{{r}}{{B}}}^{d^{\prime}{{r}}{{B}}}=w_{n-1,e_{0}}^{e_{0}}
and
{\left|{d^{\prime}{{r}}{{B}}}\right|}\equiv{\left|{e_{0}}\right|}\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
, hence Conditions 1 and 3 from the definition of
\sim_{n-1}
are satisfied.
To verify Condition 2, it suffices to remark that
e_{0}=d_{0}{{r}}{{B}}
ensures that the characterization via paths of the
n
-neighborhood of
d_{0}
fully determines the characterization of the
(n-1)
neighborhood of
e_{0}
.
But since the former coincides with the characterization of the
n
-neighborhood of
d^{\prime}
(using Condition 2 from the definition of
{\overline{d_{0}}}={\overline{d^{\prime}}}
), that fully decides the characterization of the
(n-1)
-neighborhood of
e^{\prime}
, we are done.

•

We have
d_{0}=e_{0}{{r}}{{B}}
with
\mathcal{T}\models{r^{-}}\sqsubseteq{p}
.
It follows that
{\left|{d_{0}}\right|}\equiv{\left|{e_{0}}\right|}+1\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3

and
w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1},d_{0}}^{d_{0}}=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-1,e_{0}}^{e_{0}}{{r}}{{B}}
, immediately yielding the same properties for
d
and
e
as
({\overline{d_{0}}},{\overline{e_{0}}})=({\overline{d}},{\overline{e}})
.

Let now
1\leq n\leq{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}+1
be an integer and
d^{\prime}\sim_{n}d
.
Transitivity gives
d^{\prime}\sim_{n}d_{0}
, and we have in particular
w_{1,d^{\prime}}^{d^{\prime}}=w_{1,d_{0}}^{d_{0}}={{r}}{{B}}
(it is here important to have
n\geq 1
!).
That is
d^{\prime}
ends by
{{r}}{{B}}
, and therefore we can indeed have prefix
e^{\prime}
such that
d^{\prime}=e^{\prime}{{r}}{{B}}
. ∎

Notice the “strength” of the equivalence relation
\sim_{n}
between
{\overline{e}}
and
\rho_{c}({\overline{e}})
decreases as we move further in the neighbourhood of
{\overline{c}}
.
However, since we start from
\rho_{c}({\overline{c}}):=c\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}}c
and explore a
{\left|{q}\right|}
-neighbourhood, the index remains at least
1
.
This is essential as
\sim_{1}
notably encodes relations to elements of
{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
as required by Condition 2.(b) from the definition of
\sim_{1}
.

It remains to abstract from
the particular choice of
{\overline{d}}
, which is likely not to be the only element of
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
connected to
{\overline{e}}
.
Taking a closer look at Lemma
18
, we observe that
\rho_{c}({\overline{e}})
, that is
e^{\prime}
, is obtained either by adding a letter to
\rho_{c}({\overline{d}})
, that is
d^{\prime}
, or by removing the last letter of
\rho_{c}({\overline{d}})
, and that these letters coincide with those in the suffixes of elements
{d}
and
{e}
.
Therefore, when moving from
{\overline{c}}
to
{\overline{e}}
, each added letter appears already in the suffix
w_{{\left|{q}\right|}+1,e}^{e}
of
{e}
and, similarly, each removed letter appeared in the suffix
w_{{\left|{q}\right|}+1,c}^{c}
of
c
.
This is however not completely true as some steps along the way from
{\overline{c}}
to
{\overline{e}}
may cancel each other,
e.g.
a letter is introduced at one step and removed on the next one.

The challenge is therefore to decide the numbers
l^{+}
of
actual
additions and
l^{-}
of
actual
removals to build
\rho_{c}({\overline{e}})
directly from
c
and
{\overline{e}}
(which determines
w_{{\left|{q}\right|}+1,e}^{e}
).
We then drop the
l^{-}
last letters from
c
, which intuitively corresponds to keeping its prefix
p_{l^{-},c}
, and keep the
l^{+}
last letters from
w_{{\left|{q}\right|}+1,e}^{e}
, which intuitively corresponds to
w_{l^{+},e}^{e}
, and concatenate those two words.
To this end, the next definition captures the relative difference of letters between
{\overline{c}}
and
{\overline{e}}
, encoded in
{\left|{c}\right|}
and
{\left|{e}\right|}\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
.

Definition 5
.

Let
{\overline{c}}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
and
n\leq{\left|{q}\right|}
. The
relative depth
of
{\overline{e}}\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
from
{\overline{c}}
is the integer
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})\in[-n,n]
such that
{\left|{e}\right|}\equiv{\left|{c}\right|}+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
.

Remark 4
.

By induction on
n\leq{\left|{q}\right|}
, it is straightforward to see that
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})
is well defined. Uniqueness is ensured by
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})\leq n\leq{\left|{q}\right|}
.
A consequence of Lemma
18
is that for the smallest
n\leq{\left|{q}\right|}
such that
{\overline{e}}\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
we have
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})=n\mod 2
.

We can now identify how many additions and removals canceled each other.
Indeed, if it takes exactly
n
steps to reach
{\overline{e}}
from
{\overline{c}}
, with relative difference of
\delta:=\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})
, then
n-{\left|{\delta}\right|}
is the number of steps that canceled each other, hence:

\frac{n-{\left|{\delta}\right|}}{2}
canceled additions and
\frac{n-{\left|{\delta}\right|}}{2}
canceled removals.
Therefore, the number
l^{+}
of
actual
additions is
\frac{n-{\left|{\delta}\right|}}{2}+\delta
if
\delta\geq 0
, or
\frac{n-{\left|{\delta}\right|}}{2}
if
\delta\leq 0
, that is in both cases
\frac{n+\delta}{2}
.
Similarly we obtain
\frac{n-\delta}{2}
for the number
l^{-}
of
actual
removals.
Notice Remark
4
ensures both
l^{+}
and
l^{-}
are integers.

The next theorem formalizes the above intuitions.
Notably, in the non-trivial cases,
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})
is obtained by
removing the
\frac{n-\delta}{2}
last letters of
{c}
and keeping the
\frac{n+\delta}{2}
last letters from the suffix of
{e}
, which results in the word
p_{\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},c}w_{\frac{n+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},e}^{e}
.
It is then a technicality to verify these syntactical operations on words produces an element in the domain of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and that the defined
\rho_{n,c}
is a homomorphism as desired.

Lemma 19
.

For all
c\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
and all
n\leq{\left|{q}\right|}
, the following mapping
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}l}\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})&\rightarrow&\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime},{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}(c)\\
{\overline{e}}&\mapsto&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{e}})\qquad\text{if }{\overline{e}}\in\mathcal{N}_{n-1}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})\\
e\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{if }{\overline{e}}\in{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}\\
p_{\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},c}w_{\frac{n+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},e}^{e}\quad\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

is a homomorphism satisfying
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{{e}}})\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-n}{e}
and
\rho_{n,c}^{-1}({\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}})\subseteq{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
.

Proof.

Let
c\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.
We proceed by induction on
n\leq{\left|{q}\right|}
and prove along a technical statement.
Property
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-n}{e}
already ensures
w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-n,\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})}^{\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})}=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-n,e}^{e}
; we reinforce this latter fact as follows.
If
e\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})\setminus\mathcal{N}_{n-1}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
, then:

w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})}^{\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})}=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},e}^{e}

(
*
)

It is indeed a stronger statement since
-n\leq\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})\leq n
leads to
0\leq\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2}\leq n
, hence
{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-n\leq{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2}
. Property

∗

‣
C
therefore provides a more precise information about the suffix of
\rho_{n,c}{e}
.

Base case:
n=0
.

Let
{\overline{e}}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
, hence
{\overline{e}}={\overline{c}}
. If
{\overline{c}}\in{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
, then
\rho_{0,c}{e}=e=c
. Otherwise we have
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})=0
, hence
\rho_{0,c}{e}=p_{0,c}w_{0,c}^{c}=c
. In both cases
\rho_{0,c}{e}=c
, and it is straightforward that all the desired properties hold. In particular, agreeing that
\mathcal{N}_{-1}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
can reasonably be set to
\emptyset
, our technical statement holds.

Induction case.

Assume the statement holds for
0\leq n-1<{\left|{q}\right|}
.
Let
{\overline{e}}\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
.
If
{\overline{e}}\in\mathcal{N}_{n-1}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
, then the induction hypothesis applies directly on
{\overline{e}}
and provides (stronger versions of) the desired properties.
Otherwise, we have by definition of neighbourhoods an element
{\overline{d}}\in\mathcal{N}_{n-1}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
, not belonging to
{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
nor to
\mathcal{N}_{n-2}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
, and a role
{{p}}\in\mathsf{N_{R}}^{\pm}
such that
({\overline{d}},{\overline{e}})\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
We apply the induction hypothesis on
{\overline{d}}
, which gives
\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})=p_{\frac{n-1-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2},d}w_{\frac{n-1+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2},d}^{d}
since
{\overline{d}}\notin{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
.
We further distinguish between
{\overline{e}}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}
and
{\overline{e}}\notin{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}
, the latter subcase yielding two subcases by applying Lemma
18
and distinguishing between Cases r
+
and r
-
.
We have therefore three cases to treat.

{\overline{e}}\in{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
.

We have
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})=e
and the only non-trivial property to prove is that
e\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime},{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}(c)
.
Recall the induction hypothesis ensures in particular
\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})\sim_{1}d
.
Condition 2.(b) from the definition of
\sim_{1}
ensures
(\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}}),e)\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, which provides the desired property.

r
+
.

Case r
+
ensures
{\left|{e}\right|}={\left|{d}\right|}+1\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
, hence
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})=\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})+1
, and
w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1},e}^{e}=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-1,d}^{d}{{r}}{{B}}
.
Therefore, our element
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})
of interest simplifies as:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}l}\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})&=&p_{\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},c}w_{\frac{n+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},e}^{e}\\
&=&p_{\frac{n-(\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})+1)}{2},c}w_{\frac{n+(\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})+1)}{2},e}^{e}\\
&=&p_{\frac{(n-1)-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2},c}w_{\frac{(n-1)+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2}+1,e}^{e}\\
&=&p_{\frac{(n-1)-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2},c}w_{\frac{(n-1)+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2},d}^{d}{{r}}{{B}}\\
&=&\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}}){{r}}{{B}},\end{array}

which is well-defined and satisfies
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-n}e
from Lemma
18
.
Recalling that the induction hypothesis gives
\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})\in\mathcal{N}_{n-1}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime},{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}(c)
, it follows that
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime},{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}(c)
.
Furthermore, notice that
{\overline{e}}
and
{\overline{d}}
satisfy all conditions of our additional statement.
Since in Case r
+
we have
\mathcal{T}\models{r}\sqsubseteq{p}
, reusing

\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})=\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}}){{r}}{{B}}
immediately yields
(\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}}),\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}}))\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

Checking that Property

∗

‣
C
holds is now a technicality, and recall that since
d\in\mathcal{N}_{n-1}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})\setminus\mathcal{N}_{n-2}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
, we can apply it to
d
by induction hypothesis. We hence have:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace*{-50pt}}l}w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})}^{\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})}\hskip-50.\\
\hskip-50.&=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2}-1,\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}^{\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}{{r}}{{B}}\\
\hskip-50.&=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{(n-1)+1-(\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})+1)}{2}-1,\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}^{\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}{{r}}{{B}}\\
\hskip-50.&=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{(n-1)-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2}-1,\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}^{\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}{{r}}{{B}}\\
\hskip-50.&=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{(n-1)-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2}-1,d}^{d}{{r}}{{B}}\\
\hskip-50.&=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},e}^{e}.\end{array}

r
-
.

Case r
-
ensures
{\left|{e}\right|}={\left|{d}\right|}-1\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
, hence
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})=\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})-1
, and
w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1},d}^{d}=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-1,e}^{e}{{r}}{{B}}
.
By induction hypothesis, element
\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})=p_{\frac{(n-1)-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2},d}w_{\frac{(n-1)+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2},d}^{d}
is well-defined.
Notice Property

∗

‣
C
on
d
(which, again can be applied as
d\in\mathcal{N}_{n-1}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})\setminus\mathcal{N}_{n-2}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
) gives more precise information on the suffix of
\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})
than the definition of
\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})
, because
n\leq{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}
leads to
\frac{(n-1)+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2}+1\leq{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{(n-1)-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2}
.
Therefore,
w_{\frac{(n-1)+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2}+1,d}^{d}
is itself a suffix of
w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{(n-1)-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2},d}^{d}
, which equals
w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{(n-1)-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2},\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}^{\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}
.
Hence we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}l}\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})&=&p_{\frac{(n-1)-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2}+1,d}w_{\frac{(n-1)+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2}+1,d}^{d}\\
&=&p_{\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},d}w_{\frac{n+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2}+1,d}^{d}\\
&=&p_{\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},d}w_{\frac{n+\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},e}^{e}{{r}}{{B}}\\
&=&\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}}){{r}}{{B}}\end{array}

Lemma
18
now ensures
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})\sim_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-n}e
.
Recalling that the induction hypothesis gives
\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})\in\mathcal{N}_{n-1}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime},{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}(c)
, it follows that
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime},{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}(c)
.
Furthermore, notice that
{\overline{e}}
and
{\overline{d}}
satisfy all conditions of our additional statement. Since in Case r
-
we have
\mathcal{T}\models{r^{-}}\sqsubseteq{p}
, reusing

\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})=\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}}){{r}}{{B}}
immediately yields
(\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}}),\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}}))\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

Again, we check Property

∗

‣
C
holds:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace*{-50pt}}l}w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})}^{\rho_{n,c}({\overline{e}})}{{r}}{{B}}\hskip-50.\\
\hskip-50.&=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2}+1,\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}^{\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}\\
\hskip-50.&=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{(n-1)+1-(\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})-1)}{2}+1,\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}^{\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}\\
\hskip-50.&=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{(n-1)-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2},\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}^{\rho_{n-1,c}({\overline{d}})}\\
\hskip-50.&=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{(n-1)-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{d}})}{2},d}^{d}\\
\hskip-50.&=w_{{{\left|{q}\right|}+1}-\frac{n-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{e}})}{2},e}^{e}{{r}}{{B}}\end{array}

We now verify that
\rho_{n,c}
is a homomorphism.

•

Let
{\overline{u}}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}\cap\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
. By definition of
{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, we have
e\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. Since
n\leq{\left|{q}\right|}
we have
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{u}})\sim_{1}e
, hence applying Remark
2
we obtain
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{u}})\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

•

Let
({\overline{u}},{\overline{v}})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}\cap(\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})\times\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}}))
.
If
{\overline{u}}\in{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
or
{\overline{v}}\in{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
, then Condition 2.(b) from the definition of
\sim_{1}
applies on
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{u}})
or on
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{v}})
(recall
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{u}})\sim_{1}u
and
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{v}})\sim_{1}v
) and gives
(\rho_{n,c}({\overline{u}}),\rho_{n,c}({\overline{v}}))\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Otherwise
{\overline{u}}\notin{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
and
{\overline{v}}\notin{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
.
Let
n_{1},n_{2}
be the minimum integers such that
{\overline{u}}\in\mathcal{N}_{n_{1}}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
and
{\overline{v}}\in\mathcal{N}_{n_{2}}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}({\overline{c}})
.
Since
({\overline{u}},{\overline{v}})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, we have
n_{1}-n_{2}\in\{-1,0,1\}
.
Definitions of
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{u}})
and
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{v}})
lead to
{\left|{u}\right|}-{\left|{v}\right|}\equiv\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{u}})-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{v}})\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
.
Lemma
18
gives
{\left|{u}\right|}\equiv{\left|{v}\right|}\pm 1\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
.
Recall
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{u}}),\delta_{{\overline{d}}}({\overline{v}})\in[-{\left|{q}\right|},{\left|{q}\right|}]
, hence
-2{\left|{q}\right|}-1\leq\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{u}})-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{u}})\mp 1\leq 2{\left|{q}\right|}+1
.
Since
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{u}})-\delta_{{\overline{d}}}({\overline{v}})\mp 1\equiv 0\mod 2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
and
2{\left|{q}\right|}+1<2{\left|{q}\right|}+3
, we must have
\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{u}})-\delta_{{\overline{c}}}({\overline{v}})=\pm 1
.
Joint to Remark
4
, it excludes the case
n_{1}-n_{2}=0
.
We are hence left with
n_{1}=n_{2}\pm 1
.
Applying our additional property with
k:=\max(n_{1},n_{2})
gives
(\rho_{n,c}({\overline{u}}),\rho_{n,c}({\overline{v}}))\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

Finally,
\rho_{n,c}^{-1}({{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}})\subseteq{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
is a straightforward consequence of
\rho_{n,c}({\overline{u}})\sim_{1}{u}
(and again, recall elements from
{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}
are alone in their equivalent class!).
∎

Let us now complete the proof of Lemma
7
with Lemma
19
in hand.

Proof of Lemma
7
.

We first prove that
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
by considering each possible shape of assertions and axioms:

•

{{A}}(a)
.

Since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model, we have
a\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. Therefore, the definition of
{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
gives
{\overline{a}}=a\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

{{p}}(a,b)
.

Since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model, we have
(a,b)\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. Therefore, the definition of
{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}}
gives
({\overline{a}},{\overline{b}})=(a,b)\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

{\top}\sqsubseteq{A}
.

Let
u\in\top^{\mathcal{J}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
. By definition of
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
, there exists
u_{0}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
such that
{\overline{u_{0}}}=u
. Since
u_{0}\in\top^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model, it ensures
u_{0}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. Therefore the definition of
{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
gives
u={\overline{u_{0}}}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

{A_{1}\sqcap A_{2}}\sqsubseteq{A}
.

Let
u\in({{A}}_{1}\sqcap{{A}}_{2})^{\mathcal{J}}
. By definition of
{{A}}_{1}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
{{A}}_{2}^{\mathcal{J}}
, there exists
u_{1}\in{{A}}_{1}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
and
u_{2}\in{{A}}_{2}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
with
{\overline{u_{1}}}={\overline{u_{2}}}=u
.
Remark
2
ensures
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
satisfy the same concepts, that is in particular
u_{1}\in({{A}}_{1}\sqcap{{A}}_{2})^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.
Since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model, it ensures
u_{1}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, yielding by definition of
{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
that
u={\overline{u_{1}}}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

{A_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\exists{{r}}.{A_{2}}}
.

Let
u\in{{A}}_{1}^{\mathcal{J}}
. By definition of
{{A_{1}}}^{\mathcal{J}}
there exists
u_{0}\in{{A_{1}}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
with
{\overline{u_{0}}}=u
.
Since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model, it ensures there exists
v_{0}\in{{A_{2}}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
with
(u_{0},v_{0})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. By definition of
{{A_{2}}}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, the element
v:={\overline{v_{0}}}
satisfies both
v\in{{A_{2}}}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
(u,v)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, that is
u\in(\exists{{r}}.{A_{2}})^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

{\exists{{r}}.{A_{1}}}\sqsubseteq{A_{2}}
.

Let
u\in(\exists{{r}}.{A_{1}})^{\mathcal{J}}
, that is, there exists
v\in{{A}}_{1}^{\mathcal{J}}
with
(u,v)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
By definition of
{{A}}_{1}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, there exist
(u_{0},v_{0})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
and
v_{1}\in{{A}}_{1}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
such that
{\overline{u_{0}}}=u
and
{\overline{v_{0}}}={\overline{v_{1}}}=v
.
Remark
2
ensures
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
satisfy the same concepts, in particular
u_{0}\in(\exists{{r}}.{A_{1}})^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.
Since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model, this ensures
u_{0}\in{{A}}_{2}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, yielding by definition of
{{A}}_{2}^{\mathcal{J}}
that
u={\overline{u_{0}}}\in{{A}}_{2}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

{{A}}\sqsubseteq{{\lnot B}}
.

By contradiction, assume
u\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}\cap{{B}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
By definition there exists
v\in{{A}}^{{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}}
and
w\in{{B}}^{{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}}
with
{\overline{v}}={\overline{w}}=u
.
Remark
2
ensures
v
and
w
satisfy the same concepts, contradicting
{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}
being a model.

•

{{\lnot B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
.

Let
u\in{{\lnot B}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
By definition of
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
, there exists
v\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}
such that
{\overline{v}}=u
.
Since
u\notin{{B}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, we have
{v}\notin{{B}}^{{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}}
.
Hence
{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}
being a model gives
{v}\in{{A}}^{{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}}
, yielding by definition
u={\overline{v}}\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

{p}\sqsubseteq{r}
.

Let
(u,v)\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
By definition of
{{p}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, there exists
(u_{0},v_{0})\in{{p}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
such that
{\overline{u_{0}}}=u
and
{\overline{v_{0}}}=v
.
Since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model, it ensures
(u_{0},v_{0})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, hence
({\overline{u_{0}}},{\overline{v_{0}}})=(u,v)\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
by definition of
{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

It remains to prove that
\mathcal{J}
is minimal w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
.
We use Lemma
2
with
\mathcal{I}
as reference model, and it
suffices to show that the preconditions of that lemma are
satisfied. This is in fact
easy based on
Remark
2
,
following
exactly the same lines as the
final part of the proof of Lemma
3
.

We now prove
\mathcal{J}\not\models q(\bar{a})
.
By contradiction, assume we have
p\in q
and a homomorphism
\pi:p\rightarrow\mathcal{J}
s.t.
p(\bar{x})=\bar{a}
.
Consider the set of variables
{\mathbf{v}}_{\pi}:=\{v\mid v\in{\bar{y}},\pi(v)\notin{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}\}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
denote the set of connected components of
{\mathbf{v}}_{\pi}
in
p_{\mid{\mathbf{v}}_{\pi}}
(that is the query obtained by keeping only those atoms containing variables from
{\mathbf{v}}_{\pi}
).
For each connected component
C\in\mathcal{C}
, choose a reference variable
v_{C}\in C
.
Since
\pi
is a homomorphism and
{\left|{C}\right|}\leq{\left|{q}\right|}
, every variable
v\in C
satisfies
\pi(v)\in\mathcal{N}_{{\left|{q}\right|}}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}(\pi(v_{C}))
. Let
d_{C}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
denote one’s favorite representative for the class of
\pi(v_{C})
(that is
{\overline{d_{C}}}=\pi(v_{C})
).
From Lemma
19
, we have a homomorphism
\rho_{C}:\mathcal{N}_{{\left|{q}\right|}}^{\mathcal{J},{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}}(\pi(v_{C}))\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{{\left|{q}\right|}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime},{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}(d_{C})
.
Using these
\rho_{C}
, one per
C\in\mathcal{C}
, we define:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\pi^{\prime}:{\bar{x}}\cup{\bar{y}}&\rightarrow&\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\\
v&\mapsto&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\rho_{C}(\pi(v))&\text{ if }v\in C,C\in\mathcal{C}\\
e&\text{ if }\pi(v)={\overline{e}}\in{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

Since each
\rho_{C}
is a homomorphism (again Lemma
19
), we can check the overall
\pi^{\prime}
is also a homomorphism:

•

Consider
{{A}}(v)\in q
. If
v\in C
for some
C\in\mathcal{C}
, then
\rho_{C}
being a homomorphism gives
\pi^{\prime}(v)\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. Otherwise
\pi(v)={\overline{e}}\in{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
, but since
\pi
is a homomorphism we have
\pi(v)\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
. Since
{\overline{e}}=\{e\}
and by definition of
{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, it ensures
e\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, that is
\pi^{\prime}(v)\in{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

•

Consider
{{r}}(u,v)\in q
.

–

If both
\pi(u),\pi(v)\notin{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
, then we can find
C\in\mathcal{C}
such that
u,v\in C
, and then we use
\rho_{C}
being a homomorphism.

–

If both
\pi(u),\pi(v)\in{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
, then the definition of
{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
provides
(u_{0},v_{0})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
with
{\overline{u_{0}}}=\pi(u)\in{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
and
{\overline{v_{0}}}=\pi(v)\in{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
. Hence
{\overline{u_{0}}}=\{u_{0}\}
and
{\overline{v_{0}}}=\{v_{0}\}
, which gives
(\pi^{\prime}(u),\pi^{\prime}(v))\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

–

If
\pi(u)\notin{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
and
\pi(v)\in{\overline{{\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}}}
, then we have
\pi^{\prime}(u)=\rho_{C}(\pi(u))
for some
C\in\mathcal{C}
. Lemma
19
ensures
\pi^{\prime}(u)\sim_{1}\pi(u)
, and since
\pi
is a homomorphism, we also have
(\pi(u),\pi(v))\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}
. Therefore we can apply Condition 2.(b) and we obtain
(\pi^{\prime}(u),\pi^{\prime}(v))\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
.

In particular,
\pi^{\prime}
is a homomorphism s.t.
\pi^{\prime}(\bar{x})=\bar{a}
, hence the desired contradiction with
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
being a countermodel.

∎

See
3

Proof.

Assume that we are given a circumscribed
\mathcal{ALCHI}
KB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
with

\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
and a UCQ
q
. We describe a
\Sigma^{p}_{2}
procedure to decide whether
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\not\models q(\bar{a})
.

We first guess an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
with
|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|\leq|\mathcal{A}|+(2^{|\mathcal{T}|+2}+1)^{3|q|}
.
We next check in polynomial time that
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
and that
\mathcal{I}
is minimal w.r.t.
<_{{\sf CP}}
by co-guessing a
model
\mathcal{J}
of
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{J}<_{{\sf CP}}\mathcal{I}
. If one of the
checks fails, we reject. Otherwise, for each CQ
p
in
q
, we verify whether there is a homomorphism from
p
to
\mathcal{I}
. This is done brute-force, in time
O(|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|^{|q|})
.
We accept if there is no such homomorphism and reject otherwise.
This procedure is correct due to Lemma
7
.
∎

See
4

Proof.

We give a polynomial time reduction from

\forall\exists{\sf 3SAT}

[
\citeauthoryearStockmeyer1976
]
, that is, the
problem to decide whether a given
\forall\exists
-3CNF
sentence is true. Thus let

\forall\bar{x}\exists\bar{y}\,\varphi
be such a sentence where

\bar{x}=x_{1}\cdots x_{m}
,
\bar{y}=y_{1}\cdots y_{n}
, and

\varphi=\bigwedge^{\ell}_{i=1}\bigvee_{j=1}^{3}L_{ij}
with

L_{ij}=v
or
L_{ij}=\lnot{v}
for some

v\in\{x_{1},\dots x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}
. We construct a
circumscribed
{\cal EL}
KB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
and an atomic query

q
such that
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models q
iff

\forall\bar{x}\exists\bar{y}\,\varphi
is true.

The circumscription pattern minimizes the concept name
{\mathsf{Min}}

and lets all other concept names vary, and the instance query
is
q={\mathsf{Goal}}(c_{0})
.
We now describe how to construct the KB
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
, not
strictly
separating
\mathcal{T}
from
\mathcal{A}
. We first represent
\varphi
in

\mathcal{A}
using the following assertions:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Var}}(v)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }v\in{\bar{x}}\cup{\bar{y}}

(18)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{pos}}_{j}(c_{i},v)

\displaystyle\quad\text{if }L_{ij}=v

(19)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{neg}}_{j}(c_{i},v)

\displaystyle\quad\text{if }L_{ij}=\lnot v

(20)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{First}}(c_{0}),{\mathsf{Last}}(c_{\ell})

(21)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{next}}(c_{i},c_{i+1})

\displaystyle\quad 0\leq i<\ell

(22)

We next require each variable to be connected via the role
name
{\mathsf{eval}}
to two instances of
{\mathsf{Min}}
, a “positive” one and a “negative” one:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Var}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,\exists{\mathsf{eval}}.({\mathsf{Min}}\sqcap{\mathsf{Pos}})

(23)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Var}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,\exists{\mathsf{eval}}.({\mathsf{Min}}\sqcap{\mathsf{Neg}}).

(24)

As potential witnesses for the above existential restrictions, we provide
several instances of
{\mathsf{Min}}
, a positive and a negative one for each truth value:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Min}}(t_{+})

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Min}}(t_{-})

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Min}}(f_{+})

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Min}}(f_{-})

(25)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{True}}(t_{+})

\displaystyle{\mathsf{True}}(t_{-})

\displaystyle{\mathsf{False}}(f_{+})

\displaystyle{\mathsf{False}}(f_{-})

(26)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Pos}}(t_{+})

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Neg}}(t_{-})

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Pos}}(f_{+})

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Neg}}(f_{-})

(27)

If such an instance is used as a witness, then the respective
variable
is assigned the corresponding truth value:

\displaystyle\exists{\mathsf{eval}}.{\mathsf{True}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{True}}

(28)

\displaystyle\exists{\mathsf{eval}}.{\mathsf{False}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{False}}

(29)

We next introduce a mechanism for “freezing” the valuation of the

{\bar{x}}
variables in the sense that this valuation must be identical in
all models that are smaller w.r.t. ‘
<_{\sf CP}
’. This reflects the fact
that the
{\bar{x}}
variables are chosen prior to the
{\bar{y}}
variables.
Freezing is achieved by the following:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{freeze}}_{\mathsf{True}}({x}_{{\mathsf{True}}},x)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }x\in{\bar{x}}

(30)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{freeze}}_{\mathsf{False}}({x}_{{\mathsf{False}}},x)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }x\in{\bar{x}}

(31)

\displaystyle\exists{\mathsf{freeze}}_{\mathsf{True}}.{\mathsf{True}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{Min}}

(32)

\displaystyle\exists{\mathsf{freeze}}_{\mathsf{False}}.{\mathsf{False}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{Min}}

(33)

Note that if a variable
x\in\bar{x}
satisfies
{\mathsf{True}}
, then

{x}_{{\mathsf{True}}}
must satisfy
{\mathsf{Min}}
, and likewise for
{\mathsf{False}}
.
Since
{\mathsf{Min}}
is minimized, any model that is smaller w.r.t. ‘
<_{\sf CP}
’ must thus have the same valuation for the variables in

\bar{x}
.

There is a problem with this approach, though. On the one hand,

{\mathsf{Min}}
acts as an incentive to use
t_{+}
,
t_{-}
,
f_{+}
, and
f_{-}
as
witnesses for (
23
)
and (
24
). On the other hand, freezing
implies that if a variable
x
does so, then it triggers an instance
of
{\mathsf{Min}}
on
{x}_{{\mathsf{True}}}
or
{x}_{{\mathsf{False}}}
. There can
hence be models in which fresh instances
e^{+}
of
{\mathsf{Min}}\sqcap{\mathsf{Pos}}

and
e^{-}
of
{\mathsf{Min}}\sqcap{\mathsf{Neg}}
are created instead, leading to
x

satisfying neither
{\mathsf{True}}
nor
{\mathsf{False}}
. Since
{\mathsf{Min}}
is
minimized, however, it must then hold that
e^{+}=e^{-}
,
i.e.

x
is connected to an instance of
{\mathsf{Pos}}\sqcap{\mathsf{Neg}}
. Such models
are easily detected due to our use of positive and negative instances
of
{\mathsf{Min}}
. We make sure that problematic models satisfy the query
and are thus ruled out as a countermodel:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{checks}}(c_{0},v)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }v\in{\bar{x}}\cup{\bar{y}}

(34)

\displaystyle\exists{\mathsf{checks}}.(\exists{\mathsf{eval}}.({\mathsf{Pos}}\sqcap{\mathsf{Neg}}))

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{Goal}}

(35)

Now that we can assume that each variable is given a proper truth value, we
encode the evaluation of
\varphi
. The truth value of each clause is
represented by concept name
{\mathsf{True}}
or
{\mathsf{False}}
holding on the
corresponding individual
c_{i}
. Let

X_{i}=\{\exists{\mathsf{pos}}_{i}.{\mathsf{False}},\exists{\mathsf{neg}}_{i}.{\mathsf{True}}\}
and

X=X_{1}\times X_{2}\times X_{3}
, and add:

\displaystyle\exists{\mathsf{pos}}_{i}.{\mathsf{True}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{True}}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq 3

(36)

\displaystyle\exists{\mathsf{neg}}_{i}.{\mathsf{False}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{True}}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq 3

(37)

\displaystyle C_{1}\sqcap C_{2}\sqcap C_{3}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{False}}\quad\text{for all }(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})\in X

(38)

We next determine the truth value of
\varphi
by walking along the

{\mathsf{next}}
-chain of clauses in
\mathcal{A}
. If the formula evaluates to true,
then we make
{\mathsf{Goal}}
true at
c_{0}
and thus satisfy the query. If it
evaluates to false, we make
{\mathsf{Min}}
true at
c_{0}
. This
reflects the existential quantification over the variables
\bar{y}
:
when they exist, we prefer valuations that make
\varphi
true since
making
\varphi
false gives us an additional instance of the
minimized concept name. Formally:

\displaystyle\exists{\mathsf{next}}.({\mathsf{Last}}\sqcap{\mathsf{True}})

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{Last}}

(39)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{First}}\sqcap{\mathsf{Last}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{Goal}}

(40)

\displaystyle\exists{\mathsf{next}}.{\mathsf{False}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{False}}

(41)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{False}}\sqcap{\mathsf{First}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{Min}}

(42)

It remains to solve another technical issue. As explained above,
we make

{\mathsf{Min}}
true not only at the individuals

t_{+},t_{-},f_{+},f_{-}
, but also at some
individuals
{x}_{{\mathsf{True}}},{x}_{{\mathsf{False}}}
and possibly at

c_{0}
. However, we do not want these individuals to be used as
witnesses for (
23
)
and (
24
) as then there could be
variables that do not receive a truth value.
We can again detect this and then make
{\mathsf{Min}}
true at a
fresh individual
a
:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{checks}}(a,{x}_{{\mathsf{True}}}),{\mathsf{checks}}(a,{x}_{{\mathsf{False}}})

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }x\in{\bar{x}}

(43)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{checks}}(a,c_{0})

(44)

\displaystyle\exists{\mathsf{checks}}.{\mathsf{Pos}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{Min}}

(45)

\displaystyle\exists{\mathsf{checks}}.{\mathsf{Neg}}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\,{\mathsf{Min}}.

(46)

We now set
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
with
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}
as defined
above. It is easily verified that
\mathcal{T}
does not depend on the
instance of
\forall\exists{\sf 3SAT}
. It remains to
prove the following claim:

Claim.

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models{\mathsf{Goal}}(c_{0})
iff
\forall{\bar{x}}\,\exists{\bar{y}}\,\varphi({\bar{x}},{\bar{y}})
.

To prepare for the proof of the claim, we first observe that
every valuation
V
for
\bar{x}\cup\bar{y}
gives rise
to a corresponding model
\mathcal{I}_{V}
of
\mathcal{K}
.
We use domain

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{V}}={\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})

and set

\begin{array}[]{rcl}A^{\mathcal{I}_{V}}&=&\{a\mid A(a)\in\mathcal{A}\}\\[2.84526pt]
r^{\mathcal{I}_{V}}&=&\{(a,b)\mid r(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}\}\end{array}

for all concept names
A\in\{{\mathsf{Var}},{\mathsf{First}},{\mathsf{Pos}},{\mathsf{Neg}}\}

and all role names

r\in\{{\mathsf{pos}}_{i},{\mathsf{neg}}_{i},{\mathsf{next}},{\mathsf{freeze}}_{\mathsf{True}},{\mathsf{freeze}}_{\mathsf{False}},{\mathsf{checks}}\}.

We interpret
{\mathsf{eval}}
according to
V
, that is

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}l}{\mathsf{eval}}^{\mathcal{I}_{V}}&=&\{(v,t_{+}),(v,t_{-})\mid v\in\bar{x}\cup\bar{y}\text{ and }V(v)=1\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{(v,f_{+}),(v,f_{-})\mid v\in\bar{x}\cup\bar{y}\text{ and }V(v)=0\}.\end{array}

The valuation
V
also assigns a truth value to each clause,
let
V(c_{i})
denote the value of the
i^{th}
clause. Put

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathsf{True}}^{\mathcal{I}_{V}}&=&\{t_{+},t_{-}\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{v\mid v\in\bar{x}\cup\bar{y}\text{ and }V(v)=1\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{c_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq\ell\text{ and }V(c_{i})=1\}\\[2.84526pt]
{\mathsf{False}}^{\mathcal{I}_{V}}&=&\{f_{+},f_{-}\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{v\mid v\in\bar{x}\cup\bar{y}\text{ and }V(v)=0\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{c_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq\ell\text{ and }V(c_{i})=1\}.\end{array}

Let
q
be largest such that valuation
V
makes the last

q
of the
\ell
clauses in
\varphi
true. Put

{\mathsf{Last}}^{\mathcal{I}_{V}}=\{c_{\ell}\}\cup\{c_{\ell-1},\dots,c_{\ell-q+1}\}.

It remains to interpret
{\mathsf{Min}}
and
{\mathsf{Goal}}
. As an

\begin{array}[]{rcl}M&=&\{t_{+},t_{-},f_{+},f_{-},\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{{x}_{{\mathsf{True}}}\mid x\in\bar{x}\text{ and }V(x)=1\}\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{{x}_{{\mathsf{False}}}\mid x\in\bar{x}\text{ and }V(x)=0\}.\end{array}

Now if
V
satisfies all clauses in
\varphi
, then put

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathsf{Min}}^{\mathcal{I}_{V}}&=&M\\[2.84526pt]
{\mathsf{Goal}}^{\mathcal{I}_{V}}&=&\{c_{0}\}\end{array}

and otherwise put

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathsf{Min}}^{\mathcal{I}_{V}}&=&M\cup\{c_{0}\}\\[2.84526pt]
{\mathsf{Goal}}^{\mathcal{I}_{V}}&=&\emptyset.\end{array}

It is straightforward to verify that
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is indeed a
model of
\mathcal{K}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{I}_{V}\models q
if and only if

V
satisfies all clauses in
\varphi
. Now for the actual

\Rightarrow

&&\{{x}_{{\mathsf{True}}}\mid x\in\bar{x}\text{ and }V(x)=1\}\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{{x}_{{\mathsf{False}}}\mid x\in\bar{x}\text{ and }V(x)=0\}.\end{array}

Since

Let

)

which implies that

For the remaining proof, we may thus assume
v^{+}\neq v^{-}

for all

.

We next argue that for all
v\in\bar{x}\cup\bar{y}
,

\{v^{+},v^{-}\}\cap\{t_{+},t_{-},f_{+},f_{-}\}\neq\emptyset

(
\ast
)

Assume to the contrary that (
*
) is violated by some

v_{0}\in\bar{x}\cup\bar{y}
, that is,

v_{0}^{+},v_{0}^{-}\notin\{t_{+},t_{-},f_{+},f_{-}\}

Then
we can find a model
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
, contradicting the

\mathcal{I}
. We distinguish two cases:

1.

a\in{\mathsf{Min}}^{\mathcal{I}}

We construct
\mathcal{J}
by choosing

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathsf{eval}}^{\mathcal{J}}&=&\{(v,a)\mid v\in\bar{x}\cup\bar{y}\}\\[2.84526pt]

{\mathsf{Neg}}^{\mathcal{J}}&=&\{t_{-},f_{-},a\}\\[2.84526pt]
{\mathsf{True}}^{\mathcal{J}}&=&\{t_{+},t_{-}\}\\[2.84526pt]

It can be verified that

and

)

Assume to the contrary of what remains to be shown that

. Since

\forall{\bar{x}}\,\exists{\bar{y}}\,\varphi({\bar{x}},{\bar{y}})

Appendix D
Proofs for Section
4.1

See

Proof.

We reduce from (Boolean) UCQ evaluation on
\textrm{DL-Lite}^{\cal H}
KBs with
closed concept names, which is defined in the expected way
and known to be
2Exp
-hard

[
\citeauthoryearNgo, Ortiz, and Simkus2016
]
.
Let
\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}
be a
\textrm{DL-Lite}^{\cal H}
KB with closed concept names,

\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
, and let
q
be a Boolean UCQ. We construct a
circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}^{\cal H}
KB
{\sf Circ}_{\sf CP}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
, with

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{T}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
, and a CQ
q^{\prime}
such that

\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}\models q
iff
{\sf Circ}_{\sf CP}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\models q^{\prime}
.

As
in the proof of
Theorem

2
, we minimize only a
single concept name
M
and include
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. To construct

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, we start from
\mathcal{T}
and extend with additional concept
inclusions. For
q^{\prime}
, we start from
q
and extend with
additional disjuncts. We will actually use individuals

a\in{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
as constants in
q^{\prime}
. These can be
eliminated by introducing a fresh concept name
A_{a}
, extending

\mathcal{A}
with
A_{a}(a)
, and replacing in each CQ in
q^{\prime}
the constant

a
with a fresh variable
x_{a}
and adding the atom
A_{a}(x_{a})
.

As in the proof of
Theorem

2
, we include in

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
the CI

\displaystyle A\sqsubseteq M

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }A\in\Sigma

(47)

and then have to rule out non-asserted instances of closed concept
names. To rule out non-asserted instances inside of
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
,
we add to
q^{\prime}
the disjunct
A(a)
for all
A\in\Sigma
and

a\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
with
A(a)\notin\mathcal{A}
.

To rule out instances of closed concept names outside of

{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
, add

\displaystyle M(t)

(48)

where
t
is a fresh individual. As in the proof of

Theorem

2
, this guarantees that models with
instances of closed concept names outside of
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
are
not minimal. To ensure that no closed concept names are made
true at
t
, we extend
q^{\prime}
with the disjunct
r(x,t)
for every
role name
r
used in
\mathcal{T}
.

Claim.

\mathcal{K}_{\Sigma}\models q
iff
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\models q^{\prime}
.

The proof is rather straightforward; we omit the details.
∎

We now work towards a proof of
Theorem
6
, first establishing the
following.
See
8

Refining the unraveling begins by noticing the set
\Omega
now only contains elements with shape
r\top
as
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
TBoxes are considered.
We drop the
\top
concept for simplicity.
Recall that we assume chosen a representative
e_{t}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
for each non-core type
t\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})
.
We define the set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{bool}}
of
\mathsf{bool}
-paths through
\mathcal{I}
along with
a mapping
h
that assigns to each
p\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{bool}}
an element of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
:

•

each element
d
of the set

\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}:={\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\cup\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}}\cup\{e_{t}\mid t\in\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{\overline{core}}}(\mathcal{I})\}.

is a path in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{bool}}
and

h(d)=d
;

•

if
p\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{bool}}
with
h(p)=d
and
r\in\Omega
such that:

(a)

f(d,r)
is defined and not from
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{{\sf core}}
and

(b)

p
does not end by
r^{-}
, which we denote
{\sf tail}(p)\neq r^{-}
.

then

p^{\prime}=pr
is a path in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{bool}}
and
h(p^{\prime})=f(d,r)
.

For every role
r
, define

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l}R_{r}&=&\{(a,b)\mid a,b\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{K}\models r(a,b)\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{(d,e)\mid d,e\in\Delta_{\mathsf{core}}^{\mathcal{I}},(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}}\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{(p,p^{\prime})\mid p^{\prime}=pr\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{(p,e)\mid e=f(h(p),r)\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}_{{\sf core}},{\sf tail}(p)\neq r^{-}\}.\end{array}

Now the
\mathsf{bool}
-unraveling of
\mathcal{I}
is defined by setting

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}l}\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{bool}}\\[2.84526pt]
A^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\{p\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{bool}}\mid h(p)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}\}\\[2.84526pt]
r^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\displaystyle R_{r}\cup\{(e,d)\mid(d,e)\in R_{r^{-}}\}\end{array}

for all concept names
A
and role names
r
. It is easy to verify
that
h
is a homomorphism from
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{I}
.
It is a technicality to verify that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}
satisfies the same properties as the previous unraveling
{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}
, that is: if
\mathcal{I}
is a countermodel for
q
over
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
, then so is
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}
. The key additional property satisfied by
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}
is the following:

Lemma 20
.

For any
{{r}}\in\mathsf{N_{R}}
and
d_{1}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}}\setminus\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, there is at most one element
d_{2}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}}
such that
(d_{1},d_{2})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}}
.

Proof.

Unfolding the definition of
{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}}
and recalling
d_{1}\notin\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, we obtain that if
d_{1}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}}\setminus\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(d_{1},d_{2})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}}
, then either:

•

(d_{1},d_{2})\in R_{r}
and either
d_{2}=d_{1}r
(Case 1) or
d_{2}=f(h(d_{1}),r)\in\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
{\sf tail}(d_{1})\neq r^{-}
(Case 2).

•

(d_{2},d_{1})\in R_{r^{-}}
and we have
d_{1}=d_{2}r^{-}
(Case 3).

To verify uniqueness, let us assume there exist
d_{2}
and
d_{2}^{\prime}
s.t.
(d_{1},d_{2}^{\prime}),(d_{1},d_{2}^{\prime})\in{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}}
.
We want to prove
d_{2}=d_{2}^{\prime}
.
From the small analysis above, there are
3\times 3
cases to consider:

1.

if
d_{2}=d_{1}r
, then:

1.

if
d_{2}^{\prime}=d_{1}r
, we obtain directly
d_{2}^{\prime}=d_{2}
.

2.

if
d_{2}^{\prime}=f(h(d_{1}),r)\in\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
{\sf tail}(d_{1})\neq r^{-}
, then it contradicts
d_{1}r\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{bool}}
(Condition (a)).

3.

if
d_{1}=d_{2}^{\prime}r^{-}
, then
d_{2}=d_{2}^{\prime}r^{-}r
, which contradicts
d_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{bool}}
(Condition (b)).

2.

if
d_{2}=f(h(d_{1}),r)\in\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
{\sf tail}(d_{1})\neq r^{-}
, then:

1.

if
d_{2}^{\prime}=d_{1}r
, then same argument as 1.2.

2.

if
d_{2}^{\prime}=f(h(d_{1}),r)\in\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
{\sf tail}(d_{1})\neq r^{-}
, then
d_{2}^{\prime}=d_{2}
from
h
and
f
being functions.

3.

if
d_{1}=d_{2}^{\prime}r^{-}
, then it contradicts
{\sf tail}(d_{1})\neq r^{-}
.

3.

if
d_{1}=d_{2}r^{-}
, then:

1.

if
d_{2}^{\prime}=d_{1}r
, then same argument as 1.3.

2.

if
d_{2}^{\prime}=f(h(d_{1}),r)\in\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
{\sf tail}(d_{1})\neq r^{-}
, then same argument as 2.3.

3.

if
d_{1}=d_{2}^{\prime}r^{-}
, we obtain directly
d_{2}^{\prime}=d_{2}
.

∎

Repeated applications of Lemma
20
ensure that each partial homomorphism of a CQ
p\in q
in the non-
\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
part of the bool-unraveling can be completed uniquely in a maximal such partial homomorphism (see further Lemma
21
).
This motivates a refined notion of the neighborhood of an element
d
, restricting the (usual) neighborhood to those elements
e
that can be reached by a homomorphism of some connected subquery involving both
d
and
e
.
Formally, for
n\geq 0
and

\Delta\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, we use
\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I},\Delta}(d)

to denote the
n
-bool-neighborhood of
d
in
\mathcal{I}
up to

\Delta
, that is, the set of all elements
e\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{I},\Delta}(d)

such that there exists a connected subquery
p^{\prime}\subseteq p
for some
p\in q
and a homomorphism
\pi:p^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathcal{I},\Delta}(d)
s.t.
d,e\in\pi(\mathsf{var}(p^{\prime}))
.

The following polynomial bound on their size in the bool-unraveling is the central property allowing bool-neighborhoods to improve our construction.

Lemma 21
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}
its bool-unraveling.
Consider
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}}\setminus\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, then
{\left|{\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}(d)}\right|}\leq{\left|{q}\right|}^{2}({\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1)
.

Proof.

Let
c\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}}}\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}

in a first step, we prove the number of elements in
\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}}(c)\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
is at most
{\left|{q}\right|}^{2}
.
In a second step, we notice each element
e\in\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}}(c)\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
must be connected to an element
d\in\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}}(c)\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
by construction of (usual) neighborhoods.
However, from Lemma
20
, each such element
d
is connected to at most
{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}
elements.
From the first step it follows there are at most
{\left|{q}\right|}^{2}\cdot{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}
elements in
e\in\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}}(c)\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{base}}}
, hence the claimed bound.

It remains to prove the first step.
We start by proving that if the connected subquery
p^{\prime}\subseteq p
for some
p\in q
and the variable
v_{0}
that shall map on
c
are fixed, then all homomorphisms
p^{\prime}\rightarrow{(\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}(c)\,\setminus\,\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}})}
mapping
v_{0}
on
c
are equal.
Consider two such homomorphisms
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
.
We proceed by induction on the variables
v
of
p^{\prime}
being connected.
For
v=v_{0}
, we have
\pi_{1}(v_{0})=\pi_{2}(v_{0})
by definition.
For a further term
v
, we use the induction hypothesis, that is, the existence of an atom
{{r}}(v^{\prime},v)\in p^{\prime}
(or the other way around) such that
\pi_{1}(v^{\prime})=\pi_{2}(v^{\prime})
.
Recall
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are homomorphisms of
p^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}(c)\,\setminus\,\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, in particular
\pi_{1}(v^{\prime}),\pi_{2}(v^{\prime})\notin\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, hence we can apply Lemma
20
, yielding
\pi_{1}(v)=\pi_{2}(v)
.

This proves that, for a fixed
v_{0}\in\mathsf{var}(p)
, each connected subquery
p^{\prime}\subseteq p
admitting a homomorphism in
\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}(c)\,\setminus\,\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
defines at most
{\left|{p}\right|}
new neighbors, but also that if
p^{\prime}\subseteq p^{\prime\prime}\subseteq p
are two such subqueries, then the neighbors defined by
p^{\prime}
are subsumed by those defined by
p^{\prime\prime}
(the restriction to the variables of
p^{\prime}
of the unique homomorphism of
p^{\prime\prime}
mapping
v_{0}
on
c
must coincide with the unique homomorphism of
p^{\prime}
mapping
v_{0}
on
c
).
Still, for a fixed
v_{0}
, consider now two connected subqueries
p_{1},p_{2}\subseteq p
, each admitting a (unique) homomorphism
\pi_{1}
resp.
\pi_{2}
, to
\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}(c)\,\setminus\,\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
mapping
v_{0}
to
c
, and each maximal, w.r.t. the inclusion, for this property.
By the previous property, we know
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
coincide on
\mathsf{var}(p_{1})\cap\mathsf{var}(p_{2})
.
Therefore,
p_{1}\cup p_{2}
admits a homomorphism to
\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}(c)\,\setminus\,\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
mapping
v_{0}
to
c
, being
\pi_{1}\cup\pi_{2}
.
However, since
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are assumed maximal for this property, we must have
p_{1}=p_{2}
.

Therefore, for a fixed
v_{0}\in\mathsf{var}(p)
, there is a unique maximal connected subquery
p_{\textsf{max}}\subseteq p
admitting a homomorphism in
\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}(c)\,\setminus\,\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and mapping
v_{0}
to
c
.
As previously seen, the neighbors defined by
p_{\textsf{max}}
subsume those defined by other subqueries of
p
, and since the homomorphism for
p_{\textsf{max}}
is unique, it defines at most
{\left|{p}\right|}
neighbors.
This holds for each possible choices of term
v_{0}
, hence a total number of possible neighbors issued by
p
bounded by
{\left|{p}\right|}^{2}
.
Iterating over each
p\in q
, we hence obtain the claimed bound of at most
{\left|{q}\right|}^{2}
elements in
\mathcal{N}_{n,\mathsf{bool}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{bool}},\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}}(c)\,\setminus\,\Delta_{\mathsf{base}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
∎

Using bool-neighborhoods in the quotient construction presented in
Section
3.3
, the number of equivalence classes
drops, which yieldings Lemma
8
.

Lemma 22
.

For all
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, we have:

•

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
models
\mathcal{A}
as it inherits interpretations of concepts and roles on
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
from
\mathcal{I}
, which is a model of
\mathcal{A}
.

•

Axioms in
\mathcal{T}
with shape
\top\sqsubseteq{{A}}
,
{{A_{1}}}\sqcap{{A_{2}}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
,
{{A}}\sqsubseteq{{\lnot B}}
or
{{\lnot B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
are satisfied since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
inherits interpretations of concept names from
\mathcal{I}
, which is a model of
\mathcal{T}
.

•

Axioms in
\mathcal{T}
with shape
A\sqsubseteq\exists r
are witnessed with an
r
-edge pointing to
w_{r}
.

•

Axioms in
\mathcal{T}
with shape
\exists r\sqsubseteq A
are satisfied since every element in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
having some
r
-edge already has one in
\mathcal{I}
(and interpretations of concept names are preserved).

•

Role inclusions
r\sqsubseteq s
are satisfied on
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\times{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
from
\mathcal{I}
being a model of
\mathcal{T}
in the first place, otherwise directly from the definition of
s^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

This proves
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}\models\mathcal{K}
as desired.
∎

See
9

To piece together the
\mathcal{J}_{e}
and reconstruct an eventual
\mathcal{J}
, we first prove the following property:

Lemma 23
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{1}
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}
two models of
\mathcal{K}
s.t.
\mathcal{J}_{1}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.
If
\mathcal{J}_{1}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}}=\mathcal{J}_{2}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}}
, then
\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

Proof.

Assume
\mathcal{J}_{1}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}}=\mathcal{J}_{2}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}}
.
It is easily verified that
\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}
is also a model of
\mathcal{K}
since
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
agree on their shared domain.
We now check that all four conditions from the definition of
<_{\sf CP}
are satisfied:

1.

From
\mathcal{J}_{1}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
we have
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}
and similarly for
\mathcal{J}_{2}
we have
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
It follows from its definition that
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}\cup\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
Therefore we have as desired
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.

2.

Let
A\in\mathsf{F}
.
From
\mathcal{J}_{1}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
we have
A^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}
and similarly for
\mathcal{J}_{2}
we have
A^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
It follows from its definition that
A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}\cup A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
Therefore we have as desired
A^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.

3.

Let
A\in\mathsf{M}
such that
A^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}\not\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
We may assume w.l.o.g. that
A
is minimal w.r.t.
\prec
for this property.
We thus have
e\in A^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}\setminus A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
We either have
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}
or
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}
.
We treat the case
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}
, the arguments for
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}
are similar.
From
\mathcal{J}_{1}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
, there exists a concept
B_{1}\prec A
s.t.
B_{1}^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}\subsetneq B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}
.
We chose such a
B_{1}
that is minimal for this property, that is s.t. for all
B\prec B_{1}
, we have
B^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}=B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}
(recall
A
is minimal for its property so that such a
B
must verify
B^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subseteq B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
hence
B^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}\subseteq B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}
).
If
B_{1}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subseteq B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
, then we are done as we obtain
B_{1}^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subsetneq B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
Otherwise, we have
B_{1}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\not\subseteq B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
and from
\mathcal{J}_{2}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, there exists a concept
B_{2}\prec B_{1}
s.t.
B_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subsetneq B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
Since
B_{1}
has been chosen minimal, we have in particular
B_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}=B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}
, which yields
B_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subsetneq B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.

4.

From
\mathcal{J}_{1}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
, there exists a concept
A_{1}\in\mathsf{M}
s.t.
A_{1}^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}\subsetneq A_{1}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}
and for all
B\prec A_{1}
, we have
B^{\mathcal{J}_{1}}=B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}
.
If
A_{1}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subsetneq A_{2}^{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
and for all
B\prec A_{1}
,
B^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}=B^{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, then we obtain directly
A_{1}^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subsetneq A_{1}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
and for all
B\prec A_{1}
,
B^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}=B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
and we are done.
Otherwise:

•

If
A_{1}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\not\subseteq A_{1}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
, then from
\mathcal{J}_{2}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, there exists a concept
A_{2}\prec A_{1}
s.t.
A_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subsetneq A_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
Consider a minimal such
A_{2}
, that is s.t. for all
A\prec A_{2}
, we have
A^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
or
A^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\not\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
Notice the second option cannot happen otherwise from
\mathcal{J}_{2}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
we could obtain
A_{3}\prec A_{2}
s.t.
A_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subsetneq A_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
, contradicting the minimality of
A_{2}
.
Therefore
A_{2}
being minimal yields that for all
A\prec A_{2}
, we have
A^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
Now, from the minimality of
A_{1}
, we extend this to
\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}
and obtain
A_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subsetneq A_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
and for all
B\prec A_{2}
,
B^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}=B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.

•

If there exists
A_{2}\prec A_{1}
s.t.
A_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\neq A_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
Consider a minimal such
A_{2}
, that is s.t. for all
A\prec A_{2}
, we have
A^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
Notice we must have
A_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subseteq A_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
, otherwise from
\mathcal{J}_{2}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
we could obtain
A_{3}\prec A_{2}
s.t.
A_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subsetneq A_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
, contradicting that for all
A\prec A_{2}
, we have
A^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
Therefore, since
A_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\neq A_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
, it must be that
A_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subsetneq A_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.
Now, from the minimality of
A_{1}
, we extend this to
\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}
and obtain
A_{2}^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}\subsetneq A_{2}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
and for all
B\prec A_{2}
,
B^{\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}}=B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}}
.

Overall, this proves
\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}}
as desired.
∎

We are now ready to properly prove Lemma
9
.

Proof of Lemma
9
.

“
1\Rightarrow 2
”.
Assume there exists
\mathcal{J}\models\mathcal{K}
s.t.
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
.
Based on
\mathcal{J}
, we build a subset
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
containing:

•

for each role
r
s.t.
r^{\mathcal{J}}\neq\emptyset
, an element
w_{r}^{\prime}\in(\exists r^{-})^{\mathcal{J}}
;

•

for each
A\in\mathsf{M}
s.t.
A^{\mathcal{J}}\not\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}}
, an element
e_{A}\in B^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus B^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
B\prec A
(Condition 3 in the definition of
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
ensures existence of such
B
and
e_{A}
);

•

an element
e_{\mathsf{M}}\in A^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus A^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
A\in\mathsf{M}
s.t.
A^{\mathcal{J}}\subsetneq A^{\mathcal{I}}
and for all
B\prec A
,
B^{\mathcal{J}}=B^{\mathcal{I}}

(Condition 4 in the definition of
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
ensures existence of such
A
and
e_{\mathsf{M}}
).

Notice
\mathcal{P}
has size at most
2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
.
We now build
\mathcal{J}_{e}
as:

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}=\;

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}}

\displaystyle{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}=\;

\displaystyle{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}

\displaystyle{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}=\;

\displaystyle{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}\cap({\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\times{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A}))

\displaystyle\cup\{(e,w_{s}^{\prime})\mid e\in(\exists s)^{\mathcal{J}},\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(w_{s}^{\prime},e)\mid e\in(\exists s)^{\mathcal{J}},\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r^{-}\}

It is easily verified that
\mathcal{J}_{e}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
, and we now prove
\mathcal{J}_{e}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}
.
We check that all four conditions from the definition of
<_{\sf CP}
are satisfied:

1.

By definition, we have
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}}
.

2.

Let
A\in\mathsf{F}
.
Definitions of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{e}
ensure
A^{\mathcal{I}}\cap{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}}
and
A^{\mathcal{J}}\cap{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}}}=A^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}
.
From
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
, we get
A^{\mathcal{I}}=A^{\mathcal{J}}
, which thus yields
A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}}=A^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}
.

3.

Let
A\in\mathsf{M}
such that
A^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}\not\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}}
.
Therefore
A^{\mathcal{J}}\not\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}}
, and recall we kept in
\mathcal{P}
an element
e_{A}\in B^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus B^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
B\prec A
to belong to
\mathcal{P}
.
Joint with
B^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}\subseteq B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}}
being trivial,
e_{A}
additionally witnesses that
B^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}\subsetneq B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}}
.

4.

Recall we kept in
\mathcal{P}
an element
e_{\mathsf{M}}\in A^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus A^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
A\in\mathsf{M}
s.t.
A^{\mathcal{J}}\subsetneq A^{\mathcal{I}}
and for all
B\prec A
,
B^{\mathcal{J}}=B^{\mathcal{I}}
.
It gives immediately that
A^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}\subsetneq A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}}
and for all
B\prec A
,
B^{\mathcal{J}_{e}}=B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}}
.

This proves
\mathcal{J}_{e}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}
.
It is straightforward from the definition of each
\mathcal{J}_{e}
that for all
e,e^{\prime}
in
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, we have
\mathcal{J}_{e}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}}=\mathcal{J}_{e^{\prime}}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}}
, concluding the proof of “
1\Rightarrow 2
”.

“
2\Rightarrow 1
”.
Assume there exist
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

with
{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
and a
family

(\mathcal{J}_{e})_{e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}}
of models of
\mathcal{K}
such that

\mathcal{J}_{e}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}
and

\mathcal{J}_{e}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}}=\mathcal{J}_{e^{\prime}}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}}
for all
e,e^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Consider such a
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, and family of models
\mathcal{J}_{e}
for each
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.
We build:

\mathcal{J}=\bigcup_{e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}}\mathcal{J}_{e}

It is clear
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and since all
\mathcal{J}_{e}
agree on the shared domain
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
, we apply Lemma
23
to obtain
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
.
∎

See
6

Proof.

We exhibit a
NExp
procedure to decide the complement of our problem, that is, existence of a countermodel for UCQ
q
over
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
cKB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
Our procedure starts by guessing an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
whose domain has size at most
\mathcal{A}+2^{{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}{\left|{q}\right|}}
.
This can be done in exponentially many non-deterministic steps.
It further checks whether
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
that does not entail
q
and rejects otherwise.
This can essentially be done naively in
{\left|{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}}\right|}^{{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}{\left|{q}\right|}}
deterministic steps, that is still simply exponential w.r.t. our input.
The procedure finally checks whether
\mathcal{I}
complies with
{\sf CP}
by
iterating over each
\mathcal{P}
with
{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
and over each model
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P}}
of
\mathcal{K}
s.t.
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P}}<\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
.
If, for a choice of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P}}
, we can find a
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P},e}
for each
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
s.t.
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P},e}\models\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P},e}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P},e}|_{\Delta^{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P}}}}=\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P}}
, then our procedure rejects.
Otherwise, that is all choices of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P}}
led to the existence of a
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
without a fitting
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P},e}
, then it accepts.
Notice that: iterating over such
\mathcal{P}
can be done in
{\left|{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}}\right|}^{2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1}
iterations since we require
{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
.
Computing
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
follows directly from the choice of
\mathcal{P}
.
Since each
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
has polynomial size, iterating over each
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P}}
can be done naively with exponentially many steps.
Further iterating over each
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
can be done in
{\left|{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}}\right|}
steps, which is at most exponential by construction.
Since each
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\{e\}}
has polynomial size, deciding the existence of
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P},e}
can be done naively in exponentially many steps.
Overall, the procedure uses an exponential number of non-deterministic steps at the beginning and further performs several checks using exponentially many more deterministic steps.

It remains to prove an accepting run exists iff there is a countermodel for
q
over
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
If there exists an accepting run, then the corresponding guessed interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
that does not entail
q
, and it must also comply with
{\sf CP}
. Otherwise, the “
1\Rightarrow 2
” direction from Lemma
9
ensures the procedure would have rejected it.
Conversely, if a countermodel exists, Lemma
8
ensures the existence of a countermodel
\mathcal{I}
whose domain has exponential size.
This
\mathcal{I}
can be guessed by the procedure that checks whether it is indeed a model of
\mathcal{K}
not entailing
q
