; hence, do not reject it at first.
The “
2\Rightarrow 1
” direction from Lemma
9
further ensures
\mathcal{I}
also passes the remaining check performed by the procedure; otherwise, it would contradict
\mathcal{I}
being a model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
∎

See
7

Proof.

The proof proceeds by reduction from the complement of the
NExp
-complete
Succinct
-
3COL
problem. An instance of
Succinct
-
3COL
consists of a Boolean circuit
C
with
2n
input gates.
The graph
G_{C}
encoded by
C
has
2^{n}
vertices, identified by binary encodings on
n
bits.
Two vertices
u
and
v
, with respective binary encodings
u_{1}\dots u_{n}
and
v_{1}\dots v_{n}
, are adjacent in
G_{C}
iff
C
returns True when given as input
u_{1}\dots u_{n}
on its first
n
gates and
v_{1}\dots v_{n}
on the second half.
The problem of deciding if
G_{C}
is 3-colorable has been proven to be
NExp
-complete in
[
\citeauthoryearPapadimitriou and
Yannakakis1986
]
.

Let
C
be a Boolean circuit with
2n
input gates.
Let
G=(V,E)
be the corresponding graph with
V=\{v_{1},v_{2},\cdots,v_{2^{n}}\}
.
We denote
\bar{i}
the binary encoding on
n
bits of vertex
v_{i}
.
We also identify
{\sf t}
(True) and
{\sf f}
(False) with their usual binary valuation
1
and
0
, respectively.
We construct a circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf core}
KB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
with
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
and a UCQ
q
that encode a given problem instance.
With the aim to restrict colors and truth values to those defined in the ABox (see axioms (
53
)), we minimize exactly the concept name
{\sf Min}
and let others vary freely.
Our reduction starts by representing the colors (
49
) and truth values (
51
) in the ABox.
The role name
{\sf neq}
makes the inequality relations within them explicit; see axioms (
50
) and (
52
).

\displaystyle{\sf Col}(c)

\displaystyle\text{ for all }c\in\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}

(49)

\displaystyle{\sf neq}(c,c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\text{ for all }c,c^{\prime}\in\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}\text{ with }c\neq c^{\prime}

(50)

\displaystyle{\sf Val}_{b}({\sf val}_{b})

\displaystyle\text{ for all }b\in\{{\sf f},{\sf t}\}

(51)

\displaystyle{\sf neq}({\sf val}_{b},{\sf val}_{b^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\text{ for all }b,b^{\prime}\in\{{\sf f},{\sf t}\}\text{ with }b\neq b^{\prime}

(52)

\displaystyle{\sf Min}(x)

\displaystyle\text{ for all }x\in\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b},{\sf val}_{\sf t},{\sf val}_{\sf f}\}

(53)

We aim countermodels to contain
2^{2n}
elements that each encode a pair
(v_{k},v_{l})\in V^{2}
.
Towards this goal, we lay the foundation to branch a binary tree of depth
n
from a root
a_{\sf tree}
, which will be later enforced via the constructed UCQ further down.
The concept names
({\sf Index}_{i})_{i=1,\cdots,2n}
encode the tree levels, which fork via the role names
{\sf next}_{i,{\sf t}}
and
{\sf next}_{i,{\sf f}}
, encoding whether the
i
-th bit in
\bar{k}\cdot\bar{l}
should be set to
{\sf t}
or
{\sf f}
, respectively.
The desired elements will therefore be (a subset of) the extension
{\sf Index}_{2n}
.
A role name
({\sf hBit}_{j})_{j=1,\cdots,2n}
will later set the actual bit values.
Note that axioms (
57
) require any
i
-th tree level to set the bits
1
to
i
so that the UCQ can later ensure the truth values to coincide with the choice via
{\sf next}
.
Formally:

\displaystyle\mathclap{{\sf Index}_{0}(a_{\sf tree})}

(54)

\displaystyle{\sf Index}_{i-1}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\exists{\sf next}_{i,b}

(55)

\displaystyle\exists{\sf next}^{-}_{i,b}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq{\sf Index}_{i}

(56)

\displaystyle{\sf Index}_{i}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\exists{\sf hBit}_{j}

\displaystyle\text{ for all }j\in\{1,\cdots,i\}

(57)

\displaystyle\exists{\sf hBit}_{j}^{-}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq{\sf Min}

\displaystyle\text{ for all }j\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}

(58)

for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}
and
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
.

We encode the color assignments of
(v_{k},v_{l})
only at the
2n
-th level via the role name
{\sf hCol}
for
v_{k}
and
{\sf hCol}^{\prime}
for
v_{l}
:

\displaystyle{\sf Index}_{2n}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\exists{\sf hCol}

\displaystyle{\sf Index}_{2n}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\exists{\sf hCol}^{\prime}

(59)

\displaystyle\exists{\sf hCol}^{-}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq{\sf Min}

\displaystyle\exists{{\sf hCol}^{\prime}}^{-}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq{\sf Min}

(60)

Finally, the computation of a gate
g
in
C
is enforced for each pair by an outgoing edge
{\sf gate}_{g}
at the
2n
-th level.
Add:

\displaystyle{\sf Index}_{2n}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\exists{\sf gate}_{g}

(61)

\displaystyle\exists{\sf gate}_{g}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq{\sf Min}

(62)

for all gates
g
in
C
.

Let the constructed UCQ
q
be the disjunction over all subsequent queries.
We first make sure that no color is used as a truth value (CQs (
63
) and (
64
)) or vice-versa (CQs (
65
) and (
66
)):

\displaystyle\exists x,y\,{\sf hBit}_{j}(x,y)\wedge{\sf Col}(y)

\displaystyle\text{ for all }j\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}

(63)

\displaystyle\exists x,y\,{\sf gate}_{g}(x,y)\wedge{\sf Col}(y)

\displaystyle\text{ for all gates }g\text{ in }G^{\prime}

(64)

\displaystyle\exists x,y\,{\sf hCol}(x,y)\wedge{\sf Val}_{b}(y)

\displaystyle\text{ for all }b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}

(65)

\displaystyle\exists x,y\,{\sf hCol}^{\prime}(x,y)\wedge{\sf Val}_{b}(y)

\displaystyle\text{ for all }b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}

(66)

We next simultaneously enforce that a) the structure constructed by axioms (
54
)–(
56
) really is a tree, and b) that the valuation required by axioms (
57
) and (
58
) is consistent with the branching of the tree.
We achieve this via CQs (
67
), which makes countermodels assign the bits by
{\sf hBit}
as dictated by
{\sf next}
, and via CQs (
68
), which makes countermodels propagate the bit assignment of a node downwards the tree to all successors:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists x,y,z_{1},z_{2}\,&{\sf next}_{i-1,b}(x,y)\wedge{\sf hBit}_{j}(y,z_{1})\\
&\wedge{\sf neq}(z_{1},z_{2})\wedge{\sf Val}_{b}(z_{2})\end{split}

(67)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists x,y,z_{1},z_{2}\,&{\sf next}_{i-1,b}(x,y)\wedge{\sf hBit}_{j}(x,z_{1})\\
&\wedge{\sf hBit}_{j}(x,z_{2})\wedge{\sf neq}(z_{1},z_{2})\end{split}

(68)

for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}
,
j\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
and
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
.
Observe that the above indeed enforces a tree.

We go on to construct CQs (
69
) and (
70
), which prohibit any two encoded pairs of vertices
(v,u)
and
(v,u^{\prime})
or
(v,u)
and
(u,u^{\prime})
to assign different colors to
v
or
u
, respectively:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists&x,y,c,d,z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\,{\sf hCol}(x,c)\wedge{\sf hCol}(y,d)\\
&\wedge{\sf neq}(c,d)\wedge\bigwedge_{1\leq j\leq n}\big({\sf hBit}_{j}(y,z_{j})\wedge{\sf hBit}_{j}(x,z_{j})\big)\end{split}

(69)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists&x,y,c,d,z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\,{\sf hCol}(x,c)\wedge{\sf hCol}^{\prime}(y,d)\\
&\wedge{\sf neq}(c,d)\wedge\bigwedge_{1\leq j\leq n}\big({\sf hBit}_{j}(y,z_{j})\wedge{\sf hBit}^{\prime}_{j+n}(x,z_{j})\big)\end{split}

(70)

To ensure that the computation of the boolean circuit is consistent, we construct CQs restricting
{\sf gate}_{g}
to follow the logical operator of
g
.
For this, we assume w.l.o.g. that
C
only contains unary NOT-gates, binary AND- and OR-gates, and
2n
nullary INPUT-gates (each encoding a bit in
\bar{k}\cdot\bar{l}
).
Given an INPUT-gate
g
from
C
, we denote the index of the encoded bit from
\bar{k}\cdot\bar{l}
with
i_{g}
.
Construct, for each INPUT-gate
g
from
C
, the CQ as given next:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists x,y,z\,{\sf gate}_{g}(x,y)\wedge{\sf hBit}_{i_{g}}(x,z)\wedge{\sf neq}(y,z)\end{split}

(71)

For all NOT-gates
g
with their parent
g^{\prime}
, construct

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists x,y\,{\sf gate}_{g}(x,y)\wedge{\sf gate}_{g^{\prime}}(x,y)\end{split}

(72)

For all AND-gates
g
with parents
g_{1},g_{2}
, construct

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists x,y,z\,&{\sf gate}_{g}(x,y)\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf t}(y)\wedge{\sf gate}_{g_{1}}(x,z)\\
&\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf f}(z)\end{split}

(73)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists x,y,z\,&{\sf gate}_{g}(x,y)\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf t}(y)\wedge{\sf gate}_{g_{2}}(x,z)\\
&\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf f}(z)\end{split}

(74)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists x,y,z\,&{\sf gate}_{g}(x,y)\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf f}(y)\wedge{\sf gate}_{g_{1}}(x,z)\\
&\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf t}(z)\wedge{\sf gate}_{g_{2}}(x,z)\end{split}

(75)

For all OR-gates
g
with parents
g_{1},g_{2}
, construct

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists x,y,z\,&{\sf gate}_{g}(x,y)\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf f}(y)\wedge{\sf gate}_{g_{1}}(x,z)\\
&\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf t}(z)\end{split}

(76)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists x,y,z\,&{\sf gate}_{g}(x,y)\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf f}(y)\wedge{\sf gate}_{g_{2}}(x,z)\\
&\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf t}(z)\end{split}

(77)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists x,y,z\,&{\sf gate}_{g}(x,y)\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf t}(y)\wedge{\sf gate}_{g_{1}}(x,z)\\
&\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf f}(z)\wedge{\sf gate}_{g_{2}}(x,z)\end{split}

(78)

Finally, we rule out the existence of monochromatic edges in countermodels via CQ (
79
):

\displaystyle\exists x,y,z\,{\sf hCol}(x,y)\wedge{\sf hCol}^{\prime}(x,y)\wedge{\sf gate}_{\dot{g}}(x,z)\wedge{\sf Val}_{\sf t}(z)

(79)

where we here and in what follows denote by
\dot{g}
the output gate of
C
.

Lemma 24
.

G
is 3-colorable iff
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\not\models q
.

“
\Rightarrow
”.
Let
G
be 3-colorable.
Then there exists a 3-coloring
\pi:V\rightarrow\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}
such that
\pi(v)\neq\pi(u)
for all
(v,u)\in V
.
To show that
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\not\models q
, we construct a countermodel
\mathcal{I}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
as follows.
Let
X\coloneqq\bigcup_{j=1,\cdots,2n}\{a_{j,1},\cdots,a_{j,2^{j}}\}
; then set

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\cup X

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\{a\mid A(a)\in\mathcal{A}\}

\displaystyle{\sf neq}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\{(a,b)\mid{\sf neq}(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}\}

\displaystyle{\sf Index}^{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\{a_{i,1},\cdots,a_{i,2^{i}}\}

\displaystyle{\sf next}^{\mathcal{I}}_{i,{\sf t}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\bigcup_{j=1,\cdots,2^{i-1}}\{(a_{i-1,j},a_{i,2j})\}

\displaystyle{\sf next}^{\mathcal{I}}_{i,{\sf f}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\bigcup_{j=1,\cdots,2^{i-1}}\{(a_{i-1,j},a_{i,2j-1})\}

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\sf hBit}^{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\coloneqq&\bigcup_{j=1,\cdots,2^{i-1}}\big(\{(a,{\sf val}_{\sf t})\mid a\in R(a_{i,2j})\}\\
&\phantom{\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq 2^{i-1}}\big(}\cup\{(a,{\sf val}_{\sf f})\mid a\in R(a_{i,2j-1})\}\big)\end{split}

for all concept names
A\in\{{\sf Col},{\sf Val}_{\sf t},{\sf Val}_{\sf f},{\sf Min},{\sf Index}_{0}\}
and
i\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}
, where we use

•

a_{0,1}
to denote
a_{\sf tree}
, and

•

R(a_{k,l})
to denote the set of instances that are reachable in
\mathcal{I}
from
a_{k,l}\in X
via the family of role names
{\sf next}_{i,{\sf t}}
and
{\sf next}_{i,{\sf f}}
(including
a_{k,l}
itself).

We furthermore set
{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}},{{\sf hCol}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{I}}
as follows.
Given some
a_{2n,i}\in X
with
i\in\{1,\cdots 2^{2n}\}
, we denote by
\vartheta(a_{2n,i})
the encoded pair
(v_{k},u_{l})\in V^{2}
, i.e., such that for all
j\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}
, we have
(a_{2n,i},{\sf val}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{j}^{\mathcal{I}}
iff the
j
-th bit in
\bar{k}\cdot\bar{l}
is
b
with
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
.

\displaystyle{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\left\{\big(a_{2n,i},\pi(v)\big)\mid\vartheta(a_{2n,i})=(v,u)\right\}

\displaystyle{{\sf hCol}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\left\{\big(a_{2n,i},\pi(u)\big)\mid\vartheta(a_{2n,i})=(v,u)\right\}

Finally, we set
{\sf gate}_{g}
for all gates
g
in
G_{C}
.
We denote the sub-circuit of
G_{C}
that contains exactly
g
and all its ancestors with
G_{C}^{g}
.
Note that the output gate of
G_{C}^{g}
is
g
, i.e.,
G_{C}^{g}(\bar{k},\bar{l})
computes the value of
g
in
G_{C}
given the input
(\bar{k},\bar{l})
.
Then, for all gates
g
, set:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\sf gate}_{g}^{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq&\{(a_{2n,i},{\sf val}_{b})\mid\vartheta(a_{2n,i})=(v_{k},u_{l})\\
&\phantom{\{(a_{2n,i},{\sf val}_{\sf t})\mid}\text{ and }G_{C}^{g}(\bar{k},\bar{l})=b\}\\
\end{split}

It is obvious that
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
.
As
a\in{\sf Min}^{\mathcal{I}}
iff
{\sf Min}(a)
for all
a\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
{\sf Min}
is the only minimized concept,
\mathcal{I}
must furthermore be minimal.

To see that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
, it is readily checked that

•

\mathcal{I}
by definition has no answer to CQs (
63
)–(
70
),

•

additionally,
\mathcal{I}
has no answer to CQs (
71
)–(
78
), as otherwise, the computation of
G_{C}
would be inconsistent, and

•

finally,
\mathcal{I}
has no answer to CQ (
79
), as otherwise,
\pi
would not be a 3-coloring of
G
.

“
\Leftarrow
”.
Let
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\not\models q
.
Then there is a model
\mathcal{I}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
.
We aim to extract a function
\pi:V\rightarrow\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}
and to show that
\pi
is indeed a 3-coloring of
G
.
This is rather laborious, as we have to prepare the final argument in five steps. In Claim (
3
)–(
7
), respectively, we

1.

briefly argue that
{\sf hBit}
and
{\sf gate}
have the range
\{{\sf val}_{\sf t},{\sf val}_{\sf f}\}
and that
{\sf hCol}
and
{\sf hCol}^{\prime}
have the range
\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}
, which will be convenient later on,

2.

identify within
\mathcal{I}
the intended tree-structure,

3.

show that each leaf indeed encodes a pair
(v_{k},v_{l})\in V^{2}
, setting precisely the bits from
\bar{k}\cdot\bar{l}
,

4.

extract
\pi
from
\mathcal{I}
and show that it is sound in the sense that any two leaves that encode the same vertex also assign it the same color, and

5.

proof that the leaves correctly compute all gates.

We start with the ranges of
{\sf hBit},{\sf gate}
and
{\sf hCol}
:

Claim 3
.

The following properties hold.

•

both
(x,y)\in{\sf hBit}^{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
and
(x,y)\in{\sf gate}^{\mathcal{I}}_{g}
entail
y\in\{{\sf val}_{\sf t},{\sf val}_{\sf f}\}
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}
and gates
g
in
C
, and

•

both
(x,y)\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(x,y)\in{{\sf hCol}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{I}}
entail
y\in\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}
.

The proof is simple: As
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{A}
, axioms (
53
) imply
\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b},{\sf val}_{\sf t},{\sf val}_{\sf f}\}\subseteq{\sf Min}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Furthermore, it is obvious that there cannot be any
a\in{\sf Min}^{\mathcal{I}}
with
{\sf Min}(a)\notin\mathcal{A}
, since
\mathcal{I}
would otherwise not be
<_{\sf CP}
minimal (it would then be straightforward to construct a smaller model).
Ergo,
{\sf Min}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b},{\sf val}_{\sf t},{\sf val}_{\sf f}\}
.

The rest of the claim follows from axioms (
49
), (
51
), (
58
), (
60
), (
62
), CQs (
63
)–(
66
), and
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
:
For example, in the case of
{\sf hCol}
, consider some
(x,y)\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
By axiom (
60
), we have that
y\in{\sf Min}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
CQ (
65
) and the fact that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
together yield
y\in{\sf Min}^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus({\sf Val}_{\sf t}\cup{\sf Val}_{\sf f})
.
As
\{{\sf val}_{\sf t},{\sf val}_{\sf f}\}\subseteq{\sf Val}_{\sf t}\cup{\sf Val}_{\sf f}
by axioms (
51
), we thus have that
y\in\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}
.
The other cases are analogous.

This finishes the proof of Claim (
3
).

We go on by identifying within
\mathcal{I}
the intended tree structure:

Claim 4
.

For all
i\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}
there is a set
Y_{i}\subseteq{\sf Index}^{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
that satisfies the following properties:

1.

for all
y\in Y_{i}
, there is exactly one
parent
p\in Y_{i-1}
of
y
with
(p,y)\in{\sf next}_{i-1,b}^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
(we set
Y_{0}=\{a_{\sf tree}\}
),

2.

for all
y\in Y_{i}
and
j\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
, there is exactly one
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
such that
(y,{\sf val}_{n})\in{\sf hBit}_{j}
,

3.

for all
x,y\in Y_{i}
with
x\neq y
, there is some
j\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
such that
(x,{\sf val}_{\sf t})\in{\sf hBit}_{j}
iff
(y,{\sf val}_{\sf f})\in{\sf hBit}_{j}
, and

4.

|Y_{i}|=2^{i}
.

The proof of Claim (
4
) goes via induction over
i
:

Base case (
i=1)
.

Axioms (
54
)–(
56
) require the existence of some
x,y\in{\sf Index}_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(a_{\sf tree},x)\in{\sf next}_{1,{\sf t}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(a_{\sf tree},y)\in{\sf next}_{1,{\sf f}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Set
Y_{1}=\{x,y\}
.
Property 1. of Claim (
4
) obviously holds as
a_{\sf tree}
is, on the one hand, the only element in
Y_{0}
, and, on the other hand, the parent of both
x
and
y
.
Together with Claim (
3
), axioms (
52
), and (
57
), and CQs (
67
) (and the fact that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
), we have that
(x,{\sf val}_{\sf t})\in{\sf hBit}_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(y,{\sf val}_{\sf f})\in{\sf hBit}_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
, while
(x,{\sf val}_{\sf f})\notin{\sf hBit}_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(y,{\sf val}_{\sf t})\notin{\sf hBit}_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e., also properties 2. and 3. hold.
The above furthermore entails
x\neq y
, which is why also property 4. must hold.

Induction step (
i>1
).

By the induction hypothesis, there is a set
Y_{i-1}
with the claimed properties.
For all
y\in Y_{i-1}
, axioms (
54
)–(
56
) entail the existence of elements
a_{y},a_{y}^{\prime}\in{\sf Index}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(y,a_{y})\in{\sf next}_{i,{\sf t}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(y,a^{\prime}_{y})\in{\sf next}_{i,{\sf f}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Similar as in the base case, we deduct that
(a_{y},{\sf val}_{\sf t})\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(a^{\prime}_{y},{\sf val}_{\sf f})\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
, while
(a_{y},{\sf val}_{\sf f})\notin{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(a^{\prime}_{y},{\sf val}_{\sf t})\notin{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}

({\dagger})
via Claim (
3
), axioms (
52
) and (
57
), and CQs (
67
) (and the fact that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
).
Set
Y_{i}=\bigcup_{y\in Y_{i-1}}\{a_{y},a_{y}^{\prime}\}
.

To see that
Y_{i}
satisfies property 1., assume to the contrary that there is some
a\in Y_{i}
with two parents from
Y_{i-1}
, i.e., that there are two elements
y,y^{\prime}\in Y_{i-1}
such that
(y,a)\in{\sf next}_{i-1,b}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(y^{\prime},a)\in{\sf next}_{i-1,b^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
b,b^{\prime}\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
.
By property 3. of the induction hypothesis, there must be some
j\in\{1,\cdots,i-1\}
such that
(x,{\sf val}_{\sf t})\in{\sf hBit}_{j}^{\mathcal{I}}
iff
(x^{\prime},{\sf val}_{\sf f})\in{\sf hBit}_{j}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Furthermore observe that Claim (
3
) and axioms (
57
) require
a
to assign the
j
-th bit, i.e., we have
(a,{\sf val}_{\sf t})\in{\sf hBit}_{j}^{\mathcal{I}}
or
(a,{\sf val}_{\sf f})\in{\sf hBit}_{j}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
In either case, due to axioms (
52
), we can find a homomorphism from CQ (
68
) to
\mathcal{I}
, which contradicts the fact that
\mathcal{I}
is a countermodel to
q
.
Ergo,
Y_{i}
must satisfy property 1.

Property 2. follows, in case of
j=i
, from
({\dagger})
, and, if otherwise
j\in\{1,\cdots,i-1\}
, from
Y_{i-1}
satisfying property 2. and the fact that Claim (
3
), axioms (
52
) and (
57
), and CQs (
68
) require all
a\in Y_{i}
to assign the bits
1,\cdots,i-1
exactly as their parents.

For property 3, consider some
a,a^{\prime}\in Y_{i}
with
a\neq a^{\prime}
.
If
a
and
a^{\prime}
have the same parent
x
, property 3. follows from
({\dagger})
and property 1.
Otherwise, if
a
has a parent
x
and
a^{\prime}
has a parent
y
such that
x\neq y
, observe that property 3. of the induction hypothesis implies the existence of some
j\in\{1,\cdots,i-1\}
such that
(x,{\sf val}_{\sf t})\in{\sf hBit}_{j}
iff
(y,{\sf val}_{\sf f})\in{\sf hBit}_{j}
.
Property 3. then follows from the fact that Claim (
3
), axioms (
52
) and (
57
), and CQs (
68
) require
a
and
a^{\prime}
to assign the bits
1,\cdots,i-1
exactly as their parents.

Regarding property 4., observe that by
({\dagger})
,
Y_{i}
contains two elements
a_{y},a_{y}^{\prime}
with
a_{y}\neq a_{y}^{\prime}
for each
y\in Y_{i-1}
.
Given any two elements
x,y\in Y_{i-1}
, it is furthermore easy to see that
\{a_{x},a_{x}^{\prime}\}
and
\{a_{y},a_{y}^{\prime}\}
are disjoint, as by property 2. and 3. of the induction hypothesis, there must be some bit
j\in\{1,\cdots,i-1\}
that
x
and
y
assign differently, which subsequently must then also be assigned differently by
\{a_{x},a_{x}^{\prime}\}
and
\{a_{y},a_{y}^{\prime}\}
due to Claim (
3
), axioms (
52
) and (
57
), and CQs (
68
).
As, thus,
|Y_{i}|=2|Y_{i-1}|
, property 4. of the induction hypothesis implies
|Y_{i}|=2^{i}
.

This finishes the proof of Claim (
4
).

We go on to identify the instances in
\mathcal{I}
that encode pairs of vertices:

Claim 5
.

We can identify a set
Y\subseteq{\sf Index}_{2n}^{\mathcal{I}}
and a bijective function
\vartheta:Y\rightarrow V^{2}
such that
\vartheta(x)=(v_{k},u_{l})
implies
(x,{\sf val}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
iff the
i
-th bit in
\bar{k}\cdot\bar{l}
is
b
for all
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
and
i\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}
.

Claim (
5
) builds upon Claim (
4
); set
Y=Y_{2n}
.
By property 2. of Claim (
4
), each
y\in Y
assigns a unique
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
to every
i
-th bit with
i\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}
, encoded via
(y,{\sf val}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Ergo, we can find a function
\vartheta:Y\rightarrow V^{2}
such that
\vartheta(y)=(v_{k},u_{l})
implies
(y,{\sf val}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
iff the
i
-th bit in
\bar{k}\cdot\bar{l}
is
b
for all
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
and
i\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}
.

Note that
\vartheta
must be surjective, as by
|Y|=2^{2n}
(property 4 of Claim (
4
)) and the fact that all
x,y\in Y
pairwise assign at least one bit differently (property 3. of Claim (
4
)).

To see that
\vartheta
is injective, consider some
x,y\in Y
with
\vartheta(x)=(v_{k},u_{l})=\vartheta(y)
.
By definition of
\vartheta
, we have that
(x,{\sf val}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
iff
(y,{\sf val}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
for all with
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
and
i\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}
, and, together with property 2 of Claim (
4
), even that
(x,{\sf val}_{\sf t})\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
iff
(y,{\sf val}_{\sf f})\notin{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Property 3. of Claim (
4
) then entails
x=y
.

This finishes the proof of Claim (
5
).

We go on to extract
\pi
from
\mathcal{I}
and show that
\mathcal{I}
is sound in the sense that any two elements
x,y\in Y
that encode the same vertex also assign it the same color as defined by
\pi
.

Claim 6
.

The relation
\pi:V\rightarrow\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}
with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\pi(v)\coloneqq\{c\in\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}\mid&\exists y\in Y\text{ such that }\vartheta(y)=(v,u)\\
&\text{ and }(y,c)\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}\}.\end{split}

is a function such that
(y,c_{1})\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(y,c_{2})\in{{\sf hCol}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{I}}
hold for all
y\in Y
with
\vartheta(y)=(v,u)
,
\pi(v)=c_{1}
and
\pi(u)=c_{2}
.

Indeed,
\pi
is a function:
On one hand, Claim (
3
) and axioms (
59
) ensure the existence of some
c,d\in\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}
such that
(y,c)\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(y,d)\in{\sf hCol}^{\prime\mathcal{I}}
for all
y\in Y
.
On the other hand,
\pi(y)
is unique for all
y\in Y
:
Assume to the contrary that there are two elements
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
such that
\vartheta(y)=(v,u)
with
(y,c)\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
\vartheta(y^{\prime})=(v,u^{\prime})
with
(y^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
c^{\prime}\neq c
.
By definition of
\vartheta
,
y
and
y^{\prime}
assign the bits with
1,\cdots,n
the same, i.e.,
(y,z)\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
iff
(y^{\prime},z)\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Furthermore,
c^{\prime}\neq c
yields
(c^{\prime},c)\in{\sf neq}^{\mathcal{I}}
via axioms (
50
).
Then, however, there must be a homomorphism from CQ (
69
) to
\mathcal{I}
, which contradicts the fact that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
.

Now, consider some
y\in Y
with
\vartheta(y)=(v,u)
,
\pi(v)=c_{1}
and
\pi(u)=c_{2}
.
We first show that
(y,c_{1})\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
The definition of
\pi
requires the existence of some
y^{\prime}\in Y
such that
\vartheta(y^{\prime})=(v,u^{\prime})
and
(y^{\prime},c_{1})\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
, while there must also be some
c\in\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}
such that
(y,c)\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
via Claim (
3
) and axioms (
59
).
By
\vartheta(y)=(v,u)
and
\vartheta(y^{\prime})=(v,u^{\prime})
, Claim (
5
) implies that
(y,{\sf val}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
iff
(y^{\prime},{\sf val}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
and
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
.
Then, we have that
(c_{1},c)\notin{\sf neq}^{\mathcal{I}}
via CQs (
69
) and the fact that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
.
Ergo, by Claim (
3
), and axioms (
50
) and (
59
), we have that
c=c_{1}
, i.e.,
(y,c_{1})\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

We go on to show that
(y,c_{2})\in{{\sf hCol}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Again, the definition of
\pi
requires the existence of some
y^{\prime}\in Y
such that
\vartheta(y^{\prime})=(u,u^{\prime})
and
(y^{\prime},c_{2})\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
, while there must also be some
c\in\{{\sf r},{\sf g},{\sf b}\}
such that
(y,c)\in{{\sf hCol}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{I}}
via Claim (
3
) and axioms (
59
).
By
\vartheta(y)=(v,u)
and
\vartheta(y^{\prime})=(v,u^{\prime})
, Claim (
5
) implies that
(y,{\sf val}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{i+n}^{\mathcal{I}}
iff
(y^{\prime},{\sf val}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
and
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
.
Then, we have that
(c_{2},c)\notin{\sf neq}^{\mathcal{I}}
via CQs (
70
) and the fact that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
.
Ergo, by Claim (
3
), and axioms (
50
) and (
59
), we have that
c=c_{1}
, i.e.,
(y,c_{1})\in{{\sf hCol}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

This finishes the proof of Claim (
6
).

As a last step before proving that
\pi
indeed is a 3-coloring, we need to argue that the computation of the logical gates within
\mathcal{I}
is sound.

Claim 7
.

For all
y\in Y
, we have that
\vartheta(y)\in E
implies
(y,{\sf var}_{\sf t})\in{\sf gate}_{\dot{g}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, where
\dot{g}
is the output gate of
C
.

As
\vartheta(y)\in E
implies
C^{\dot{g}}(\bar{k},\bar{l})={\sf t}
, it suffices to show that
C^{g}(\bar{k},\bar{l})=b
implies
(y,{\sf var}_{b})\in{\sf gate}_{g}^{\mathcal{I}}
for all
y\in Y
with
\vartheta(y)=(u_{k},v_{l})
, gates
g
in
C
and
b\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
.
We do this per induction over the structure of
C^{g}
:

g
is an INPUT gate.

Let
C^{g}(\bar{k},\bar{l})=b
.
Then, as
g
is an INPUT gate, the
i_{g}
-th bit in
\bar{k}\cdot\bar{l}
must be
b
.
Thus,
(y,{\sf var}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{i_{g}}^{\mathcal{I}}
by definition of
\vartheta
.
Claim (
3
) and axioms (
61
) entail
(y,{\sf var}_{b^{\prime}})\in{\sf gate}_{g}^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
b^{\prime}\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
.
Finally,
(y,{\sf var}_{b})\in{\sf hBit}_{i_{g}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, axioms (
52
), CQs (
71
), and the fact that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
entail
b^{\prime}=b
, i.e.,
(y,{\sf var}_{b})\in{\sf gate}_{g}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

g
is a NOT gate with parent
g^{\prime}
.

Let
C^{g}(\bar{k},\bar{l})=b
.
As
g
is a NOT gate with parent
g^{\prime}
, we have that
C^{g^{\prime}}(\bar{k},\bar{l})=b^{\prime}
for some
b^{\prime}\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
such that
b^{\prime}\neq b
.
By the induction hypothesis, we then have that
(y,{\sf var}_{b^{\prime}})\in{\sf gate}_{g^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Claim (
3
) and axioms (
61
) entail
(y,{\sf var}_{b^{\prime\prime}})\in{\sf gate}_{g}^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
b^{\prime\prime}\in\{{\sf t},{\sf f}\}
.
Due to
b^{\prime}\neq b
, CQs (
72
) and the fact that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
, it must be so that
b^{\prime\prime}=b
, i.e.,
(y,{\sf var}_{b})\in{\sf gate}_{g}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

g
is a AND or OR gate.

We omit the details, as these cases are very similar to the NOT case, except that the respective semantics of the logical operators need to be applied.

We are now in the position to show that
\pi
indeed is a 3-coloring.
Assume to the contrary that there is an edge
(v,u)\in E
with
\pi(v)=\pi(u)\coloneqq c
.
Since
\theta
is a bijection as of Claim (
5
), there exists a unique
y\in Y
such that
\vartheta(y)=(v,u)
.
We have
(y,\pi(v))\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(y,\pi(u))\in{{\sf hCol}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{I}}
by Claim (
6
), i.e.,
(y,c)\in{\sf hCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
(y,c)\in{{\sf hCol}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Furthermore, by Claim (
7
),
(v,u)\in E
entails
(y,{\sf var}_{\sf t})\in{\sf gate}_{\dot{g}}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Together with axioms (
51
), there must be a homomorphism from CQ (
79
) to
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.
However, this contradicts the fact that
\mathcal{I}
is a countermodel to
q
.
Ergo,
\pi
must indeed be a 3-coloring, i.e.,
G
is 3-colorable.
∎

Appendix E
Proofs for Section
4.2

See
10

Proof.

Before proving the lemma, we recall the set
E
gathers elements
e_{t_{1},t_{2}}
which will serve as references for others with
same types.
To lighten notation, we introduce the mapping
\mathsf{ref}:e\mapsto e_{\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{A}}({e}),\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({e})}
defined on
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
.

“
1\Rightarrow 2
”.
Assume
\mathcal{I}\models{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
Consider
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
s.t.
{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
.
We first verify that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
models
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
:

•

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
models
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P}}
as it inherits interpretations of concepts on
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
from
\mathcal{I}
, which is a model of
\mathcal{A}
.

•

Axioms in
\mathcal{T}
with shape
\top\sqsubseteq{{A}}
,
{{A_{1}}}\sqcap{{A_{2}}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
,
{{A}}\sqsubseteq{{\lnot B}}
or
{{\lnot B}}\sqsubseteq{{A}}
are satisfied since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
inherits interpretations of concept names from
\mathcal{I}
, which is a model of
\mathcal{T}
.

•

Axioms in
\mathcal{T}
with shape
A\sqsubseteq\exists r
are witnessed with an
r
-edge pointing to
w_{r}
.

•

Axioms in
\mathcal{T}
with shape
\exists r\sqsubseteq A
are satisfied since every element in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
receiving some
r
-edge already received some in
\mathcal{I}
(and interpretations of concept names are preserved).

•

Role inclusions
r\sqsubseteq s
are satisfied directly from the definition of
s^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

It remains to prove that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
also complies with
{\sf CP}
.
Assume to the contrary that there exists a model
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
with
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
.
Notice that, for each role
r
s.t.
r^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
, there must be an element
w_{r}^{\prime}\in(\exists r^{-})^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
.
We can chose such an element
w_{r}^{\prime}
for each such role
r
, and build the following interpretation
\mathcal{J}
:

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}=\;

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}=\;

\displaystyle{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\cup\{e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\mid\mathsf{ref}(e)\in A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}}=\;

\displaystyle\{(a,b)\mid\mathcal{K}\models r(a,b)\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(e,w_{s}^{\prime})\mid\mathsf{ref}(e)\in(\exists s)^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}},\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(w_{s}^{\prime},e)\mid\mathsf{ref}(e)\in(\exists s)^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}},\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r^{-}\}

It is easily verified that
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
, the most interesting cases being axioms in
\mathcal{T}
with shape either
A\sqsubseteq\exists r
or
\exists r\sqsubseteq A
.
The former are satisfied using the witness
w_{r}
.
The latter, when triggered on
a\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
due to a pair
(a,b)
s.t.
\mathcal{K}\models r(a,b)
, hold thanks to
\mathsf{ref}(a)
having same ABox-type as
a
.
We now prove
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
, which will contradict
\mathcal{I}\models{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
It suffices to prove that for each concept name
A
and
\odot\in\{\subseteq,\supseteq\}
, we have
A^{\mathcal{J}}\odot A^{\mathcal{I}}
iff
A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\odot A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
Let
A
be a concept name.
We prove the claim for
\odot=\;\subseteq
only; for
\odot=\;\supseteq
, the arguments are similar.

“
\Rightarrow
”.
Let
A^{\mathcal{J}}\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}}
and
d\in A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
.
In particular,
d\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
by definition of
A^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Thus, by hypothesis,
d\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
, and since
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
we obtain
d\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
by definition of
A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

“
\Leftarrow
”.
Let
A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
d\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
; we need to show
d\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
.
It is straightforward if
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
.
Otherwise, we know that
\mathsf{ref}(d)\in A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
by definition of
A^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Therefore
\mathsf{ref}(d)\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
by hypothesis, that is
A\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({\mathsf{ref}(d)})
.
Since
d
and
\mathsf{ref}(d)
have same type, it yields
A\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}({d})
, that is
d\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
.

“
2\Rightarrow 1
”.
Assume
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}\models{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}})
for all
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
s.t.
{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
.
By hypothesis, we already have
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{K}
.
By contradiction, assume now there exists
\mathcal{J}<\mathcal{I}
.
Based on
\mathcal{J}
, we build a subset
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
containing:

•

for each role
r
s.t.
r^{\mathcal{J}}\neq\emptyset
, an element
w_{r}^{\prime}\in(\exists r^{-})^{\mathcal{J}}
;

•

for each
A\in\mathsf{M}
s.t.
A^{\mathcal{J}}\not\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}}
, an element
e_{A}\in B^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus B^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
B\prec A
(Condition 3 in the definition of
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
ensures existence of such
B
and
e_{A}
);

•

an element
e_{\mathsf{M}}\in A^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus A^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
A\in\mathsf{M}
s.t.
A^{\mathcal{J}}\subsetneq A^{\mathcal{I}}
and for all
B\prec A
,
B^{\mathcal{J}}=B^{\mathcal{I}}

(Condition 4 in the definition of
<_{\sf CP}
ensures the existence of such
A
and
e_{\mathsf{M}}
).

Notice
\mathcal{P}
has size at most
2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
.
We now build
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
as:

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\;

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}

\displaystyle{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\;

\displaystyle{{A}}^{\mathcal{J}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle{{r}}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\;

\displaystyle\{(e,w_{s}^{\prime})\mid e\in(\exists s)^{\mathcal{J}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}},\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(w_{s}^{\prime},e)\mid e\in(\exists s)^{\mathcal{J}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}},\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r^{-}\}

It is easily verified that
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
, and we now prove
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
, which will contradict
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}\models{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}})
.
We now check that all four conditions from the definition of
<_{\sf CP}
are satisfied:

1.

By definition, we have
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

2.

Let
A\in\mathsf{F}
. Definitions of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
ensure
A^{\mathcal{I}}\cap{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}}=A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
A^{\mathcal{J}}\cap{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}}=A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
.
From
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
, we get
A^{\mathcal{I}}=A^{\mathcal{J}}
, which thus yields
A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}=A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
.

3.

Let
A\in\mathsf{M}
such that
A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\not\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
Therefore
A^{\mathcal{J}}\not\subseteq A^{\mathcal{I}}
, and recall we kept in
\mathcal{P}
an element
e_{A}\in B^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus B^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
B\prec A
to belong to
\mathcal{P}
.
Joint with
B^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\subseteq B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
being trivial,
e_{A}
additionally witnesses that
B^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\subsetneq B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

4.

Recall we kept in
\mathcal{P}
an element
e_{\mathsf{M}}\in A^{\mathcal{J}}\setminus A^{\mathcal{I}}
for some
A\in\mathsf{M}
s.t.
A^{\mathcal{J}}\subsetneq A^{\mathcal{I}}
and for all
B\prec A
,
B^{\mathcal{J}}=B^{\mathcal{I}}
.
It gives immediately that
A^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\subsetneq A^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and for all
B\prec A
,
B^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

This proves
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
, contradicting
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}\models{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}})
as desired.
∎

See
8

Proof.

We exhibit an
NP
procedure to decide the complement of our problem, that is existence of a countermodel for UCQ
q
over
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}^{\cal H}
cKB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
Our procedure starts by guessing an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
whose domain has size at most
|\mathcal{A}|+(2^{|\mathcal{T}|+2}+1)^{3|q|}
.
This can be done in linearly many non-deterministic steps (w.r.t. data complexity)
It further checks whether
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
that does not entail
q
and rejects otherwise.
This can essentially be done naively in
{\left|{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}}\right|}^{{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}{\left|{q}\right|}}
deterministic steps, that is still polynomial
w.r.t. data complexity.
The procedure finally checks whether
\mathcal{I}
complies with
{\sf CP}
by
iterating over each
\mathcal{P}
with
{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
and checking whether there exists a model
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P}}
of
\mathcal{K}
s.t.
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P}}<\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
.
If, for a choice of
\mathcal{P}
and we can find such a
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P}}
, then our procedure rejects.
Notice that iterating over such
\mathcal{P}
can be done in
{\left|{\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}}\right|}^{2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1}
iterations since we require
{\left|{\mathcal{P}}\right|}\leq 2{\left|{\mathcal{T}}\right|}+1
.
Computing
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
follows directly from the choice of
\mathcal{P}
.
Since each
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{P}}
has constant size w.r.t. data complexity, iterating over each possible
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{P}}
can be done naively with constantly many steps.
Overall, the procedure uses a linear number of non-deterministic steps at the beginning and further performs several checks using a polynomial number of
additional deterministic steps.

It remains to prove that an accepting run exists if there is a countermodel for
q
over
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
If there exists an accepting run, then the corresponding guessed interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}
that does not entail
q
, and the “
2\Rightarrow 1
” direction from Lemma
10
ensures it also comply+ies with
{\sf CP}
.
Conversely, if there exists a countermodel, then Lemma
7
ensures existence of a countermodel
\mathcal{I}
whose domain has size at most
|\mathcal{A}|+(2^{|\mathcal{T}|+2}+1)^{3|q|}
.
The procedure can guess this
\mathcal{I}
, and as
\mathcal{I}
is indeed a model of
\mathcal{K}
not entailing
q
, it will not be rejected.
The “
1\Rightarrow 2
” direction from Lemma
9
further ensures
\mathcal{I}
also passes the remaining checks performed by the procedure.
∎

See
9

Proof.

We reduce the complement of the graph 3-colorability problem (3Col) to evaluating the Boolean CQ:

q=\exists y_{1}\,\exists y_{2}\,\exists y~\mathsf{edge}(y_{1},y_{2})\land\mathsf{hasCol}(y_{1},y)\land\mathsf{hasCol}(y_{2},y)

over the
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf pos}
TBox:

\mathcal{T}=\{\mathsf{Vertex}\sqsubseteq\exists\mathsf{hasCol},\exists\mathsf{hasCol}^{-}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Color}\},

where
\mathsf{Color}
is minimized while all other predicates vary.
Let
{\sf CP}
be the resulting circumscription pattern.
Given an instance
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
of 3Col, we build an ABox
\mathcal{A}
containing the following assertions:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Vertex}(v)

\displaystyle\text{ for all }v\in\mathcal{V}

(80)

\displaystyle\mathsf{edge}(v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle\text{ for all }\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in\mathcal{E}

(81)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Color}(c)

\displaystyle\text{ for all }c\in\{r,g,b\}

(82)

We now prove the following claim:

\mathcal{G}\notin\text{3Col}\iff{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models q

First notice that every model
\mathcal{I}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
verifies
\mathsf{Color}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{r,g,b\}
.
Indeed, by contradiction, if
\mathcal{I}
contains
e\in\mathsf{Color}^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\{r,g,b\}
, then we can build
\mathcal{J}
a model of
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
by modifying
\mathcal{I}
as follows:

•

remove
e
from
\mathsf{Color}^{\mathcal{I}}
, that is
\mathsf{Color}^{\mathcal{J}}=\mathsf{Color}^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\{e\}
;

•

reroute
\mathsf{hasCol}
as:
\mathsf{hasCol}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(v,r)\mid v\in(\exists\mathsf{hasCol})^{\mathcal{I}}\}
.

It is then straightforward that
\mathcal{J}
satisfies the desired properties, contradicting
\mathcal{I}
being a model of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.

“
\Rightarrow
”. Assume
\mathcal{G}\notin\text{3Col}
and consider a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{J}
.
By the above remark, we have
\mathsf{Color}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{r,g,b\}
and from
\mathcal{I}
being a model of
\mathcal{K}
, we can find a mapping
\tau:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\{r,g,b\}
of
\mathcal{G}
such that for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
, if
\tau(v)=c
then
(v,c)\in\mathsf{hasCol}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Since, by assumption,
\mathcal{G}\notin\text{3Col}
, there exists an edge
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in\mathcal{E}
such that
\tau(v_{1})=\tau(v_{2})=c
for some
c\in\{r,g,b\}
, which ensures
y_{1}\mapsto v_{1},y_{2}\mapsto v_{2},y\mapsto c
is an homomorphism of
q
in
\mathcal{I}
.

“
\Leftarrow
”. Assume
\mathcal{G}\in\text{3Col}
and consider a 3-coloring
\tau:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\{r,g,b\}
.
We build a model
\mathcal{I}_{\tau}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
by interpreting all concepts and roles as in
\mathcal{A}
except for
\mathsf{hasCol}
, which is interpreted as:

\mathsf{hasCol}^{\mathcal{I}_{\tau}}=\{(v,c)\mid v\in\mathcal{V},\tau(v)=c\}.

It is straightforward that
\mathcal{I}
models
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
and does not embed
q
(since
\tau
is a 3-coloring).
∎

Appendix F
Proofs for Section
5.1

We briefly discuss the variation of Proposition
1
mentioned in the main part of the paper.
Assume given a circumscribed
\mathcal{ALCHIO}
KB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
with
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
, an AQ
A_{0}(x)
and an individual
a_{0}\in\mathsf{ind}(\mathcal{A})
.
We again replace all occurrences of a nominal
a
with fresh concept
A_{a}
, add
A_{a}(a),B_{a}(a)
to
\mathcal{A}
, minimize
B_{a}
and set
B_{a}\prec A
for all
A\in\mathsf{M}
.
However, in contrast with Proposition
1
, we also add axioms
A_{a}\sqcap\neg B_{a}\sqsubseteq\exists r.(X\sqcap A_{0})
and
X\sqsubseteq Y
to
\mathcal{T}
for some fresh concept names
X
and
Y
and fresh role name
r
, and
Y(a_{0})
to
\mathcal{A}
.
Finally, we also minimize
Y
with higher preference than concepts from
\mathsf{M}
: We set
Y\prec A
for all
A\in\mathsf{M}
.
Denoting
{\sf Circ}_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
the resulting circumscribed
\mathcal{ALCHI}
KB, it can then be verified that

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models A_{0}(a_{0})
iff

{\sf Circ}_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\models A_{0}(a_{0})
.

We now move to a proof of Theorem
11
.

See
11

Proof.

Let
{\sf Circ}_{\sf CP}(\mathcal{K})
be a circumscribed
\mathcal{ALC}
KB, with
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
,
A_{0}
an AQ, and
a_{0}\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
. We construct a
circumscribed
{\cal EL}
KB
{\sf Circ}_{\sf CP}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
, with

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{T}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
, such that

{\sf Circ}_{\sf CP}(\mathcal{K})\models A_{0}(a_{0})
iff
{\sf Circ}_{\sf CP}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\models A_{0}(a_{0})
.

With
{\cal ELU}_{\bot}
, we mean the extension of
{\cal EL}
with disjunction and
\bot
.
It is well-known that every
\mathcal{ALC}
TBox
\mathcal{T}
can be rewritten in polynomial time into an
{\cal ELU}_{\bot}
TBox
\mathcal{T}^{\ast}
that is a conservative extension of
\mathcal{T}
in the sense that every
model of
\mathcal{T}^{\ast}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
and every model of
\mathcal{T}
can be extended to a model of
\mathcal{T}^{\ast}
by interpreting the fresh concept and role names in
\mathcal{T}^{\ast}

[
\citeauthoryearBaader, Brandt, and
Lutz2005
]
. It follows that
{\sf Circ}_{\sf CP}(\mathcal{K})\models A_{0}(a_{0})
iff
{\sf Circ}_{\sf CP}(\mathcal{T}^{\ast},\mathcal{A})\models A_{0}(a_{0})
, assuming that all the fresh concept names in
\mathcal{T}^{\ast}
vary in
{\sf CP}
.
In the following, we recall the rewriting, which proceeds in three steps:

1.

Remove value restrictions
by replacing every subconcept

\forall r.C
with
\neg\exists r.\neg C
.

2.

Remove negation of compound concepts
by replacing
every subconcept
\neg C
, with
C
compound, by
\neg X

where
X
is a fresh concept name, and adding the CIs

C\sqsubseteq X\qquad X\sqsubseteq C.

3.

Remove negation entirely
by replacing every subconcept

\neg A
with the fresh concept name
\overline{A}
, and adding
the CIs

\top\sqsubseteq A\sqcup\overline{A}\qquad A\sqcap\overline{A}\sqsubseteq\bot.

We may thus assume w.l.o.g. that the TBox
\mathcal{T}
in the given circumscribed KB

\mathcal{K}
is
an
{\cal ELU}_{\bot}
TBox. We may further assume that disjunction occurs in
\mathcal{T}
only in CIs of the form

B\sqsubseteq B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}

where
B,B_{1},B_{2}
are concept names, by replacing every disjunction

C_{1}\sqcup C_{2}
with a fresh concept name
X
and adding the CIs

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}lcr@{\;}c@{\;}lcr@{\;}c@{\;}l}X&\sqsubseteq&Y_{1}\sqcup Y_{2}&&Y_{1}&\sqsubseteq&X&&Y_{2}&\sqsubseteq&X\\[2.84526pt]
C_{i}&\sqsubseteq&Y_{i}&&Y_{i}&\sqsubseteq&C_{i}&&\text{for }i\in\{1,2\}\end{array}

where also
Y_{1},Y_{2}
are fresh concept names. Finally, we may assume
that
\mathcal{T}
contains only a single CI
B\sqsubseteq B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}

by replacing every such CI with

B\sqsubseteq\exists r_{B_{1},B_{2}}.D\quad\exists r_{B_{1},B_{2}}.D_{1}\sqsubseteq B_{1}\quad\exists r_{B_{1},B_{2}}.D_{2}\sqsubseteq B_{2}

where
r_{B_{1},B_{2}}
is a fresh role name and
D,D_{1},D_{2}
are fresh concept names,
and adding the CI

D\sqsubseteq D_{1}\sqcup D_{2}.

We may clearly also assume that
\bot
occurs in
\mathcal{T}
only in the form

C\sqsubseteq\bot
with
C
an
{\cal EL}
-concept.
Note that the TBoxes resulting from these transformations are conservative
extensions of the original ones. In
{\sf CP}
, all of the freshly introduced
concept names vary.

We are now ready for the actual reduction, which combines ideas from
the proofs of Theorems
2
and
4
. To construct the TBox
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
,
We start with relativized versions of the
{\cal EL}
-CIs in
\mathcal{T}
.
For an
{\cal EL}
concept
C
,
inductively define the concept
C_{X}
as follows:

\displaystyle A_{X}

\displaystyle\coloneqq X\sqcap A

\displaystyle(\exists r.D)_{X}

\displaystyle\coloneqq X\sqcap\exists r.D_{X}

\displaystyle\top_{X}

\displaystyle\coloneqq X

\displaystyle(D\sqcap D^{\prime})_{X}

\displaystyle\coloneqq D_{X}\sqcap D^{\prime}_{X}

We then include in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
all CIs

\displaystyle C_{X}\sqsubseteq D_{X}

(83)

such that
C\sqsubseteq D\in\mathcal{T}
is neither the unique disjunctive
CI
B\sqsubseteq B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}

nor of the form
C\sqsubseteq\bot
. To construct the ABox
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
,
we start with the extension of
\mathcal{A}
by

\displaystyle X(a)\quad\text{ for all }a\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A}).

(84)

Of course, we need to compensate for removing the mentioned CIs.
To simulate the disjunctive CI
B\sqsubseteq B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}
, we
extend
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
with

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\exists r_{D}.(D\sqcap{\sf Pos})\qquad\exists r_{D}.D_{1}\sqsubseteq B_{1}

(85)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\exists r_{D}.(D\sqcap{\sf Neg})\qquad\exists r_{D}.D_{2}\sqsubseteq B_{2}

(86)

where
D,D_{1},D_{2},{\sf Pos}
, and
{\sf Neg}
are fresh concept names and
r_{D}
is a fresh role name.
We update
{\sf CP}
so that
D
is minimized. We further extend
\mathcal{A}
with the
assertions

\displaystyle D(d_{1}^{+})

\displaystyle D(d_{1}^{-})

\displaystyle D(d_{2}^{+})

\displaystyle D(d_{2}^{-})

(87)

\displaystyle D_{1}(d_{1}^{+})

\displaystyle D_{1}(d_{1}^{-})

\displaystyle D_{2}(d_{2}^{+})

\displaystyle D_{2}(d_{2}^{-})

(88)

\displaystyle{\sf Pos}(d_{1}^{+})

\displaystyle{\sf Neg}(d_{1}^{-})

\displaystyle{\sf Pos}(d_{2}^{+})

\displaystyle{\sf Neg}(d_{2}^{-}).

(89)

where
d^{+}_{1},d^{-}_{1},d^{+}_{2},d^{-}_{2}
are fresh individuals. Note that these individuals are
not
marked with
X
, exempting them from
\mathcal{T}
is why the CIs from
\mathcal{T}
are relativized in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. Note that the way we simulate disjunction is similar to how we choose truth values in the proof of Theorem
4
, and also the reason for using a positive and a negative witness is the same.
We need to make sure that the positive and negative witnesses are distinct, which is achieved by otherwise making the query true.
To this end, we introduce a fresh role name
u
and fresh concept
names
G,A_{0}^{\prime}
. We update
{\sf CP}
so that
G
is minimized and extend
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
with:

\displaystyle{\sf Pos}\sqcap{\sf Neg}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\exists u.(G\sqcap A^{\prime}_{0})

(90)

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}_{0}

(91)

We add

G(a_{0})

to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and the new query is
A^{\prime}_{0}
.

To compensate for removing the CIs
C\sqsubseteq\bot
, we consider another fresh concept name
M
and update
{\sf CP}
so that
M
is minimized. We further extend
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
with

\displaystyle C\sqsubseteq M\sqcap\exists u.(G\sqcap A^{\prime}_{0})\quad\text{ for all }C\sqsubseteq\bot\in\mathcal{T}.

(92)

So if a concept
C
with
C\sqsubseteq\bot\in\mathcal{T}
is satisfied, then we force the minimized concept name
M
to be non-empty and make the (new) query true.

Let
{\sf CP}^{\prime}
be the extended circumscription pattern.
It remains to establish the correctness of the reduction.

Claim.

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models A_{0}(a_{0})
iff
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\models A^{\prime}_{0}(a_{0})
.

To prepare for the proof of the claim, we first observe that every
model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
gives rise to a model
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}

in a natural way. More precisely, we set
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\uplus\{d^{+}_{1},d^{-}_{1},d^{+}_{2},d^{-}_{2}\}
,
define the extension of all concept and role names that occur in
\mathcal{K}
exactly as in
\mathcal{I}
, and interpret the fresh concept and role names as
follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}X^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\\[2.84526pt]
D^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\{d^{+}_{1},d^{-}_{1},d^{+}_{2},d^{-}_{2}\}\\[2.84526pt]
D_{i}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\{d^{+}_{i},d^{-}_{i}\}\text{ for }i\in\{1,2\}\\[2.84526pt]
{\sf Pos}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\{d^{+}_{1},d^{+}_{2}\}\\[2.84526pt]
{\sf Neg}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\{d^{-}_{1},d^{-}_{2}\}\\[2.84526pt]
r_{D}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\{(d,d^{+}_{1}),(d,d^{-}_{1})\mid d\in(B\sqcap B_{1})^{\mathcal{I}}\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{(d,d^{+}_{2}),(d,d^{-}_{2})\mid d\in(B\sqcap B_{2})^{\mathcal{I}}\}\\[1.42262pt]
{\sf check}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\{(a_{0},a)\mid a\in{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\\[2.84526pt]
{A^{\prime}_{0}}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&A_{0}^{\mathcal{I}}\\[2.84526pt]
u^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\emptyset\\[2.84526pt]
M^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\emptyset\\[2.84526pt]
G^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}&=&\{a_{0}\}\\[2.84526pt]
\end{array}

It is easily checked that
{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
is indeed a model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
.
Now for the actual proof of the claim

“
\Leftarrow
”. Assume that
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\not\models A_{0}(a_{0})
. Then there is a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}
that is minimal w.r.t.
{\sf CP}
and satisfies
\mathcal{I}\not\models A_{0}(a_{0})
. Let
{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
be the corresponding model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
defined above. We
clearly have

{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\not\models A^{\prime}_{0}(a_{0})
. To show that
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\not\models A^{\prime}_{0}(a_{0})
, it thus remains to show that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is minimal w.r.t.
{\sf CP}^{\prime}
.

Assume to the contrary that there is a model
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
.
As
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
interprets
D,M,
and
G
minimally among all models of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
,
we must have
D^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=D^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{d^{+}_{1},d^{-}_{1},d^{+}_{2},d^{-}_{2}\}
,
M^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
, and

G^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=G^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{a_{0}\}
. We show that the restriction
\mathcal{J}

of
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
to domain
X^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
is a model
of
\mathcal{K}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
D^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=D^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
,
M^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=M^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, and

G^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=G^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
imply
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
, which then yields a contradiction against the minimality of
\mathcal{I}
.

Based on the facts that
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
,
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq X^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
due to (
84
), and
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, it is clear that
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{A}
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, by
construction of
\mathcal{J}
it is easy to see that
\mathcal{J}
satisfies all CIs in
\mathcal{T}
with the possible
exceptions of the disjunctive CI
B\sqsubseteq B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}
and the
CIs of the form
C\sqsubseteq\bot
. The former, however, is satisfied due to
CIs (
85
) and (
86
) and since

D^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\{d^{+}_{1},d^{-}_{1},d^{+}_{2},d^{-}_{2}\}
. And the latter is satisfied
due to the CIs (
92
) and since
M^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
.

“
\Rightarrow
”. Assume that
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\not\models A^{\prime}_{0}(a_{0})
. Then there is a model
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
that is minimal w.r.t.
{\sf CP}^{\prime}
and satisfies
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\not\models A^{\prime}_{0}(a_{0})
.
We first argue that
G^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{a_{0}\}
as, otherwise, we can find a model
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
, contradicting the minimality of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.
We have
G^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\subseteq\{a_{0}\}
since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Assume to the contrary of what we want to show that the converse fails. Then we construct
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
by modifying
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
as follows:

•

set
G^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\{a_{0}\}
;

•

add
a_{0}
to
{A^{\prime}_{0}}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
;

•

reroute
u
as
u^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\{(e,a_{0})\mid e\in(\exists u)^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\}
.

It is easy to see that, indeed,
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is still a model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.

Since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
satisfies CIs (
85
) and (
86
), for every
e\in B^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
we can choose two elements
e^{+}\in(D\sqcap{\sf Pos})^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
and
e^{-}\in(D\sqcap{\sf Neg})^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
such that
(e,e^{+}),(e,e^{-})\in r_{D}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. If
e^{+}=e^{-}
for some
e
, then by (
90
) and
G^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{a_{0}\}
, we have
a_{0}\in A_{0}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
,
which is impossible since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\not\models A^{\prime}_{0}(a_{0})
. Thus
e^{+}\neq e^{-}
for
all
e
.

We next argue that the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

for all
e\in B^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
:

\{e^{+},e^{-}\}\cap\{d^{+}_{1},d^{-}_{1},d^{+}_{2},d^{-}_{2}\}\neq\emptyset
;

2.

M^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
.

If, in fact, any of the above conditions are violated, then we can again find a model
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
, contradicting the minimality of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.
We start with Condition 1.

Assume to the contrary that for some
e\in B^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
,
e^{+},e^{-}\notin\{d^{+}_{1},d^{-}_{1},d^{+}_{2},d^{-}_{2}\}
. Then we construct

\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
by modifying
\mathcal{J}
as follows:

•

remove
e^{-}
from
D^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
;

•

add
e^{+}
to
{\sf Neg}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
;

•

add
a_{0}
to
{A^{\prime}_{0}}^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
;

•

add
(e^{+},a_{0})
to
u^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
.

It is easy to see that, indeed,
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is still a model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.

In the case of Condition 2, we can construct the model
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
simply as
\mathcal{J}
modified by setting
M^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
. The resulting
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. The CIs (
92
) are the only potentially problematic items. But since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\not\models A^{\prime}_{0}(a_{0})
and
G^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{a_{0}\}
, we can infer from (
92
) that
C^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for all
C\sqsubseteq\bot\in\mathcal{T}
, and thus the same holds for
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
. Consequently, the CIs (
92
) are never triggered in
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
and it is safe to set
M^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be the restriction of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
to
X^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. It can be verified that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{A}
and that it satisfies all CIs in
\mathcal{T}
with the possible
exceptions of the disjunctive CI
B\sqsubseteq B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}
and the
CIs of the form
C\sqsubseteq\bot
. The former, however, is satisfied by
Condition 1 and due to
CIs (
85
) and (
86
). And the latter is satisfied
by Condition 2 and
due to CI (
92
).

Since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\not\models A^{\prime}_{0}(a_{0})
and by (
91
), we have
\mathcal{I}\not\models A_{0}(a_{0})
. To show that
\mathcal{K}\not\models A_{0}(a_{0})
, it thus remains
to show that
\mathcal{I}
is minimal w.r.t.
<_{\sf CP}
.
Assume to the contrary that there is a model
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{K}

and let
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
be the corresponding model of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
constructed at
the beginning of the correctness proof. Since
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
interprets
the minimized concept names
D
,
M
, and
G
in a minimal way among
the models of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
, it follows from
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
that

\mathcal{J}^{\prime}<_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, in contradiction to
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
being minimal.
∎

Appendix G
Proofs for Section
5.2

We now move to a proof of Theorem
13
, which strongly combines
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
expressiveness with fixed concept names.
Interestingly, fixed concept names are actually not needed.
Indeed, given a circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
KB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}
(
\mathcal{K}
), each
A\in\mathsf{F}
can be replaced by the two minimized predicates
A
and
\overline{A}
, where
\overline{A}
is a fresh concept name set to be the complement of
A
via the
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
axioms
\overline{A}\sqsubseteq\lnot A
and
\lnot A\sqsubseteq\overline{A}
that can be added in the TBox.
Concept names
A
and
\overline{A}
are then given higher preference than any other concept name from the original set of minimized concept names
\mathsf{M}
.
It is then straightforward to verify that the resulting circumscribed
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
KB
{\sf Circ}_{{\sf CP}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
has the same models as
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
(up to the interpretation of
\overline{A}
, which always equals the interpretation of
\lnot A
).

See
13

Proof.

The proof proceeds by reduction from the complement of the
NExp
-complete
Succinct
-
3COL
problem. An instance of
Succinct
-
3COL
consists of a Boolean circuit
\mathcal{C}
with
2n
input gates.
The graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
encoded by
\mathcal{C}
has
2^{n}
vertices, identified by binary encodings on
n
bits.
Two vertices
u
and
v
, with respective binary encodings
u_{1}\dots u_{n}
and
v_{1}\dots v_{n}
, are adjacent in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
iff
\mathcal{C}
returns True when given as input
u_{1}\dots u_{n}
on its first
n
gates and
v_{1}\dots v_{n}
on the second half.
The problem of deciding if
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
is 3-colorable has been proven to be
NExp
-complete in
[
\citeauthoryearPapadimitriou and
Yannakakis1986
]
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Boolean circuit with
2n
input gates.
We build a
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf bool}
cKB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
with
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{A})
s.t. the AQ
\mathsf{Goal}(x)
admits the individual
a
as a certain answer iff
\mathcal{C}
does not belong to
Succinct
-
3COL
.
Let us already clarify that
{\sf CP}
uses three minimized concept names, namely
\mathsf{Center}
,
\mathsf{AllPairs}
and
\mathsf{GoodCol}
, with the following preference relation
\prec
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Center}\prec\mathsf{AllPairs}\prec\mathsf{GoodCol}

(93)

It also uses fixed concept names: each
\mathsf{Bit}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 2n
and the six concept names
\mathsf{R}_{1}
,
\mathsf{G}_{1}
,
\mathsf{B}_{1}
,
\mathsf{R}_{2}
,
\mathsf{G}_{2}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
, which will be useful to represent binary encodings and color assignments respectively.
All other predicates vary.

Models of
\mathcal{K}
contain a central element
a
, that we enforce via the ABox
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathsf{Center}(a)\}
and which will be unique
by virtue of the preference relation.
This central element will further be used to detect undesired behaviors of the models via the AQ.
In a model, each element represents a pair
(v_{1},v_{2})
of vertices by its combination of fixed concepts
\mathsf{Bit}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq 2n
that corresponds to the binary encodings of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.
To detect models that do not represent all possible pairs, we require each element representing a pair
(v_{1},v_{2})
to “send” at the center a binary sequence of
2n
bits that must be different from the encoding of
(v_{1},v_{2})
.
This is achieved by the following axioms in
\mathcal{T}
:

\displaystyle\top\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\exists r_{i}\sqcup\exists\overline{r_{i}}\quad\text{ for each }1\leq i\leq 2n

(94)

\displaystyle\exists r_{i}^{-}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Center}\qquad\text{ for each }1\leq i\leq 2n

(95)

\displaystyle\exists\overline{r_{i}}^{-}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Center}\qquad\text{ for each }1\leq i\leq 2n

(96)

\displaystyle\top\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathop{\mbox{\bigmathxx\char 116\relax}}}\limits_{0\leq i\leq 2n}(\exists r_{i}\sqcap\lnot\mathsf{Bit}_{i})\sqcup(\exists\overline{r_{i}}\sqcap\mathsf{Bit}_{i})

(97)

We now require the number of sent sequences to be unique, if possible, via the minimized concept name
\mathsf{AllPairs}
, triggered by the following axioms in
\mathcal{T}
:

\displaystyle\exists r_{i}^{-}\sqcap\exists\overline{r_{i}}^{-}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{AllPairs}\quad\text{ for each }1\leq i\leq 2n

(98)

Therefore, if (at least) a pair is not represented in a model, then
all
elements can send the binary encoding of a same missing pair to avoid instantiating
\mathsf{AllPairs}
.
If, on the other hand, each pair is represented (at least once), then the number of sent sequences cannot be unique, and
\mathsf{AllPairs}
must be satisfied on the central element
a
.
To detect this with the AQ, we add the following axiom to
\mathcal{T}
:

\displaystyle\lnot\mathsf{AllPairs}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Goal}

(99)

Now that we can detect whether all pairs are represented, we move to the encoding of colors.
The respective color of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
is chosen locally via the following axioms in
\mathcal{T}
:

\displaystyle\top\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{1}\sqcup\mathsf{G}_{1}\sqcup\mathsf{B}_{1}

(100)

\displaystyle\top\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{2}\sqcup\mathsf{G}_{2}\sqcup\mathsf{B}_{2}

(101)

Recall concept names
\mathsf{R}_{1},\mathsf{G}_{1},\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{R}_{2},\mathsf{G}_{2},\mathsf{B}_{2}
are fixed.
We additionally enforce that if an element represents a pair
(v_{1},v_{2})
corresponding to an edge
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
, then it must assign different colors to
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.
This is achieved by computing the output of
\mathcal{C}
on input
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
via a fresh concept
G
for each gate
G
of
\mathcal{C}
and adding the following axioms in
\mathcal{T}
:

\displaystyle G\equiv\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Bit}_{i}\quad\qquad\text{ if }G\text{ is the }i^{\text{th}}\text{ input gate of }\mathcal{C}

(102)

\displaystyle G\equiv\;

\displaystyle G_{1}\sqcap G_{2}\quad\text{ if }G\text{ is a $\land$-gate with inputs }G_{1},G_{2}

(103)

\displaystyle G\equiv\;

\displaystyle G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}\quad\text{ if }G\text{ is a $\lor$-gate with inputs }G_{1},G_{2}

(104)

\displaystyle G\equiv\;

\displaystyle\lnot G^{\prime}\quad\qquad\text{if }G\text{ is a $\lnot$-gate with input }G^{\prime}

(105)

Assuming the output gate of
\mathcal{C}
is
G_{o}
, it now suffices to add the following axioms in
\mathcal{T}
:

\displaystyle G_{o}\sqcap\mathsf{R}_{1}\sqcap\mathsf{R}_{2}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\bot

(106)

\displaystyle G_{o}\sqcap\mathsf{G}_{1}\sqcap\mathsf{G}_{2}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\bot

(107)

\displaystyle G_{o}\sqcap\mathsf{B}_{1}\sqcap\mathsf{B}_{2}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\bot

(108)

While the above allows ensuring that each element
e
representing an edge
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
assigns different colors to
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, there might still be inconsistent colorings in the sense that
v_{1}
might have been assigned another color by some other element
e^{\prime}
representing an edge
\{v_{1},v_{3}\}
.
To detect such inconsistent colorings, the central element “scans” all colors assigned to a tested vertex, whose encoding corresponds to a binary sequence of
n
bits represented by dedicated concepts
\mathsf{T}_{i}
.
To do so, the interpretation of each
\mathsf{T}_{i}
on the center element is first copied by all elements via the following axioms in
\mathcal{T}
, defined for each
1\leq i\leq n
:

\displaystyle\top\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\exists t_{i}\sqcup\exists\overline{t_{i}}

(109)

\displaystyle\exists t_{i}^{-}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Center}\sqcap\mathsf{T}_{i}

\displaystyle\exists\overline{t_{i}}^{-}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Center}\sqcap\lnot\mathsf{T}_{i}

(110)

\displaystyle\exists t_{i}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{i}

\displaystyle\exists\overline{t_{i}}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\lnot\mathsf{T}_{i}

(111)

Each element representing a pair
(v_{1},v_{2})
then compares whether the encoding of either
v_{1}
or
v_{2}
matches the sequence
\mathsf{T}_{i}
, via the following axioms:

\displaystyle\bigsqcap_{1\leq i\leq n}\left((\mathsf{Bit}_{i}\sqcap\mathsf{T}_{i})\sqcup(\lnot\mathsf{Bit}_{i}\sqcap\lnot\mathsf{T}_{i})\right)\equiv\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Test}_{1}

(112)

\displaystyle\bigsqcap_{1\leq i\leq n}\left((\mathsf{Bit}_{n+i}\sqcap\mathsf{T}_{i})\sqcup(\lnot\mathsf{Bit}_{n+i}\sqcap\lnot\mathsf{T}_{i})\right)\equiv\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Test}_{2}

(113)

It then sends the corresponding assigned color back to the central element via the following axioms in
\mathcal{T}
, defined for each
C\in\{\mathsf{R},\mathsf{G},\mathsf{B}\}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Test}_{1}\sqcap C_{1}\equiv\;

\displaystyle\exists s_{C}

(114)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Test}_{2}\sqcap C_{2}\equiv\;

\displaystyle\exists s_{C}

(115)

\displaystyle\exists s_{C}^{-}\sqsubseteq\,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Center}

(116)

The center now detects whether it receives two different colors for the tested vertex:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathop{\mbox{\bigmathxx\char 116\relax}}}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}C,C^{\prime}\in\{\mathsf{R},\mathsf{G},\mathsf{B}\}\\
C\neq C^{\prime}\end{subarray}}(\exists s_{C}^{-}\sqcap\exists s_{C^{\prime}}^{-})\equiv\;

\displaystyle\lnot\mathsf{GoodCol}

(117)

where
\mathsf{GoodCol}
is a minimized concept name, which forces the center element to detect an inconsistent coloring if it exists.
This is further translated in terms of the AQ with:

\displaystyle\lnot\mathsf{GoodCol}\sqsubseteq\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Goal}

(118)

We are now done with the construction of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
It remains to prove that:

{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})\models\mathsf{Goal}(a)\iff\mathcal{C}\notin\text{Succinct}\text{-}\text{3COL}.

“
\Rightarrow
”.
Assume
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Succinct}\text{-}\text{3COL}
and let
\rho:\{0,\dots,2^{n}-1\}\rightarrow\{\mathsf{R},\mathsf{G},\mathsf{B}\}
be a (legal) 3-coloring of the encoded graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
We build a model
\mathcal{I}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
based on
\rho
, whose domain
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
contains
a
and one fresh element
e_{v_{1},v_{2}}
per
(v_{1},v_{2})\in\{0,\dots,2^{n}-1\}\times\{0,\dots,2^{n}-1\}
.
Model
\mathcal{I}
further interprets concept and role names as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Center}}^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{GoodCol}^{\mathcal{I}}=\mathsf{AllPairs}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{a\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Bit}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=\{e_{v_{1},v_{2}}\mid i^{\text{th}}\text{ bit in }(v_{1},v_{2})\text{ encoding is }1\}

\displaystyle C_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=\{e_{v_{1},v_{2}}\mid\rho(v_{i})=C\}

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\{e_{v_{1},v_{2}}\mid G\in\mathcal{C}\text{ evaluates to }1\text{ on input }(v_{1},v_{2})\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Test}_{i}

\displaystyle=\{e_{v_{1},v_{2}}\mid v_{i}=0\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Goal}^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}=\emptyset

\displaystyle r_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=\{(e_{v_{1},v_{2}},a)\mid i^{\text{th}}\text{ bit in }(v_{1},v_{2})\text{ encoding is }0\}

\displaystyle\overline{r_{i}}^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=\{(e_{v_{1},v_{2}},a)\mid i^{\text{th}}\text{ bit in }(v_{1},v_{2})\text{ encoding is }1\}

\displaystyle t_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\overline{t_{i}}^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=\{(e_{v_{1},v_{2}},a)\mid 0\leq v_{1},v_{2}\leq 2^{n}-1\}

\displaystyle\begin{split}s_{C}&=\{(e_{v_{1},v_{2}},a)\mid\rho(0)=C\\
&\phantom{=\{(e_{v_{1},v_{2}},a)\mid}\text{ and either }v_{1}=0\text{ or }v_{2}=0\}\end{split}

Notice that we have arbitrarily chosen each element representing a pair to send back to the center it’s own encoding in which all bits have been flipped (see interpretations of roles
r_{i}
and
\overline{r_{i}}
), and the tested vertex to be
0
(interpretations of each
\mathsf{T}_{i}
being empty and
t_{i}
,
\overline{t_{i}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{i}
being set accordingly).
With the two above remarks, it is easily verified
\mathcal{I}
models
\mathcal{K}
, and, by definition
a\notin\mathsf{Goal}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
It remains to verify
\mathcal{I}
complies with
{\sf CP}
.
By contradiction, assume there exists
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
.
Notice that from the fixed predicates,
\mathcal{J}
still encodes one instance of each possible pair and the very same coloring
\rho
.
From
\mathcal{J}
being a model of
\mathcal{A}
, we have
a\in\mathsf{Center}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
From
\mathsf{Center}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{a\}
and
\mathsf{Center}
being the most preferred minimized predicate, it follows that
\mathsf{Center}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{a\}
as otherwise we would have
\mathcal{I}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{J}
.
Therefore, since each possible pair is represented at least once, the
r_{i}
and
\overline{r_{i}}
mechanism ensures
a\in\mathsf{AllPairs}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Together with
\mathsf{AllPairs}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{a\}
and
\mathsf{AllPairs}
being second most preferred minimized predicates, we obtain
\mathsf{AllPairs}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{a\}
(as otherwise, again, we would have
\mathcal{I}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{J}
).
Finally, since the coloring encoded in
\mathcal{J}
must be exactly
\rho
, whatever the interpretations of concepts
\mathsf{T}_{i}
, the concept
\exists s_{C}^{-}\sqcap\exists s_{C^{\prime}}^{-}
will always be empty for all
C,C^{\prime}\in\{\mathsf{R},\mathsf{G},\mathsf{B}\}
s.t.
C\neq C^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
a\in\mathsf{GoodCol}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Now, from
\mathsf{GoodCol}
being also minimized and
\mathsf{GoodCol}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{a\}
, we obtain
\mathsf{GoodCol}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{a\}
.
Overall
\mathcal{J}
interprets the minimized predicates as
\mathcal{I}
does, hence the desired contradiction of
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
.

“
\Leftarrow
”.
Assume
\mathcal{C}\notin\text{Succinct}\text{-}\text{3COL}
.
Consider a model
\mathcal{I}
of
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
First notice that
\mathsf{Center}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{a\}
.
Indeed,
\mathcal{I}
models
\mathcal{A}
hence
a\in\mathsf{Center}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and, if there were
b\in\mathsf{Center}^{\mathcal{I}}
with
b\neq a
, then we could find a model
\mathcal{J}
of
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
by:

1.

take
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and preserve all interpretations of fixed concept names;

2.

setting
\mathsf{Center}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{a\}
;

3.

replace every
(e,e^{\prime})\in r_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
by
(e,a)\in r_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}
and same for
\overline{r_{i}}
;

4.

define accordingly
\mathsf{AllPairs}^{\mathcal{J}}
;

5.

set for all
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
:
e\in\mathsf{T}_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}
iff
a\in\mathsf{T}_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
;

6.

set
t_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(e,a)\mid e\in\mathsf{T}_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}\}
and
\overline{t_{i}}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(e,a)\mid e\notin\mathsf{T}_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}\}
;

7.

define accordingly interpretations of concepts evaluating the circuit and
\mathsf{Test}_{1}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
\mathsf{Test}_{2}^{\mathcal{J}}
;

8.

set
s_{C}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(e,a)\mid e\in((\mathsf{Test}_{1}\sqcap C_{1})\sqcup(\mathsf{Test}_{2}\sqcap C_{2}))^{\mathcal{J}}\}
;

9.

define accordingly
\mathsf{GoodCol}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
\mathsf{Goal}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

In particular, notice Step 1 removes
b
from the interpretation of
\mathsf{Center}
.

We now want to prove that if a pair
(v_{1},v_{2})
is not represented in
\mathcal{I}
, that is:

\bigcap_{i\in\text{Ones}(v_{1},v_{2})}\mathsf{Bit}_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}\cap\bigcap_{i\in\text{Zeros}(v_{1},v_{2})}(\lnot\mathsf{Bit}_{i})^{\mathcal{J}}=\emptyset,

where
\text{Ones}(v_{1},v_{2})
is the set of
0\leq i\leq 2n-1
s.t. the
i^{\text{th}}
bit in the binary encoding of
(v_{1},v_{2})
is
1
and
\text{Zeros}(v_{1},v_{2})
is its complement, then the query must be satisfied.
Indeed, if such a
(v_{1},v_{2})
exists, then we must have
\mathsf{AllPairs}^{\mathcal{J}}=\emptyset
, thus
a\in\mathsf{Goal}^{\mathcal{J}}
, as otherwise we can obtain a model
\mathcal{J}
of
\mathcal{K}
s.t.
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
by:

1.

take
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and preserve all interpretations of fixed concept names and
\mathsf{Center}
;

2.

set
r_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\{a\}
if
i\in\text{Ones}(v_{1},v_{2})
and
r_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}=\emptyset
otherwise; set
\overline{r_{i}}^{\mathcal{J}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\{a\}
if
i\in\text{Zeros}(v_{1},v_{2})
and
\overline{r_{i}}^{\mathcal{J}}=\emptyset
otherwise;

3.

follow steps 3 to 8 from the previous construction of
\mathcal{J}
.

In particular, Step 2 of the above complies with Equation
97
as
(v_{1},v_{2})
is not represented in
\mathcal{I}
.
It further implies that
\mathsf{AllPairs}^{\mathcal{J}}=\emptyset
, which ensures the desired contradiction
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
.

We are thus left with the case in which each possible pair
(v_{1},v_{2})
is represented at least once in
\mathcal{I}
.
From Equations
100
and
101
, we know each element representing a pair assigns colors to its
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.
From Equations
102
to
108
, these colors cannot be the same if there is an edge
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
in the graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
Thus, since we assume
\mathcal{C}\notin\text{Succinct}\text{-}\text{3COL}
, the overall choices of colors cannot be consistent: there must exist a vertex
v_{0}
which is assigned a color
C_{1}
by an element
e_{1}
representing a pair
p_{1}
and a different color
C_{2}
by an element
e_{2}
representing a pair
p_{2}
.
Let us assume
p_{1}
has shape
(v_{0},v_{1})
and
p_{2}
has shape
(v_{0},v_{2})
(other cases works similarly).
We can now prove that
\mathsf{GoodCol}^{\mathcal{I}}=\emptyset
, thus
a\in\mathsf{Goal}
, as otherwise we could find a model
\mathcal{J}
of
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
by:

1.

take
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and preserve all interpretations of fixed concept names, of
\mathsf{Center}
and
\mathsf{AllPairs}
, and of roles
r_{i}
and
\overline{r_{i}}
;

2.

set for all
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
:
e\in\mathsf{T}_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}
iff
i\in\text{Ones}(v_{0})
;

3.

set
t_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(e,a)\mid e\in\mathsf{T}_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}\}
and
\overline{t_{i}}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(e,a)\mid e\notin\mathsf{T}_{i}^{\mathcal{J}}\}
;

4.

define accordingly interpretations of concepts evaluating the circuit and
\mathsf{Test}_{1}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
\mathsf{Test}_{2}^{\mathcal{J}}
;

5.

set
s_{C}^{\mathcal{J}}=\{(e,a)\mid e\in((\mathsf{Test}_{1}\sqcap C_{1})\sqcup(\mathsf{Test}_{2}\sqcap C_{2}))^{\mathcal{J}}\}
;

6.

define accordingly
\mathsf{GoodCol}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
\mathsf{Goal}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

In particular, Equation
112
and Step 4 in the above ensure
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{Test}_{1}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Therefore Step 5 above yields
(e_{1},a)\in s_{C_{1}}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
(e_{2},a)\in s_{C_{2}}^{\mathcal{J}}
, which triggers Equation
117
in Step 6 and ensures
\mathsf{GoodCol}^{\mathcal{J}}=\emptyset
.
This yields the desired contradiction
\mathcal{J}<_{\sf CP}\mathcal{I}
.
Thus,
\mathsf{GoodCol}^{\mathcal{I}}=\emptyset
, in particular Equation
118
yields
a\in\mathsf{Goal}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

∎

We work towards a proof of Theorem
14
.
Assume given a model
\mathcal{I}
of a
\textrm{DL-Lite}_{\sf horn}^{\cal H}
cKB
{\sf Circ_{\sf CP}}(\mathcal{K})
.
We “forget” about some parts of
\mathcal{I}
, only retaining which roles may be needed due to a combination of fixed predicates in the forgotten part.
To this end, we define the fixed-type
\mathsf{ftp}_{\mathcal{I}}({e})
of an element
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
as the set of
DL-Lite
concepts
C
such that
\mathcal{T}\models(\bigsqcap_{e\in F^{\mathcal{I}},\ F\in\mathsf{F}}F)\sqsubseteq C
.
Given a role
r\in\mathsf{N_{R}}^{\pm}
, we say that
r
is
forced
in
\mathcal{I}
if there exists an element
f_{r}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
\exists r\in\mathsf{ftp}_{\mathcal{I}}({f_{r}})
.
For each forced role in
\mathcal{I}
, we assume chosen such an element
f_{r}
.
Similarly, for each role
r\in\mathsf{N_{R}}^{\pm}
, we assume chosen an element
w_{r}\in(\exists r^{-})^{\mathcal{I}}
if
(\exists r^{-})^{\mathcal{I}}\neq\emptyset
.
We now construct an interpretation
{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
whose domain
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
consists in
{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})
, all chosen
f_{r}
and all chosen
w_{r}
.
The interpretation
\mathcal{I}_{0}
is now defined as:

\displaystyle{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}=\;

\displaystyle{{A}}^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}

\displaystyle{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}=\;

\displaystyle{{r}}^{\mathcal{I}}\cap({\sf ind}(\mathcal{A})\times{\sf ind}(\mathcal{A}))
